Baryon Asymmetry from the Decay and Scattering of a Majorana Fermion Pair Coupled to Quarks
Shrihari Gopalakrishna, Rakesh Tibrewala

Abstract
We compute the baryon asymmetry in decay and scattering processes involving the electromagnetically charge-neutral fermion χ \chi that carries nonzero baryon number and interacts with quark-like fermions U , D {U},{D} via a vector-vector dimension-six effective operator, in the theory we developed in our earlier work. Majorana masses for the χ \chi break baryon number and split the Dirac fermion χ \chi into a pair of Majorana fermions 𝒳 n {\cal X}_{n} with indefinite baryon number. We identify loop amplitudes for 𝒳 n {\cal X}_{n} decay and scattering processes that are sensitive to the baryon number violation. The phases in the Majorana mass and couplings, in conjunction with the phase from intermediate onshell states, lead to C C and C ​ P CP violation in these processes. For some representative parameter choices, we numerically compute the decay and scattering baryon asymmetries between the process and its conjugate process, and find that the asymmetry generated is very interesting for explaining the baryon asymmetry of the Universe.



1
Introduction

In any candidate theory, the generation of the baryon asymmetry of the Universe (BAU)
requires that the three Sakharov conditions
[
1
]
be satisfied,
namely,
baryon number violation,
C
and
CP
violation, and, departure from thermal equilibrium.
It is generally believed that in the standard model (SM), not enough baryon asymmetry is generated to explain
the observed BAU.
Various theories beyond the standard model (BSM) have been proposed to address this shortcoming of the SM.
For reviews, see for example, Refs.
[
2
,
3
]
.
One among them is the BSM effective theory we developed in Ref.
[
4
]
that has all the ingredients necessary to explain the BAU.

Our theory contains an electromagnetically (EM) charge-neutral fermion
\chi
,
interacting via a vector-vector (VV) dimension-six effective operator
coupling the
\chi
to an up-type
{U}
and two identical down-type
{D}
quark-like fermions.
The
{U},{D}
each carries baryon number
B=1/3
, like the SM quarks.
We can write the VV interaction schematically as
(1/\Lambda^{2})\,(\bar{\chi}\Gamma^{\mu}{U})\,(\bar{{D}^{c}}\Gamma_{\mu}{D})
,
where
\Lambda
is the cutoff scale.
In this work we identify loop contributions to
\chi
decay and scattering processes and compute the baryon asymmetry
that result from them.
In these works we study the VV interaction as opposed to the scalar-scalar (SS) interaction studied in the literature;
see Ref.
[
4
]
for related references.

Our motivation in carrying out an effective theory analysis is that it provides an estimate of the baryon asymmetry
generated in a whole class of related ultraviolet (UV) complete theories that have our effective operators
as the low energy limit.
A truly accurate result can only be obtained by calculating in a specific UV completion.
We take as an example the UV completion given in Ref.
[
4
]

that has a propagating state
\xi
that resolves our effective operators.

In Ref.
[
4
]
we showed that our theory has a rich enough structure to potentially satisfy the Sakharov conditions.
Baryon number violation is due to the
\chi
Majorana mass terms.

C
and
CP
violation are due to phases in the Majorana mass and couplings.
Considering the
\chi,{U},{D}
in the early Universe, if the interaction rate of the
\chi
is smaller than the Hubble expansion rate,
the
\chi
can be out of thermal equilibrium.

As we demonstrated in Ref.
[
4
]
,
the Majorana mass splits the Dirac Fermion
\chi
with Dirac mass
M_{\chi}

into two nondegenerate Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
(
n=\{1,2\}
) with masses
M_{n}
.
The
{\cal X}_{n}
have indefinite baryon number.
In our framework, the decay baryon asymmetry is given by the difference in
decay rate for the process
\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{U}{D}{D})
whose final state has baryon number
B=+1
,
and that for the conjugate process
\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\bar{U}\bar{D}\bar{D})
whose final state has
B=-1
.
The Majorana (i.e. self-conjugate) nature of the
{\cal X}_{n}
is the reason that the
{\cal X}_{n}
could decay into these decay modes with opposite baryon number.
Similarly, the scattering baryon asymmetry arises due to the difference in cross sections

\sigma({\cal X}_{n}\bar{Q}\rightarrow QQ)
and
\sigma({\cal X}_{n}Q\rightarrow\bar{Q}\bar{Q})
, where
Q=\{{U},{D}\}
,
and the former process has
\Delta B=+1
while the latter has
\Delta B=-1
.

We showed in general terms in Ref.
[
4
]

that in this effective theory,
the leading contribution to the baryon asymmetry is from the interference of tree and loop-level amplitudes.
There, we emphasized that nonzero phases in the Majorana masses and/or couplings could act like
weak phases
,
i.e. flip sign between the process and its conjugate processes,
and,
also that intermediate
{U},{D}
states going on-shell in the loops leads to a
strong phase
, i.e. is the same for the process and its conjugate process.
We showed that both weak and strong phases are essential for the generation of the baryon asymmetry,
and estimated the size of the baryon asymmetry that could be generated in this effective theory.

In this work, we concretely realize these ideas by identifying specific
one and two loop decay and scattering processes involving the
{\cal X}_{n}
with weak and strong phases,
and compute the baryon asymmetry generated from them.
We refer to the two-loop diagrams as
single operator contributions

since they require the presence of only the above VV effective operator.
In addition to the VV operator above, if an operator of the type

(1/\Lambda^{2})\,(\bar{\cal X}\Gamma^{\mu}{U})\,(\bar{U}\Gamma_{\mu}{\cal X})
is also present in the effective theory,
we show that using both the operators,
we can write one-loop diagrams that we call the
multiple operator contributions

that also yield a baryon asymmetry.

To compute the total decay and scattering rates and the asymmetry,
we have to perform integrals over phase space and loop kinematical variables.
These have multiple scales with many variables, and an analytical computation is not feasible.
We therefore resort to a numerical evaluation of these complicated integrals.
We present our results in a general way without making a particular choice of the scale
\Lambda
or
M_{\chi}
,
but give overall scaling factors involving
\Lambda
and
r_{M\Lambda}\!\!=\!\!M_{\chi}/\Lambda
.
This allows application of our results to different theories with their own preferences for the scale of such new physics.

The results of this work are inputs (as collision terms) into the Boltzmann equations (BE)
that governs the number densities of the baryons and antibaryons
at high temperature in the early Universe.
For this purpose, we therefore compute
the thermal averages of the decay and scattering baryon asymmetries resulting from the processes above.
Solving the Boltzmann equations with these inputs would let us determine the resulting BAU at late time (today).
The numerical solution of the Boltzmann equations are quite involved and is presented in Ref.
[
5
]
.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Sec.
2
we review the effective theory and the baryon asymmetry generation mechanism from Ref.
[
4
]
,
which we use in this work.
In Sec.
3
we analyze the
{\cal X}_{n}
decay,
starting with the tree-level decay rate in Sec.
3.1
,
computing the single operator decay rate difference in Sec.
3.2
,
and the multiple operator decay rate difference in Sec.
3.3
.
Our computation needs clarity on the subtraction scheme for the
{\cal X}_{n}
2-point function, which we discuss in Appendix
A
.
In Sec.
4
we compute the rate asymmetry from
{\cal X}_{n}
scattering processes,
with the single operator scattering contribution in Sec.
4.1
,
and the multiple operator scattering contribution in Sec.
4.2
.
In Sec.
5
we compute the rate difference between the
\Delta B=-2
three-to-three scattering process
and its conjugate process.
For use in a thermal setting in the early Universe,
we also compute the thermally averaged decay rate and cross sections of these processes as a function of temperature
using the method we lay down in Appendix
B
.
For computing the late time baryon asymmetry,
we develop further in Sec.
6
the Boltzmann equations applicable in the early Universe,
taking into account the relations imposed by
CPT
and unitarity on the forward and inverse reaction matrix elements
that we discuss in Appendix
C
.
In scattering contributions, the initial state momenta in the thermal spectrum can be
larger than the cutoff scale (
\Lambda
) of the effective theory,
bringing in a dependence on the UV physics, and we discuss our method of handling this in Appendix
D
.
We offer our conclusions in Sec.
7
.
We relegate to Part

Baryon Asymmetry from the Decay and Scattering of a Majorana Fermion Pair Coupled to Quarks
the more detailed technical aspects as supplementary material.

2
The Effective Theory and the Baryon Asymmetry

In this section we briefly summarize aspects of the effective theory developed in Ref.
[
4
]
that are essential for our work here.
The theory contains a Dirac (vector-like) fermion
\chi

with a Dirac mass
M_{\chi}
and also Majorana masses
\tilde{M}_{L,R}
that split the
\chi

into two Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
(with
n=\{1,2\}
)
with mass eigenvalues
M_{n}
.
The Dirac fermion
\chi
can be assigned baryon number
B\!\!=\!\!+1
,
and the Majorana masses break baryon number leading to the Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
having indefinite baryon number.
We focus on the VV interaction
1
1

1

In Ref.
[
4
]
,
we showed that the scalar-scalar (SS) interaction involving two same
{D}
flavors cannot be written down.
We solidify this claim further at the matrix element level
(cf. Appendix
E.2.1
).

dimension-six effective operator that couples each Majorana state
{\cal X}_{n}

to the quark-like fermions
{U}
and two same flavor
{D}
as

{\cal L}^{\rm VV}_{\rm int}=\frac{\epsilon^{abc}}{2\Lambda^{2}}\left\{\left[\overline{{D}^{c}_{b}}\,\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}{D}_{a}\right]\ \left[\bar{{\cal X}}_{n}\,G^{n}_{V\mu}{U}_{c}\right]+\left[\overline{{D}}_{a}\,\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{D}^{c}_{b}\right]\ \left[\bar{{U}}_{c}\,\bar{G}^{n}_{V\mu}{\cal X}_{n}\right]\right\}\ ,

(1)

where

G^{n}_{V\mu}\equiv\gamma_{\mu}\left(g_{L}\,{\cal U}^{*}_{1n}P_{L}+g_{R}\,{\cal U}_{2n}P_{R}\right)
,

\bar{G}^{n}_{V\mu}=\gamma_{\mu}\left(g_{L}^{*}\,{\cal U}_{1n}P_{L}+g_{R}^{*}\,{\cal U}^{*}_{2n}P_{R}\right)
,

\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}=\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}=\gamma^{\mu}\tilde{g}
,
with the
{\cal U}
being the
2\times 2
unitary matrix that takes us to the
{\cal X}_{n}
mass basis as worked out in Ref.
[
4
]
.
We define, for future use, the combinations
\hat{g}_{L_{n}}\equiv g_{L}\,{\cal U}^{*}_{1n}
and
\hat{g}_{R_{n}}\equiv g_{R}\,{\cal U}_{2n}
.
An equivalent form with charge-conjugated fields is derived in Ref.
[
4
]
,
and is given in terms of

G^{n}_{\Lambda\mu}=G^{n}_{V\mu}|_{(P_{L}\leftrightarrow P_{R})}
and
\bar{G}^{n}_{\Lambda\mu}=\bar{G}^{n}_{V\mu}|_{(P_{L}\leftrightarrow P_{R})}

(i.e.
G_{\Lambda}
is the corresponding
G_{V}
with
P_{L}
and
P_{R}
interchanged).
The Feynman rules given in Ref.
[
4
]
are reproduced in Fig.
1
.

\phantom{+}\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}\epsilon^{abc}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}\otimes G^{n}_{V\mu}

\phantom{+}\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}\epsilon^{abc}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}\otimes\bar{G}^{n}_{V\mu}

-\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}\epsilon^{abc}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}\otimes G^{n}_{\Lambda\mu}

-\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}\epsilon^{abc}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}\otimes\bar{G}^{n}_{\Lambda\mu}

Figure 1:
The Feynman rules for the VV interaction, from Ref.
[
4
]
.

The couplings are shown in the form
(...)\otimes(...)
, where the first factor is for the
{D}^{c}-{D}
fermion line,
and the second factor for the
{\cal X}_{n}-{U}
line.
On the fields, the superscript
c
denotes the charge conjugate, and the subscript
\{a,b,c\}
denotes the color index,
with the color indices contracted using the
\epsilon^{abc}
as shown.
The direction of the arrow denotes the flow of fermion number (also baryon number),
and even though the Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
have indefinite baryon number due to their Majorana mass,
we show an arrow on the
{\cal X}_{n}
line indicating the flow direction in the Dirac limit, i.e. when the Majorana mass is zero.
In this convention, the
{\cal X}_{n}
propagator with clashing arrows indicates a Majorana mass insertion.

Although our main focus is the effective operator in Eq. (
1
),
we also include in our study other associated operators
arising in some ultraviolet (UV) completion examples considered in Ref.
[
4
]
.
These are

{\cal L}\supset-\frac{1}{\Lambda^{2}}\,[\bar{{U}}_{c}\bar{G}^{n}_{V\mu}{\cal X}_{n}]\,[\overline{{\cal X}}_{m}{G^{m}_{V}}^{\mu}{U}_{c}]-\frac{1}{2\Lambda^{2}}(\delta^{aa^{\prime}}\delta^{bb^{\prime}}-\delta^{ab^{\prime}}\delta^{ba^{\prime}})[\overline{{D}}^{c}_{b}\,\tilde{g}\gamma^{\mu}{D}_{a}]\,[\bar{{D}}_{a^{\prime}}\,\tilde{g}\gamma_{\mu}{D}^{c}_{b^{\prime}}]\ ,

(2)

which are self-conjugate.
The Feynman rules for these vertices are reproduced in Fig.
2
.

-\frac{i}{\Lambda^{2}}\bar{G}_{V}^{n\mu}\otimes G^{m}_{V\mu}

-\frac{i}{\Lambda^{2}}G_{\Lambda}^{n\mu}\otimes\bar{G}^{m}_{\Lambda\mu}

\frac{i}{\Lambda^{2}}\bar{G}_{V}^{n\mu}\otimes G^{m}_{\Lambda\mu}

\frac{i}{\Lambda^{2}}\bar{G}_{\Lambda}^{n\mu}\otimes G^{m}_{V\mu}

\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}(\delta^{aa^{\prime}}\delta^{bb^{\prime}}-\delta^{ab^{\prime}}\delta^{ba^{\prime}})\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}\otimes\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V\mu}

Figure 2:
The Feynman rules of the other operators associated with the VV interaction, from Ref.
[
4
]
.

2.1
Computing the baryon asymmetry

We obtain the baryon asymmetry by following the method described in detail in Ref.
[
4
]
.
For the
{\cal X}_{n}
decay, we write the decay amplitude as

{\cal A}^{n}={\cal A}_{0}^{n}+{\cal A}_{1}^{n}
in terms of the tree and loop-level amplitudes,
and for the conjugate process as
{\cal A}^{cn}={\cal A}^{cn}_{0}+{\cal A}^{cn}_{1}
.
We have the decay widths given by
\Gamma^{n}\propto|{\cal A}^{n}|^{2}
and
\Gamma^{cn}\propto|{\cal A}^{cn}|^{2}
.
The baryon asymmetry arises due to a difference in the decay rates
for the process

\Gamma^{n}\equiv\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{D}\widebar{{D}^{c}}{U})

and for the conjugate process

\Gamma^{cn}\equiv\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{D}^{c}\widebar{{D}}{U}^{c})
.
The total decay width for the Majorana
{\cal X}_{n}
is the sum over the process and conjugate process partial widths,
i.e.

\Gamma_{{\cal X}_{n}}=\Gamma^{n}+\Gamma^{cn}
.

We find that there is no baryon asymmetry with only the leading order (LO), i.e. tree level, contribution.
When we include loop contributions
a nonzero difference in decay widths between the process and conjugate process could potentially arise,
implying a nonzero baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}
.
For a nonzero
{\cal A}_{B}
to arise, as explained in detail in Ref.
[
4
]
,
along with a nonzero
weak phase
that switches sign in going from the process to the conjugate process,
a
strong phase
that does not switch sign must also be present.
The latter can come from loop diagrams with on-shell intermediate states,
giving a discontinuity in the amplitude,
with each cut giving a factor of
i
(strong phase
\delta=\pi/2
).
Such a discontinuity adds to the loop amplitude
{\cal A}_{1}
the imaginary piece
i\hat{\cal A}_{1}
,
which in turn adds to the interference term
{\cal A}_{01}
the piece

i\hat{\cal A}_{01}=i\hat{\cal A}_{1}{\cal A}_{0}^{*}
,
and we write its contribution to the decay rate as
i\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
.
Similarly, for the conjugate process, the discontinuity adds the piece
i\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{c}
.
We need to carry only this discontinuity piece for obtaining
{\cal A}_{B}
as the other terms are common for the process and conjugate process
and cancel in the difference.
Using the discontinuity, we obtain
[
4
]

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{n}=\frac{1}{2M_{n}}\int d\Pi_{3}\ {\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{n}-\hat{\cal A}^{nc}_{01})=\frac{1}{2M_{n}}\int d\Pi_{3}\ 2\,{\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{n})\ ,

(3)

where
d\Pi_{3}
is the 3-body phase-space measure,
and in obtaining the last equality, we have used
{\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}_{01}-\hat{\cal A}^{c}_{01})=2\,{\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}_{01})

that follows if the weak phases are of opposite sign for the process and conjugate process,
which we will show explicitly later is the case in our setup.
From this, the leading contribution to the baryon asymmetry is

{\cal A}_{B}^{n}\equiv\frac{\Gamma^{n}-\Gamma^{cn}}{\Gamma^{n}+\Gamma^{cn}}\approx-\frac{\Delta\hat{\Gamma}^{n}_{01}}{\Gamma^{n}_{0}}\ ;\quad{\cal A}_{B}=\sum_{n=1,2}{\cal A}_{B}^{n}\ ,

(4)

where
the approximation is by taking the denominator at tree-level for the leading contribution to
{\cal A}_{B}
,
and,
we write the total baryon asymmetry from decay
{\cal A}_{B}
as the sum over the contributions
{\cal A}_{B}^{n}
from
n=1,2
.

Proceeding along similar lines to decay, we argue in Ref.
[
4
]

that a baryon asymmetry could also be generated in the scattering processes,
SC-1:
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\bar{D}(k_{i})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1}){U}(q_{2})
, and
SC-2:
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\bar{U}(k_{i})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1})\widebar{D}^{c}(q_{2})
.
Writing the cross section difference between the SC-1, SC-2 process and its corresponding conjugate process
in terms of the tree-loop interference term

\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}=\hat{\sigma}_{01}-\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{c}=\frac{1}{v}\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\int d\Pi_{2}\ {\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}_{01}-\hat{\cal A}^{c\,(\sigma)}_{01})=2\,{\rm Im}(\hat{\sigma}_{01})\ .

(5)

where
d\Pi_{2}
is the 2-body phase space measure,
we define the scattering baryon asymmetry as

{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\equiv\frac{\sigma^{n}-\sigma^{cn}}{\sigma^{n}+\sigma^{cn}}\approx-\frac{\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}}{\sigma_{0}^{n}}\ ;\quad{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}=\sum_{n=1,2}{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\ ,

(6)

where
\sigma_{0}
is the LO cross section.

This completes a brief summary of the theoretical framework
and the baryon asymmetry generation mechanism put forth in Ref.
[
4
]
.
In the rest of this paper,
we compute in this theory the baryon asymmetry in
{\cal X}
decay and scattering processes and their thermal averages.
We show the expressions we obtain for the matrix elements as Dirac traces.
We evaluate these traces
to yield dot-products of the 4-momenta involved,
but will not present these lengthy expressions as they are not particularly illuminative.
Based on a numerical computation, we present the size of the baryon asymmetry generated in
{\cal X}
decay and scattering processes
and study their dependence on the theory parameters.

3
The Baryon Asymmetry from
{\cal X}
Decays

For obtaining the baryon asymmetry
between the decay process
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
and its conjugate process
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D}D^{c}U^{c}
,
as reviewed in Sec.
2.1
,
we need to compute the interference term between the tree-level and loop-level,
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{n}
(cf. Eq. (
3
)).
We discuss this computation and the baryon asymmetry we obtain in this section
(cf. Appendix
E
).
We discuss next the tree and loop level contributions to the
{\cal X}_{n}
decay process.

3.1
The tree-level decay width

In Fig.
3
(left) we show the Feynman diagram for the

{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
decay at LO, i.e. tree-level in the VV effective interaction, giving the decay width
\Gamma_{0}^{n}
.

Figure 3:

The tree-level (LO)
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
decay process Feynman diagram (left).
The momentum, spin index, and color index, are shown as
(q,s,a)
respectively.
The LO
\Gamma_{0}
with the dashed lines showing the cut propagators giving 3-body phase-space (right).

We write the LO matrix element as

i{{\cal A}^{n}_{0}}=\frac{i}{2\Lambda^{2}}C_{0}\,({{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U}})^{\mu}_{n}\,(M^{12}_{0})_{\mu}\ ,

(7)

where

(M^{12}_{0})
is the
{D}{D}
part,

C_{0}\equiv\epsilon^{abc}/\sqrt{6}
is the color-factor
in which the
1/\sqrt{6}
is from the final state normalization
(cf. Appendix
E.1
).
The amplitude
{\cal A}_{0}^{cn}
for the conjugate process
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\bar{D}D^{c}U^{c}

is got from Eq. (
7
)
by replacing
({{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U}})_{n}\rightarrow({{\cal M}_{0}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}})_{n}
in which
{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{n}\phantom{\,\!}}\rightarrow{G_{\Lambda}^{n}}
, and we have
(M^{c12}_{0})=(M^{12}_{0})
.

We evaluate the 3-body phase-space element equivalently as a cut of a loop diagram as we show in Fig.
3
(right).
We thus obtain the LO decay width

\Gamma_{0}^{n}=\frac{1}{2M_{n}}\int d\Pi_{3}\ |{\cal A}_{0}^{n}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2M_{n}}\frac{1}{4\Lambda^{4}}\int d\Pi_{3}\ (\frac{1}{2}|{{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U\,\!}}|^{2})^{\mu\tau}_{nn}\,C_{00}\,(L_{12})_{\mu\tau}\ ,

(8)

where
C_{00}=C_{0}^{2}=\epsilon^{abc}\epsilon^{abc}/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=1
is the color factor,
the 1/2 in front of the
|{{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U\,\!}}|^{2}
is from averaging over the initial state spins,
and we have

(|{{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U\,\!}}|^{2})^{\mu\tau}_{nn}={\rm Tr}[{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{n}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.03125pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}p_{n}+M_{n}){G_{V}^{n}}^{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{U}+M_{U})]\ ;\quad(L_{12})_{\mu\tau}=2|\tilde{g}|^{2}{\rm Tr}[\gamma_{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)\gamma_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]\ .

(9)

The conjugate process LO decay width
\Gamma_{0}^{cn}
is obtained similarly using
|{\cal A}_{0}^{cn}|^{2}
above.
We can write Eq. (
8
) in terms of the dimensionless
f_{00}^{n}
as
(cf. Appendix
E.2
)

\Gamma_{0}^{n}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{n}^{5}}{4\Lambda^{4}}f_{00}^{n}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{5}}{\Lambda^{4}}\right)\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}\ ;\quad{\rm where}\ \hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}\equiv\frac{1}{8}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{5}f_{00}^{n}\ .

(10)

This also defines a dimensionless rate
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}
with the
M_{\chi}
and
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)
factors removed.
The
M_{n}^{5}
scaling can be anticipated on dimensional grounds.
As already mentioned above, for obtaining the baryon asymmetry at leading order, we take the dimensionless total width to be
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\cal X}_{n}}\approx 2\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}
.

We find no rate asymmetry at tree-level, and have to go to higher order in the VV interaction in order to potentially have a nonzero baryon asymmetry.
We turn next to identifying loop diagrams with this goal in mind.
We first discuss loop contributions involving only the single operator in Eq. (
1
),
and following this, with multiple operators including those in Eq. (
2
).

3.2
Single operator loop contribution to decay baryon asymmetry

We identify here loop diagrams involving only the operator of Eq. (
1
)
using the Feynman vertices in Fig.
1
,
and compute the resulting decay rate difference in the tree-loop interference terms
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
between the process and conjugate process.

In Fig.
4
, we show the Feynman diagrams contributing to the

{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
decay at the loop-level, for each
n=\{1,2\}
.

Figure 4:

The
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
loop processes A
1
, B
1
, C
1
(left, middle, right).
The dashed curve shows a cut in the loop, contributing a factor of
i
.

We show three 2-loop processes A
1
, B
1
, C
1
in Fig.
4
,
while a fourth process D
1
differs from C
1
by switching the
D
and
\widebar{D^{c}}
in each of the right two vertices
and yields the same contribution as C
1
due to them being identical particles, which we therefore do not show explicitly.
The
\otimes
shows the Majorana mass insertion
M_{m}
on the
{\cal X}_{m}
line (with
m=\{1,2\}
) that violates baryon number.
The clash in the arrows in the
{\cal X}_{m}
line signals the baryon number violation due to the Majorana mass.

We show in the figure the momenta, spin index, and color index on each fermion line as
(q,s,a)
, or sometimes as
(q,a)
with the spin label suppressed.
The final state labels are the same as in Fig.
3
.
Momentum conservation implies
q_{2}=p_{n}-q_{1}-q_{U}
,
q_{4}=p_{n}-q_{3}-q^{\prime}_{U}
, and
{p^{\prime}_{n}}=p_{n}-(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{3}+q_{4})
.
We are interested in the unpolarized decay width and therefore sum over all spins.
We carefully handle the fact that the
\widebar{D^{c}},D
lead to two identical fermions in the final state.

We do not show the loop diagrams for the conjugate process
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D}D^{c}U^{c}
in the figure,
but obtain these by switching fields and their charge-conjugated fields, i.e.
\psi\leftrightarrow\psi^{c}
,
which implies reversing all arrows on the fermion lines.
The VV interaction with the fields conjugated is derived in Ref.
[
4
]
,
and the Feynman vertices are included in Fig.
1
.
As explained in Ref.
[
4
]
, for the charge conjugated fields,
the fermion-number-flow arrow is to be read in the opposite sense in picking the spinors and the sign of the mass term in the propagator.
The process and conjugate process matrix element expressions have the
u,v
spinors in the same places,
but only the coupling is changed to the corresponding conjugated forms given in the Feynman rules.
To obtain the difference in the decay width and the baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}
,
we compute the difference between the tree-loop interference terms for the process and conjugate process,
and integrate over phase-space.
In our expressions below, we are interested only in the terms that survive when the difference is taken,
and not the common terms that cancel.

The loop contributions in diagrams
(i)
= {A
1
, B
1
, C
1
} in Fig.
4
are

i{{\cal A}^{n}_{1}}=\frac{-i}{8\Lambda^{6}}\int d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}\,\sum_{m,(i)}\frac{{({{\cal M}_{1(i)}^{\chi U\,\!}})}^{\sigma\mu\nu}_{nm}}{[{q^{\prime}_{U}}^{2}-M_{U}^{2}+i\epsilon][q_{3}^{2}-M_{D}^{2}+i\epsilon][q_{4}^{2}-M_{D}^{2}+i\epsilon]}\,\frac{C_{1}^{(i)}(M^{12}_{1(i)}L^{34}_{(i)})_{\mu\sigma\nu}}{[{p^{\prime}_{n}}^{\!\!(i)\,2}-M_{m}^{2}+i\epsilon]}\ ,

(11)

where
the
(i)
on the various factors
show that they depend on the particular diagram,

M^{12}_{1(i)}
denotes the
{D}{D}
part of the 3-body final-state phase-space,

L^{34}_{(i)}
stands for the
{D}{D}
loop part,
and

C_{1}^{(i)}
is the color factor.

d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}
is the loop integration measure.
The loop contribution for the conjugate process is obtained by making the change
({{\cal M}_{1}^{\chi U\,\!}})\rightarrow({{\cal M}_{1}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}})
above with

{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}\phantom{\,\!}}\leftrightarrow{G_{\Lambda}}
, and with
M^{c12}_{1},(L^{c}_{34})
.

The A
1
and C
1
diagrams generate a UV finite dimension-6 operator
when computed in terms of the renormalized masses, couplings and wave-functions,
and we think of our expressions being in terms of these renormalized quantities.

The B
1
diagram (with
p^{\prime}_{n}=p_{n}
), however, has a
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function piece,
which being a relevant operator, could potentially be divergent.
This has to be renormalized (subtracted) properly to be consistent with being in the mass basis.
We explain our renormalization and subtraction scheme for this 2-point function in Appendix
A
.
The divergence is only in the hermitian principal value (PV) part of the 2-point function
and is subtracted with mass and wave-function renormalization counterterms.
The subtraction ensures that we leave the
{\cal X}_{n}
masses
M_{n}

uncorrected by the loop contributions at the on-shell point, consistent with being in the mass basis.
The discontinuity in the 2-point function gives a non-hermitian (dissipative or absorptive) piece,
which we take without any subtraction as there is no counterterm for this.
For
m\!\!=\!\!n
with both legs being the same particle and on-shell, this dissipative piece
yields the LO decay width
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{c}
respectively.
For the diagram B contribution, what is picked up is only the discontinuity (in the imaginary part)
of the
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function for
m\!\!\neq\!\!n

with an offshell
{\cal X}_{m}
and the cut on the intermediate
U^{c}D^{c}\bar{D}
giving a strong phase of
i
.
The discontinuity for
m\!\!=\!\!n

will not contribute to the baryon asymmetry since it puts
{\cal X}_{m}
also on-shell,
giving an extra
i

and leaving no strong phase in the amplitude.
Also, for
m\!\!=\!\!n
, the other possibility of
{\cal X}_{m}
onshell and no
UDD
cut,
although has a single factor of
i
as the strong phase,
does not survive since this is at the subtraction point at which the counterterm fully cancels it.
Therefore, the discontinuity in the
n\!\!\neq\!\!m
contribution
that leaves the
{\cal X}_{m}
offshell as an intermediate state at
p_{n}^{2}\!\!=\!\!M_{n}^{2}

is the only contribution to the baryon asymmetry from the B
1
diagram.
The enhancement in the B
1
amplitude as
M_{m}\!\rightarrow\!M_{n}
for
m\!\neq\!n
can enhance the baryon asymmetry
and is the idea in
resonant baryogenesis
;
for recent studies along these lines, but with SS interaction,
see for example Refs.
[
6
,
7
]
and references therein.

For
M_{n}>(2M_{D}+M_{U})
, for any
n
,
the three
{D},{D},{U}
intermediate states can all simultaneously go on-shell in the loop diagrams
of Fig.
4
, shown as the dashed curve in the diagrams.
The Cutkosky rule (see for example Ref.
[
8
]
) prescribes that a cut propagator be replaced as

1/(k^{2}-m^{2}+i\epsilon)\rightarrow-2\pi i\delta(k^{2}-m^{2})
.
Cutting three propagators then gives a factor of

(-2\pi i)^{3}=(2\pi)^{3}i=(2\pi)^{3}e^{i\pi/2}
, i.e. an extra phase of
\pi/2
,
that is the
same
for the process and the conjugate process, acting as a strong phase
[
4
]
.
Furthermore, for simplicity,
we choose the masses such that the
{U}\rightarrow{\cal X}_{m}{D}^{c}\bar{D}
decay is not kinematically allowed
so that a second cut cannot be made in the loop contributions
which can be ensured with
M_{m}>(M_{U}-2M_{D}),\forall m
.
If the second cut can be made, its contributions can also be included in the same way.

The discontinuity added by the cut propagators is
i{\hat{{\cal A}}^{n}_{1}}
, as discussed in Sec.
2.1
.
Replacing the cut denominators by the corresponding
\delta(...)
functions in Eq. (
11
), we find
{\hat{{\cal A}}^{n}_{1}}
to be

\displaystyle{\hat{{\cal A}}^{n}_{1}}=-\frac{1}{8\Lambda^{6}}\int d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}\sum_{m,(i)}{({{\cal M}_{1(i)}^{\chi U\,\!}})}^{\sigma\mu\nu}_{nm}\frac{C_{1}^{(i)}((M^{12}_{1})(L_{34}))_{\mu\sigma\nu}^{(i)}}{[{p^{\prime}_{n}}^{\!\!(i)\,2}-M_{m}^{2}]}\,\left[\frac{1}{2}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{\rm cut}_{34U}\right]\ ,

(12)

\displaystyle{\rm where}\ \delta^{\rm cut}_{34U}=\delta(q_{3}^{2}-M_{D}^{2})\delta(q_{4}^{2}-M_{D}^{2})\delta({q^{\prime}_{U}}^{2}-M_{U}^{2})\ ,

where the 1/2 in the last
[...]
is from the
{\rm Im}(...)=(-i/2){\rm Disc}(...)
.
Since we take
M_{m}>(M_{U}-2M_{D}),\forall m
, as mentioned earlier,
we cannot have
{\cal X}_{m}
on-shell and therefore we omit the
+i\epsilon
in the denominator.
We compute from Eq. (
12
) and (the conjugate of) Eq. (
7
)
the tree-loop interference term
\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{n}={{\cal A}^{n}_{0}}^{*}{\hat{{\cal A}}^{n}_{1}}
,
and take the average over initial-state spins and sum over final-state spins and colors, to get the interference term due to the discontinuity as

\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{n}=-\frac{1}{(16\Lambda^{8})}\int d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}\,\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{m,(i)}\frac{1}{2}{({{\cal M}_{01(i)}^{\chi U\,\!}})}^{\sigma\mu\nu\tau}_{nm}\frac{C_{01}^{(i)}(M^{12}_{1(i)}L^{34}_{(i)})_{\mu\sigma\nu}(M^{12}_{0})^{\dagger}_{\tau}}{[{p^{\prime}_{n}}^{\!\!(i)\,2}-M_{m}^{2}]}\,\ ,

(13)

where
the color factor
C_{01}^{(i)}=C_{0}C_{1}^{(i)}
,
the 1/2 in front of
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}
is from averaging over the initial state spins.
We present the
{D}
-loop factors later (cf. Eq. (
16
)).
For the conjugate process, the analogous factor,
{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}
,
is obtained by making the change
{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}\phantom{\,\!}}\leftrightarrow{G_{\Lambda}}
in
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}
above.
2
2

2

Including
the
\overline{{D}^{c}}(...){D}
part of the coupling also, the change is more correctly

[\gamma^{\mu}\otimes{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}\phantom{\,\!}}]\rightarrow[(-\gamma^{\mu})\otimes(-{G_{\Lambda}})]
,
but can effectively be thought of as
[\gamma^{\mu}\otimes{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}\phantom{\,\!}}]\rightarrow[\gamma^{\mu}\otimes{G_{\Lambda}}]
as stated above.

We now make an important observation from the expression for
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}
and
{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}
.
In the limit of the
{\cal X}_{n}
masses
M_{1,2}
becoming equal, all kinematical factors become independent of
n,m
,
and given that
{\cal A}_{B}\propto\int\sum_{n,m}\,{\rm Im}({{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}-{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}})
,
the
n,m
dependence is now only due to their dependence on
the couplings
\hat{g}_{n,m}
(and their conjugates) in Eq. (
13
).
We can explicitly show that the coupling structure in
{\rm Im}({{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}})
(or in
{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}
) when summed over
n,m

goes to zero in this limit owing to the unitarity of the
{\cal U}
.
This is expected since in this limit, the two Majorana
{\cal X}_{n}
can be assembled into a single Dirac fermion
with fermion number (baryon number) conserved in the theory,
which therefore implies that
{\rm Im}({{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}})
must go to zero in this limit
since it is a fermion number violating contribution, as evidenced by the clashing arrows in the
{\cal X}_{m}
propagator
in Fig.
4
.
In our theory, as discussed in Ref.
[
4
]
,

C
-invariance is present if and only if
\hat{g}_{L_{n}}^{*}=\hat{g}_{R_{n}}
.
Taking this limit in our expressions leads to a zero baryon asymmetry
(cf. Appendix
G.2
).
Next,
CP
-invariance is present if and only if
\hat{g}_{L_{n}},\hat{g}_{R_{n}}
are real, and we find a zero baryon asymmetry in this limit.
Lastly, in the “Dirac limit”, i.e. the
\tilde{M}_{L,R}=0
, we find a zero baryon asymmetry.
It is reassuring that our formalism passes these nontrivial checks.

Figure 5:
The tree-loop interference term diagrams A, B, C (left, middle, right respectively) required to compute
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
,
in which the left cuts in each diagram gives the imaginary piece to the amplitude
while the right cuts yields the 3-body final-state.
The conjugate process diagrams are obtained by reversing all fermion-line arrows.

\hat{\Gamma}_{01}

The fourth diagram D,

The interference term for the conjugate process
\Gamma^{c}_{01}

The difference between these contributions yields
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}

The cut shown by the dashed curve on the left gives the discontinuity as noted in Sec.

With this cut made, the diagrams in Fig.

and the superscript

Thus, in Eq. (

(16)

where the color factors are
C_{01}^{A,C}=12/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=2
,
C_{01}^{B}=36/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=6
.

We can write the single operator contribution to the decay width difference in terms of the dimensionless
f_{01}
as

\displaystyle\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{M_{n}^{9}}{16\Lambda^{8}}\sum_{m}2f_{01}^{nm}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{9}}{\Lambda^{8}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{16}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{9}\sum_{m}f_{01}^{nm}\right]\ ,

(17)

where we have a factor of 2 in
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{n}
from 2 Im(…) including the imaginary piece from the conjugate process.
Using Eqs. (
10
) and (
17
) in Eq. (
4
) we get

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{B}^{n}=-\frac{M_{n}^{4}}{4\Lambda^{4}}\frac{\sum_{m}2f_{01}^{nm}}{f_{00}^{n}}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}}{\Lambda}\right)^{4}\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n}\ ;\quad{\rm where}\ \hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n}\equiv\left[-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{4}\frac{\sum_{m}f_{01}^{nm}}{f_{00}^{n}}\right]\ .

(18)

For the conjugate process, as already discussed,
{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}
is got by
{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}\phantom{\,\!}}\leftrightarrow{G_{\Lambda}}
in
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}

with the
L_{D}
unchanged,
which is
\hat{g}_{\lambda n}\rightarrow\hat{g}^{*}_{\lambda n}
and
P_{L}\leftrightarrow P_{R}
.
The imaginary part of the
\hat{{\cal M}}_{k\,R,L}
are due to the
\pm\gamma^{5}
in
P_{R,L}
,
and therefore we have the property
\hat{{\cal M}}_{k\,R,L}=\hat{{\cal M}}_{k\,L,R}^{*}
.
This then implies
{{\cal M}_{01\,nm}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}={{\cal M}_{01\,nm}^{\chi U\,*}}
,
and because
\hat{L}_{D}
is real, this again demonstrates explicitly that
\hat{\cal A}^{c}_{01}=\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{*}
,

which we used in writing Eq. (
3
).

Next, we choose some example values of masses, couplings and phases, compute the
integrals numerically,
and obtain the
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
and
{\cal A}_{B}
.
We choose these parameters such that the conditions discussed above
for
C
-invariance and
CP
-invariance
do not hold, allowing for a potentially nonzero
{\cal A}_{B}
.

3.2.1
Numerical Analysis

In order to illustrate the size of the baryon asymmetry that is generated in our effective theory from the decay of the
{\cal X}_{n}
,
we take a sample parameter-space point “
benchmark point A
” (BP-A) with values:

{\rm\bf BP\!\!-\!\!A}\!:M_{D}=0.25,\ M_{U}=0.375,\ M_{\chi}=1,\ \tilde{M}_{L}=0.1,\ |\tilde{M}_{R}|=0.11,\ \phi^{\prime}_{R}=-\pi/3\ ,

where the masses we show are the values scaled by
M_{\chi}
.
For the above choices, the
{\cal X}_{n}
mass eigenvalues, are
M_{1}/M_{\chi}=0.91
and
M_{2}/M_{\chi}=1.09
.
Although our analytical treatment is general, we restrict our numerical analysis to
\tilde{M}_{L,R}\ll M_{\chi}
, the so-called
pseudo-Dirac
limit.
The choice of
M_{D},M_{U}
implicitly assumes that the
{D},{U}
are BSM states since they are a sizable fraction of
M_{\chi}
.
However, if the
{D},{U}
are SM quarks (cf. Ref
[
4
]
) and the
M_{D},M_{U}
are much smaller, qualitatively our results should be similar.

We evaluate all the kinematical quantities and the Dirac traces using
FeynCalc
(version 9.3.0)
[
9
]
.
We perform the numerical integrations in
Mathematica
(version 13) using the integration technique implemented therein.
The errors quoted in our results below are integration errors which we propagate to
\Gamma_{0}
,
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
and
{\cal A}_{B}
.
We ensure that these errors are better than about 10 %.
Next, we show for BP-A the

\Gamma_{{\cal X}}
,
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
, and
{\cal A}_{B}
values for some sample coupling choices,
and highlight their dependence on the parameters of the theory.

For the BP-A, with the values of the integrals evaluated numerically, if we additionally take, for example,
\tilde{g}=1
,
g_{L}=1
,
\phi_{L}=0
,
g_{R}=0
,
we find the following (dimensionless) values:

\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{(n=1)}=1.5\times 10^{-8}
;
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{(n=2)}=8.5\times 10^{-7}
;

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{(n=2)}=-2.3\times 10^{-12}
;
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(n=2)}=2.7\times 10^{-6}
.

We omit showing the
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
and
\hat{\cal A}_{B}
values for (n=1) as they are about a factor of 100 smaller.
The
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}
values shown are to be multiplied by
(M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})
, the
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
by
(M_{\chi}^{9}/\Lambda^{8})
, and the
\hat{\cal A}_{B}
by
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{4}
,
to obtain the actual values.
We can then write for
n\!=\!2

the actual total decay width
\Gamma_{{\cal X}_{2}}\sim 10^{-6}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}(M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})
,
the actual tree-loop interference decay width difference

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\sim

-10^{-12}\,\tilde{g}^{4}g_{L,R}^{4}(M_{\chi}^{9}/\Lambda^{8})
,
and
the actual baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}\sim 10^{-6}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{4}
,
including the couplings scaling.

From the above
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
numbers given for BP-A,
g_{L}=1,g_{R}=0,\tilde{g}=1
,
we extract the LO width and tree-loop interference functions parametrized
in Eqs. (25) and (26) respectively of Ref.
[
4
]
.
For
n\!\!=\!\!2
, we find these to be:

\hat{f}_{00}\approx 2.7\times 10^{-4}
,
and

\hat{f}_{01}\approx-2.4\times 10^{-6}
.
We can trace the reason for the suppression in
\hat{f}_{00}

to a 3-body phase-space suppression leading to a suppressed weight in the phase space measure
due to the 3-momentum magnitude being about
{\cal O}(0.1)

for the decay kinematics in BP-A.
Similarly,
we can trace the reason for the suppressed
\hat{f}_{01}

to a product of the suppression factor in the 3-body phase-space as above in the LO integrals,
and another
10^{-3}
factor from the suppressed weight in the loop-integral measure
due to the cut of the loop-diagram picking out similar sizes of loop-momenta as phase-space momenta,
i.e. suppressed
{\cal O}(0.1)
3-momentum magnitudes.

and
