interchanged.

We discuss in Appendix
D
the UV state
\xi
propagator correction resolving the VV effective interaction.
In the decay processes considered here, we expect the effect of these corrections to be minimal
since in decays, the momenta are limited by
M_{\chi}
,
and the propagator corrections begin to kick-in for vertex momenta
\gtrsim\Lambda/M_{\chi}
.
Furthermore, the 3-momentum magnitudes are much lesser than
M_{\chi}
given the sizeable final state
M_{{U},{D}}
masses.
The loop 3-momentum magnitudes are also limited since we enforce the cuts on the
{U},{D}
propagators.
We perform a numerical computation with the UV propagator correction included and find that indeed the
correction to
\Gamma_{{\cal X}_{n}}
,
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
, and
{\cal A}_{B}
are smaller than a few percent.
We therefore have only presented our
{\cal X}
decay baryon asymmetry results without these UV propagator corrections included.

We find that diagrams without an arrow clash do not contribute a baryon asymmetry
(cf. Appendix
E.4.1
).
Next, we explore potential contributions to
{\cal A}_{B}
due to adding operators other than the one we have been focusing on.

3.3
Multiple operator loop contribution to decay baryon asymmetry

The UV completion examples in Ref.
[
4
]
suggest that
associated with the VV interaction of Eq.(
1
) considered in the previous section,
other operators such as those in Eq. (
2
) could also be generated.
Here we include these other operators also and ask if potentially new contributions to
{\cal A}_{B}
could arise,
adding to the baryon asymmetry found in the previous section.

We show in Fig.
8
such potential new contributions
to the
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\overline{{D}^{c}}{D}{U}
decay,
characterized by using two different effective operators
whose Feynman rules are given in Figs.
1
and
2
.
3
3

3

In Ref.
[
10
]
, a GUT UV completion is considered with an SS operator of the type

[\overline{{D}}\Gamma\chi]\,[\bar{\chi}\Gamma{D}]
.
A
VV
operator with these fields is not in the set we are considering here
(for related details on the UV completion, see Ref.
[
4
]
),
but the contribution from analogous operators in our effective theory is that of diagram
A_{1}^{\prime(1)}
.

The diagrams for the conjugate mode
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\overline{{D}}{D}^{c}{U}^{c}
are obtained by
reversing the arrows in Fig.
8
,
i.e. by taking the conjugate of the fields shown.

Figure 8:

The
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{D^{c}}DU
loop diagrams A
{}_{1}^{\prime(1)}
(left), A
{}_{1}^{\prime(2)}
(middle), and A
{}_{1}^{\prime(3)}
(right),
arising from using more than one operator.

We take the independent 1-loop momentum that is integrated over as
q_{3}
,
and 4-momentum conservation implies
q_{4}=p_{n}-q_{3}-q_{U}
.
We do not show another diagram that involves a correction to the on-shell
{U}
leg which is subtracted away
when renormalized fields are used.
If the required operators are present in the effective theory such that the one-loop contributions in Fig.
8

can be present,
and if the parameters are favorable as discussed below,
the baryon asymmetry generated by this could potentially be larger than the two-loop contributions we analyzed in Sec.
3.2
.
Using the formalism detailed in Sec.
2.1
we compute the decay baryon asymmetry from this mode, which we discuss next.

We denote the 1-loop amplitudes for the diagrams in Fig.
8

as
{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime(1)}
,
{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime(2)}
and
{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime(3)}
respectively,
and the corresponding loop amplitudes of the conjugate modes as

{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime c(1)}
,
{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime c(2)}
, and
{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime c(3)}
.
For a nonzero rate asymmetry,
in addition to having a weak phase that flips sign between the process and the conjugate process,
we must also have a common strong phase that does not flip sign.
Such a common phase can come from a cut of the loop when the
two intermediate state particles go on-shell.
In diagrams A
′(1)
and A
′(2)
, the loop can be cut only for
nm=21
provided
M_{2}>M_{1}+2M_{U}
, while in A
′(3)
a cut is always possible.
We assume here that this condition is satisfied.

We write the tree-loop interference term for the process as

{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1),(2),(3)}\equiv{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime(1),(2),(3)}{\cal A}_{0}^{*}
, where
{\cal A}_{0}
is the tree amplitude,
and for the conjugate process we correspondingly write
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime c(1),(2),(3)}\equiv{\cal A}_{1}^{\prime c(1),(2),(3)}{\cal A}_{0}^{c*}
.
The decay rate difference between the process and conjugate process is given by

\Delta\Gamma\,\propto\,\Delta{\cal A}^{\prime}_{01}\equiv{\cal A}^{\prime}_{01}-{\cal A}^{\prime c}_{01}
,
summed over the processes.
We include the 3-body phase-space as cuts of a loop integral,
and show the
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime}
diagrammatically in Fig.
9
.
The dashed curves represent the cuts, with the cut on the left yielding the strong phase and the cut on the right the 3-body phase space.

Figure 9:

A diagrammatic representation of the tree-loop interference terms

{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
(left),
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(2)}
(middle), and
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(3)}
(right),
corresponding to the contributions in Fig.
8
.
The dashed curves show the cuts.

Computing this with the cuts shown in Fig.
9
(left) gives

\displaystyle{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{C_{01}^{\prime(1)}}{(4\Lambda^{6})}{\rm Tr}[{G_{V}^{n}}_{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{U}+M_{U}){\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{m}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}+M_{m}){\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{\Lambda}^{m}\phantom{\,\!}}_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M_{U}){G_{\Lambda}^{n}}_{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.03125pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}p_{n}+M_{n})]\cdot

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt\tilde{g}^{2}{\rm Tr}[\gamma^{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M_{D})\gamma^{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M_{D})]\ ,

(19)

where the
1/2
is from averaging over the initial state spins,
and the color-factor
C_{01}^{\prime(1)}=\epsilon_{abc}\epsilon_{abc}/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=1
.
4
4

4

We notice that the contribution in Fig.
9
(left) is very similar to the one in Fig.
5
(left)
when one maps the
q_{3}q_{4}

{D}{D}
loop and the associated vertices in the former to the left-most vertex in the latter
(corresponding to a heavy UV field exchange).
Consequently, by mapping the momenta appropriately,
we see that Eq. (
19
) has the same structure as Eq. (
15
) with
L_{12}
included.
We also notice that the same number of
{G_{V}},{G_{\Lambda}}
couplings are involved in these two contributions.

We find no asymmetry from diagrams
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(2),(3)}

as we could anticipate from there being no arrow clash in these diagrams.

To illustrate the baryon asymmetry arising from diagram
A_{1}^{\prime(1)}
,
we define another parameter-space point with
M_{2}>M_{1}+2M_{U}
, namely, “
benchmark point B
” (BP-B) with values:

{\rm\bf BP\!\!-\!\!B}\!:M_{D}=0.05,\ M_{U}=0.05,\ M_{\chi}=1,\ \tilde{M}_{L}=0.1,\ |\tilde{M}_{R}|=0.11,\ \phi^{\prime}_{R}=-\pi/3\ ,

where again, the masses we show are the values scaled by
M_{\chi}
.
In BP-B, the decay mode
{\cal X}_{2}\rightarrow{\cal X}_{1}{U}\bar{U}
is also open (only for
n\!\!=\!\!2
) in addition to the earlier
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{U}{D}\overline{{D}^{c}}
.
We compute numerically the partial decay widths to the two modes for
\tilde{g}=1
,
g_{L}=1
,
g_{R}=0
,
and we find the LO decay widths in units of
(M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})
to be

\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{U}{D}\overline{{D}^{c}})=(3.1,7.8)\times 10^{-6}
for
n\!\!=\!\!(1,2)
,
while

\Gamma({\cal X}_{2}\rightarrow{\cal X}_{1}{U}\bar{U})=5.3\times 10^{-8}
.
Thus, the branching ratio to the second mode is about
0.3\,\%
.
The suppression is due to the small phase space available for the final-state in the second mode,
the 3-momentum magnitude being of the order
(M_{2}-(M_{1}+2M_{U}))/M_{2}\approx 0.07
for BP-B.

We obtain the baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}
contributed by
the tree-loop interference term
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
depicted in Fig.
9
(left)
following the discussion in Sec.
2.1
.
Given that this diagram has an arrow clash, we anticipate a potentially nonzero
{\cal A}_{B}
.
We make the cuts shown in the figure and obtain the
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
.
We obtain the tree-loop interference term for the conjugate process
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime c(1)}
by interchanging
{G_{V}}\leftrightarrow{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{\Lambda}\phantom{\,\!}}
.
We compute the difference
\Delta{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}={\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}-{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime c(1)}=2\,{\rm Im}({\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)})
,
and integrate this after folding in the 3-body phase-space measure
d\Pi_{3}
and the one-loop measure
d\Pi^{\prime}_{2}
with the cuts shown
to obtain the decay width difference,
which when written in terms of the scaled (hatted) momenta and masses is

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{7}}{\Lambda^{6}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime(1)}_{01}\right]\ ;\quad{\rm where}\ f^{\prime(1)}_{01}\equiv\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{7}\int d\hat{\Pi}_{3}\,d\hat{\Pi}_{2}^{\prime}\frac{1}{2}\,\Delta\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}\ ,

(20)

where the
M_{n}^{7}
scaling is expected on dimensional grounds.
We then obtain the baryon asymmetry due to the contribution from Fig.
9
(left) using Eq. (
4
) as

{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}=-\frac{\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}}{\Gamma^{n}_{0}}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}}{\Lambda}\right)^{2}\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}\ ;\quad{\rm where}\ \hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}\equiv\frac{1}{2}\frac{f^{\prime(1)}_{01}}{\hat{\Gamma}^{n}_{0}}\ .

(21)

As already discussed above, for the benchmark point BP-B,
the BR into the additional decay mode
{\cal X}_{2}\rightarrow{\cal X}_{1}{U}\bar{U}
is suppressed
compared to that into the
{\cal X}_{2}\rightarrow\overline{{D}^{c}}{D}{U}
mode,
and we therefore take the LO decay width
\Gamma_{0}
to be given by the latter (see Eq. (
10
)).

In Fig.
10
we show the total (dimensionless)
{\cal X}_{n}
LO decay width
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\cal X}_{n}}\approx 2\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}
,
for BP-B,
on the left as a function of
g_{L}
with
g_{L}+g_{R}=1
, and,
on the right as a function of
\phi_{L}
for
|g_{L}|=1/4
,
g_{R}=3/4
.
The shown
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\cal X}_{n}}
values are to be multiplied by
M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4}
to get the actual decay widths.

Figure 10:
For BP-B, the LO total
{\cal X}_{n}
decay width
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\cal X}_{n}}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4}
),
with
\tilde{g}=1
,
\phi_{L}=0
,
g_{L}
varied with
g_{L}+g_{R}=1
(left), and,
|g_{L}|=1/4
,
g_{R}=3/4
with
\phi_{L}
varied (right).

In Fig.
11
we show the resulting
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
,
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}

for various choices of couplings.

Figure 11:

For BP-B,
the multiple operator
{\cal X}_{n}
decay baryon asymmetry
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}
(in units of
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{2}
),
and the (negative of the) tree-loop interference decay width difference
-\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{7}/\Lambda^{6}
),
with
\tilde{g}=1
,
showing contours in the
(g_{L},g_{R})
plane with
\phi_{L}=0
(top panel);

|g_{L}|=1/4
,
g_{R}=3/4
with
\phi_{L}
varied (bottom panel).

We evaluate the integrals numerically and use them to compute the rate asymmetry.
For
g_{L}=1/4
,
g_{R}=3/4
, the decay widths
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{n}
are:
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{1}=1.8\times 10^{-6}
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{0}^{2}=4.7\times 10^{-6}
,
which are to be multiplied by
(M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})
to get the actual widths.
The tree-loop interference term from
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
is
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}=-1.3\times 10^{-13}
.
The resulting baryon asymmetry is
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}=2.7\times 10^{-8}
.
We can thus write for
n\!=\!2
the actual total decay width
\Gamma_{{\cal X}_{2}}\sim 10^{-5}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}(M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})
,
the actual interference term
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\sim-10^{-13}\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{4}(M_{\chi}^{7}/\Lambda^{6})

and the actual baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}\sim 10^{-8}\,g_{L,R}^{2}(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{2}
, including the couplings scaling.

Comparing the
\hat{\cal A}_{B}\sim 10^{-6}
we found from the single operator contribution for BP-A in Sec.
3.2
,
the suppressed
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}\sim 10^{-8}
from the multiple operator contribution for BP-B we find here is surprisingly small
considering the latter is one loop-order lower and is therefore expected to be enhanced.
The reason for the suppression is because we are in the pseudo-Dirac limit with
(M_{2}-M_{1})/M_{2}\sim 0.1
,
and since we require the
{\cal X}_{1}
to be on-shell in the loop,
the available phase-space and loop momenta are limited by
{q}_{U_{r}}\lesssim 0.1
, leading to a suppression in the integrals,
as we detailed above.
The smallness is the result of suppressions due to
the 3-body phase-space weight,
the loop measure weight
and,
the dimensionless
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
(without the
1/\Lambda^{6}
) of
\sim 10^{-4}
including the (imaginary part of) coupling factors.
However, it should be borne in mind that the actual
{\cal A}_{B}
has a
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
scaling factor
while
{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}
has a less suppressed
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
scaling factor due to which the above suppression
may be overcome.
From the above

\Gamma_{{\cal X}_{2}}
and
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
numbers given for BP-B,
g_{L}=1/4,g_{R}=3/4,\tilde{g}=1
,
we extract the LO width and tree-loop interference functions parametrized
in Eqs. (25) and (26) respectively of Ref.
[
4
]
.
For
n\!\!=\!\!2
, we find these to be:

\hat{f}_{00}\approx 4.3\times 10^{-4}
,
and

\hat{f}_{01}\approx-3\times 10^{-9}
.

In summary,
if the second operator is also present in the effective theory,
and if
M_{2}>M_{1}+2M_{U}
,
the
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
of Fig.
9
(left)
contributes to the baryon asymmetry.
Comparing this
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{01}
contribution to the single operator
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}
contribution of Sec.
3.2
, we have

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}^{\prime}/\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\sim 2\pi^{2}(1/\tilde{g}^{2})(\Lambda/M_{\chi})^{2}\,\hat{f}_{01}^{\prime}/\hat{f}_{01}\sim 0.1\,(1/\tilde{g}^{2})(\Lambda/M_{\chi})^{2}

for our parameter choices.
Thus, for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\lesssim 1/10
, the multiple operator contribution, if present, could dominate over the single operator contribution.
If any of the above conditions for the presence of the multiple operator contribution are not met,
the single operator baryon asymmetry contribution would remain as the only contribution.
We thermally average the decay width and the width difference so computed
as described in Appendix
B
, and obtain the
\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle
and
\langle\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\rangle
.

4
The Baryon Asymmetry from
{\cal X}
Scattering

Here we discuss the baryon asymmetry in
the scattering rates between the
2\rightarrow 2
scattering processes of
{\cal X}_{n}
with
{U},{D}
,
and the corresponding ones for
{\cal X}_{n}
with
{U}^{c},{D}^{c}
,
following up on our discussion in Ref.
[
4
]
.
In particular, we consider the difference in scattering cross sections for the process and that for its conjugate process
in each of the channels:

SC-1:
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\bar{D}(k_{i})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1}){U}(q_{2})
,

SC-2:
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\bar{U}(k_{i})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1})\widebar{D}^{c}(q_{2})
,

for
n=\{1,2\}
.
SC-1 includes both
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n}){D}^{c}(k_{i})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1}){U}(q_{2})
and
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\bar{D}(k_{i})\rightarrow\widebar{{D}^{c}}(q_{1}){U}(q_{2})
.
The conjugate processes are obtained by interchanging
Q\leftrightarrow Q^{c}
for
Q=\{{U},{D}\}
.
We only deal with unpolarized rates and therefore average over initial-state spins and sum over final-state spins.

The cross section for each of the SC-1 and SC-2 channels is

\sigma^{n}=\frac{1}{v}\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\int d\Pi_{2}\,|{\cal A}^{n(\sigma)}|^{2}\ ,

(22)

where

|{\cal A}^{n(\sigma)}|^{2}
is the scattering matrix element mod-squared,

v\equiv v_{rel}=|v_{n}-v_{i}|
is the relative velocity between the incoming particles,
we denote the energies of the various particles involved as

E_{n}\equiv E_{p_{n}}
,
E_{i}\equiv E_{k_{i}}
,
E_{1}\equiv E_{q_{1}}
,
E_{2}\equiv E_{q_{2}}
, etc.,
and,
the 3-momentum magnitude as
{p}_{r}\equiv\sqrt{{\bf p}\!\cdot\!{\bf p}}
.
The
d\Pi_{2}
is the final-state 2-body phase-space element.

We obtain the
|{\cal A}^{n(\sigma)}|^{2}
,
averaged over initial-state spins and summed over final-state spins,
in close analogy with the decay amplitude of Sec.
3
as we describe next.
Like the decay amplitude earlier,
the scattering amplitude too can be written as a sum over the tree and loop amplitudes

{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}={\cal A}_{0}^{(\sigma)}+{\cal A}_{1}^{(\sigma)}
for each
n
.
Using
crossing relations

[
8
]
, we can reuse the computation of the decay matrix element of Sec.
3

to obtain the scattering matrix element.
The tree and loop diagrams are similar to those shown in Figs.
3
,
4
, and
8
,
but with one
{U}
or
{D}
leg crossed to the initial state to give the scattering processes SC-1 and SC-2.
In taking the average,
since we sum over the initial state spins, a correspondence to the decay matrix element is maintained,
and for each
n
we can write the crossing relations

\displaystyle{\it\rm SC\!-\!1~crossing\!:}\ |{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}(p_{n}k_{i}\rightarrow q_{1}q_{2})|^{2}=-(1/2)|{\cal A}(p_{n}\rightarrow q_{1}q_{2}q_{U})|^{2}_{q_{2}\rightarrow(-k_{i});\,q_{U}\rightarrow q_{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle{\it\rm SC\!-\!2~crossing\!:}\ |{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}(p_{n}k_{i}\rightarrow q_{1}q_{2})|^{2}=-(1/2)|{\cal A}(p_{n}\rightarrow q_{1}q_{2}q_{U})|^{2}_{q_{U}\rightarrow(-k_{i})}\ ,

(23)

where
the 1/2 is due to now an average over the second initial-state particle spins as opposed to just a sum over the spins earlier,
the minus sign arises from the spinor sum changing from
u\bar{u}
to
v\bar{v}
for the spinor moved from the final state to the initial state,
and the change in sign of the momenta is because we are crossing an out-going final-state momentum to an incoming initial-state momentum.

Our intent
[
5
]
is to apply these results to scattering processes
happening in the early Universe at temperature
T
.
The initial state momenta sampled from the thermal spectrum are typically of order

{p_{n}}_{r},{k_{i}}_{r}\sim T
, and if
T>\Lambda
is relevant for baryon asymmetry generation, we will be sensitive to the UV physics.
To investigate this effect, we include a correction factor while computing our diagrams,
arising from the effective vertex being resolved by a propagating UV state
\xi
in the UV completion example,
as discussed in Appendix
D
.
We discuss next, in turn, the single operator and multiple operators scattering contributions to the baryon asymmetry,
with the effect from the UV physics correction factor also brought forth.
Multiple operator scattering contributions involving the SS interaction have been considered, for instance,
in Ref.
[
11
]
.

4.1
Scattering baryon asymmetry from single operator loop contributions

The LO cross section for each of SC-1 and SC-2 from Eq. (
22
) is

\sigma^{n}_{0}\,v=\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\int d\Pi_{2}\ |{\cal A}_{0}^{n(\sigma)}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\,\frac{1}{4\Lambda^{4}}\,\int d\Pi_{2}\left[-\frac{1}{4}(|{{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U\,\!}}|^{2})^{\mu\tau}_{nn}\,C_{00}\,(|M^{12}_{0}|^{2})_{\mu\tau}\right]_{(q_{i}\ {\rm cross})}\ ,

(24)

where we obtain the
|{\cal A}_{0}^{n(\sigma)}|^{2}
using the crossing relations of Eq. (
23
)
in the LO matrix element (cf. Eq. (
59
)),
with the (
q_{i}
cross) denoting the momentum substitutions shown in the crossing relations for each of SC-1 and SC-2,
and we include the extra (-1/2) as given there.
We write the LO cross sections equivalently in terms of the dimensionless
f_{00}^{n(\sigma)}
as

\sigma^{n}_{0}\,v=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{2}}{\Lambda^{4}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{2}f_{00}^{n(\sigma)}\right]\equiv\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{2}}{\Lambda^{4}}\right)\hat{\sigma}^{n}_{0}\,v\ ,

(25)

for each of SC-1 and SC-2.
We expect the
M_{n}^{2}
scaling on dimensional grounds.

We are interested in the scattering cross section difference

\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}
and asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
between the process and conjugate-process for each of SC-1 and SC-2,
the conjugate process amplitude being
{\cal A}^{c\,(\sigma)}={\cal A}_{0}^{c\,(\sigma)}+{\cal A}_{1}^{c\,(\sigma)}
.
Again, like in decay, the scattering baryon asymmetry
arises due to the cut in
{\cal A}_{1}^{(\sigma)}
(shown as the dashed curve in Fig.
4
)
giving a discontinuity
(1/2)\,{\rm Disc}({\cal A}_{1}^{(\sigma)})=i{\cal\hat{A}}_{1}^{(\sigma)}
.
For the same reason as in decay, our choice of masses ensures that a second cut through the
{\cal X}_{m}
is not possible.
We denote the interference between the tree
{\cal A}_{0}^{(\sigma)}
and the loop
{\cal\hat{A}}_{1}^{(\sigma)}

as
\hat{\cal A}_{01}^{(\sigma)}
,
and analogous to Eq. (
3
), but now integrating over 2-body phase-space, we have

\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v=\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\int d\Pi_{2}\ {\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}_{01}-\hat{\cal A}^{c\,(\sigma)}_{01})=\frac{1}{2E_{n}2E_{i}}\int d\Pi_{2}\ 2\,{\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}^{(\sigma)}_{01})\ .

(26)

We obtain the interference term
\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v
for scattering from the decay interference term Eq. (
13
)
by using the crossing relation of Eq. (
23
).

Analogous to the discontinuous piece in decay (cf. Eq. (
3
)),
we write the scattering cross section difference between the process and conjugate process
given by the discontinuity in the scattering tree-loop interference term in terms of the dimensionless
f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}

\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}\,v=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{6}}{\Lambda^{8}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{16}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{6}\,\sum_{m}2f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}\right]\ ,

(27)

where the factor of 2 is from equal magnitude but opposite sign contribution from the conjugate process,
and we expect the
M_{n}^{6}
scaling on dimensional grounds.

Analogous to the decay rate asymmetry of Eq. (
4
),
we define a cross section asymmetry which is given by

{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\equiv\frac{\sigma^{n}v-\sigma^{cn}v}{\sigma^{n}v+\sigma^{cn}v}=-\frac{2\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}v}{\sigma^{n}v+\bar{\sigma}^{n}v}\approx-\frac{\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}v}{\sigma_{0}^{n}v}\ ;\quad{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}=\sum_{n=1,2}{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\ ,

(28)

where
we have a factor of 2 in the numerator from including the
h.c.
of the interference term.
For leading order accuracy of the asymmetry we replace the sum in the denominator with the factor of 2 as shown,
and we write the total baryon asymmetry from scattering

{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
as the sum over the contributions
{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}
from
n=1,2
.
We obtain the baryon asymmetry from
{\cal X}_{n}
scattering to be

{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}}{\Lambda}\right)^{4}\left[-\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{4}\sum_{m}\frac{2f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}}{f_{00}^{n(\sigma)}}\right]\ .

(29)

In Fig.
12
we show as a function of the
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}\equiv{p_{n}}_{r}/M_{n}
, for SC-1 and SC-2 scattering processes,
in the left column the
{\sigma_{0}^{n}\,v}
in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
,
in the middle column (the absolute value of) the tree-loop interference scattering cross section difference between
the process and conjugate process
|\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v|
,
and,
in the right column the baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
summed over n=1,2.
These are all shown in the CM frame with
{p_{n}}_{r}={k_{i}}_{r}
and
c_{i}\equiv\cos{\theta_{i}}=-1
,
after integrating over the final-state phase-space angular variables,
for the benchmark point BP-A, and the couplings
g_{L}=1
,
g_{R}=0
.
The top row is with the effective VV interaction,
and we should bear in mind that
one should trust the effective theory results only up to
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}<\Lambda/M_{\chi}

and read the top-row plots only up to that for a specific choice of
M_{\chi}/\Lambda
.
The rise in the cross section we see in the effective theory here is turned around beyond

\hat{p}_{n_{r}}>\Lambda/M_{\chi}
by the UV completion, which we discuss in detail in Appendix
D
.
As discussed there, we include the UV state
\xi
propagator correction resolving the VV effective interaction, and
show its effect also in Fig.
12
,
with the middle row for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and bottom row for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/100
, both with
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
,
where
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=\Gamma_{\xi}/M_{\chi}
.
As mentioned, we see that for
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}>\Lambda/M_{\chi}
, the cross section indeed turns around due to the propagating
\xi
resolving the effective VV interaction.
We see that the LO SC-1 (t-channel)
{\sigma_{0}\,v}
does not fall off for large
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}
but rather flattens out,
although the growth in
{\sigma_{0}\,v}
is not present with the inclusion of the
\xi
UV propagator correction.
We trace this flattening-out to the fact that the
\xi
propagator correction becomes independent of
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}
for
\cos{\theta_{1}^{CM}}\!=\!-1

and therefore fails to fall off as
1/\hat{p}_{n_{r}}^{2}
at this phase-space point.

Figure 12:

For BP-A, in the CM frame,
with
g_{L}=1
,
g_{R}=0
,
the
{\sigma_{0}\,v}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
) (left)
for SC-1 n=1 (dotted), SC-1 n=2 (dashed), SC-2 n=1 (long-dotted), SC-2 n=2 (long-dashed),

|\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v|
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
) (middle)
and

{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
) (right)
summed over
n\!\!=\!\!\{1,2\}

for SC-1 (dashed), SC-2 (long-dashed) and the average over SC-1 and SC-2 (solid).
The topmost row is without UV propagator correction,
the middle row is with UV propagator correction with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
,
while the bottom row is with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/100
, both for
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
.

In the UV propagator correction, we have included only the real (PV) part of
f_{\xi}(p)
,
which goes to zero at the position of the pole as discussed in Appendix
D
and seen in Fig.
29
.
The imaginary
\xi
pole contribution gives the
\xi
on-shell production and decay,
which we omit in this effective theory analysis viewing it as a UV physics contribution.

Also, the departure from thermal equilibrium required by the Sakharov condition is aided by
the
{\cal X}
being almost decoupled
[
5
]
from the thermal plasma in the early Universe
due to very weak interactions with
{U},{D}
.
If so, the
{\cal X}
is produced mostly when other heavier UV states decay.
One such example is in the decay of the
\Phi_{B}
that is responsible for Majorana mass generation discussed in
Ref.
[
4
]
,
i.e.,
\Phi_{B}\rightarrow{\cal X}{\cal X}
at the scale around
\Lambda
.
If such a mechanism is operative,

{\cal X}
scattering is to be considered for
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}\lesssim M_{\Phi}/M_{\chi}
as
there is no appreciable population of
{\cal X}
at temperatures above
M_{\Phi}
.
If
M_{\Phi}\sim\Lambda
, we should then restrict
{\cal X}
scattering to
\hat{p}_{n_{r}}\lesssim\Lambda/M_{\chi}
.

As mentioned earlier, eventually, our interest is in the scattering taking place in the early Universe at temperature
T
.
We turn next to computing the thermally averaged cross section difference for use in this setting in Ref.
[
5
]
.

4.1.1
The thermally averaged cross section and baryon asymmetry

For the
2\rightarrow 2
processes SC-1 and SC-2, following the procedure laid-out in Appendix
B
,
we compute the thermally averaged cross section as

\langle\sigma v\rangle=\int\frac{d^{3}p_{n}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{d^{3}k_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,\frac{f^{(0)}(E_{n})}{n^{(0)}_{n}}\frac{f^{(0)}(E_{i})}{n^{(0)}_{i}}\,\sigma v\ .

(30)

We obtain the thermally averaged
\langle f_{00}^{n(\sigma)}\rangle

and compute the thermally averaged LO cross sections in analogy with Eq. (
25
),
for each of SC-1 and SC-2.

Next, we obtain
\langle f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}\rangle

for each of the SC-1 and SC-2,
and from these obtain the thermally averaged cross section difference between the process and conjugate process

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}\,v\rangle

given by the discontinuity in the scattering tree-loop interference term
analogous to Eq. (
27
) as

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}\,v\rangle=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{6}}{\Lambda^{8}}\right)\left[\frac{1}{16}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{6}\,\sum_{m}2\langle f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}\rangle\right]\ .

(31)

Also, similar to Eq. (
29
), we define a thermally averaged cross section asymmetry as

{\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\equiv\frac{\langle\sigma^{n}v\rangle-\langle\sigma^{cn}v\rangle}{\langle\sigma^{n}v\rangle+\langle\sigma^{cn}v\rangle}=-\frac{2\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}v\rangle}{\langle\sigma^{n}v\rangle+\langle\bar{\sigma}^{n}v\rangle}\approx-\frac{\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}v\rangle}{\langle\sigma_{0}^{n}v\rangle}\ ;\quad\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}=\sum_{n=1,2}{\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}\ .

(32)

We call attention to the fact that
{\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}
is not the thermal average of the earlier
{{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}
.
The
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
is the sum over the contributions
{\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}
from
n=1,2
.
We then obtain the thermally averaged baryon asymmetry from
{\cal X}_{n}
scattering to be

{\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{n(\sigma)}}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}}{\Lambda}\right)^{4}\left[-\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{M_{n}}{M_{\chi}}\right)^{4}\sum_{m}\frac{2\langle f_{01}^{nm(\sigma)}\rangle}{\langle f_{00}^{n(\sigma)}\rangle}\right]\ .

(33)

For the above values of the integrals for the benchmark point BP-A, for the choice
g_{L}=1,g_{R}=0,\tilde{g}=1
, for example, we find
for
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
the LO thermally averaged cross-section for (SC-1,SC-2)
for
n\!\!=\!\!1
is
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle=(0.26,8.8)\,M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
,
and for
n\!\!=\!\!2
is
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle=(0.36,12.4)\,M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
;
the difference in the interference term in the cross section
for
n\!\!=\!\!2
is

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle=(-0.67,-13)\times 10^{-5}\,M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
; and
the baryon asymmetry from scattering
for
n\!\!=\!\!2
is
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}=(1.9,1)\times 10^{-5}\,M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
. We thus can write for

\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}\!\!=\!\!1/2
, for
n\!\!=\!\!2
, for (SC-1,SC-2),

\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle=(0.36,12.4)\,(g^{2}/\Lambda^{2})\,(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{2}
,

\langle\Delta\sigma_{01}\,v\rangle=(-0.67,-13)\times 10^{-5}\,(g^{4}/\Lambda^{2})\,(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{6}
,
and

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}=(1.9,1)\times 10^{-5}\,g^{2}\,(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{4}
, including the couplings scaling.
Again, as in the decay case,
we find that the imaginary parts of these integrals are zero.

From the above
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
and
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle
numbers given, for BP-A,

x\!\!=\!\!1/2
, with the UV propagator correction factor present with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
,

g_{L}=1,g_{R}=0,\tilde{g}=1
,
we extract the thermal averages of the LO cross section and tree-loop interference functions parametrized
in Eqs. (31) and (32) respectively of Ref.
[
4
]
.
For
n\!\!=\!\!2
, we find these to be for (SC-1,SC-2):

\langle(1/(2\hat{E}_{n}\,2\hat{E}_{i}))\,\hat{f}_{00}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\approx(2,66)
,
and

\langle(1/(2\hat{E}_{n}\,2\hat{E}_{i}))\,\hat{f}_{01}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\approx(-0.03,-0.6)
.

In Fig.
13
we show, for BP-A,
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
the LO thermally averaged cross section
{\langle\hat{\sigma}_{0}\,v\rangle}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
)
averaged over the scattering processes SC-1 and SC-2,
the (negative of the) tree-loop interference scattering cross section difference

-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
)
averaged over SC-1 and SC-2 for
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
and,
the sum of the baryon asymmetry from scattering
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
) from SC-1 and SC-2,
for
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
.

Figure 13:

For BP-A,
the
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
)
averaged over SC-1 and SC-2 for
n\!\!=\!\!2
(left-panel),

-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
) averaged over SC-1 and SC-2 for
n\!\!=\!\!2
(middle-panel),
and,

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
)
from SC-1 and SC-2 for
n\!\!=\!\!2
(right-panel),
for
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
with UV propagator correction included with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
.

We choose this
x
since the
{\langle\sigma_{0}\,v\rangle}
is the largest close to this value, and focus on
n\!\!=\!\!2
only since the
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
is mostly generated for
n\!\!=\!\!2
.
In these plots we highlight the dependence of these quantities on the magnitude of
g_{L},g_{R}
and the phase of
g_{L}
(i.e.
\phi_{L}
).

In Fig.
14
we show for BP-A,
g_{R}=0
,
g_{L}=1
,
the
x
dependence in the range
x\in(0.01,20)

{\langle\hat{\sigma}_{0}\,v\rangle}
,
and for
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
the
-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle

and

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
generated in the SC-1 and SC-2 scattering processes.
The plots marked “UV” are with the UV propagator correction included with

M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
.

Figure 14:

For BP-A, for
g_{L}=1
,
g_{R}=0
,
the
x\!\!=\!\!M_{\chi}/T
dependence of

{\langle\sigma_{0}\,v\rangle}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
) (left column),
for SC-1
n\!\!=\!\!1
(dotted), SC-1 n=2 (dashed), SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!1
(long-dotted), SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
(long-dashed),
and,
for
n\!\!=\!\!2

SC-1 (long-dotted) and SC-2 (long-dashed)
the
-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
) (middle column)
and
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
) (right column),
with their averages over SC-1 and SC-2 (solid).
The top panel is without UV propagator correction,
while the bottom panel is with the UV propagator correction
for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}=1/10
.

To a very good approximation, most of the baryon asymmetry from scattering arises from
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
i.e. from
{\cal X}_{2}
scattering processes,
as we find the asymmetry generated from n=1 is
about two orders of magnitude smaller than for n=2.

We see that the
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
with the UV propagator correction included
is substantial only
in the range
x\in(0.1,10)

and there is no growth for small
x
(i.e. for
T>M_{\chi}
), unlike for the case without the UV correction.
Regarding the latter case, we should not trust an effective operator analysis for small
x
(i.e. large
T
)
as the UV state modifications would resolve the effective vertex above the scale
\Lambda
and soften the growth at small
x
.
This instructs us to restrict to the above domain while using our baryon asymmetry results.
In this restricted domain, the
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
we obtain with and without UV completion
are within an order of magnitude of each other,
being smaller in SC-1 in the effective theory, and bigger in SC-2.
The change is bigger in SC-2, which we expect since the UV state is resonant as it appears in the s-channel.
A truly accurate picture can be obtained only by computing the thermal averages in a specific UV completion
since the thermal spectrum samples UV physics contributions.

4.2
Scattering baryon asymmetry from multiple operator loop contribution

We compute here the scattering baryon asymmetry in SC-1 and SC-2 when more than one operator is involved,
obtained from crossing Fig.
8
.
We parallel the analysis in Sec.
4.1
and obtain results here using the crossing relations Eq. (
23
)
in the multiple operator decay contributions in Sec.
3.3
.
The leading contribution to the baryon asymmetry in scattering is in the interference term
between the tree and loop scattering amplitudes, similar to that shown diagrammatically in Fig.
9
,
but now crossed.

For the scattering kinematics, we now have
q_{3}+q_{4}=q_{1}-k_{i}
for SC-1, while
q_{3}+q_{4}=p_{n}+k_{i}
for SC-2.
We have argued (cf. Sec.
2.1
) that a nonzero baryon asymmetry
requires a nonzero strong phase coming from a cut in the loop process,
which in the multiple operator contribution of Fig.
8
requires
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
to
both
be put onshell.
This is possible only in SC-2 (s-channel kinematics), and not in SC-1 (t-channel kinematics).
We thus infer that a nonzero baryon asymmetry in scattering from the analog of Fig.
8
only arises
in SC-2, and we compute this contribution.
We recall that we only need the
nm=21
contribution for computing the baryon asymmetry.

For our numerical study, we choose the
benchmark point B
(BP-B) mass parameters (see p.
3.3
)
that has
M_{2}>M_{1}+2M_{U}
allowing the loop to be cut, giving a strong phase.
Again, for the same reasons explained in the decay case, only the
{\cal A}_{01}^{\prime(1)}
leads to a baryon asymmetry.
We write the
\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v
for the multiple operator scattering contribution for SC-2 in analogy with
Eq. (
26
), but now with the matrix element being
{\rm Im}(\hat{\cal A}^{\prime(\sigma)}_{01})
,
which is obtained from the decay contribution in Eq. (
19
) by using the crossing relation of Eq. (
23
).
We thermally average this and compute for SC-2
the thermally averaged cross section difference
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}^{n}\,v\rangle
between the process
and conjugate process analogous to Eq. (
31
),
and, the baryon asymmetry from scattering
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime(\sigma)}
analogous to Eq. (
32
).
We present the results next.

For illustration, we give here from the multiple operator contribution,
for BP-B, some sample numerical values for the thermally averaged
scattering cross section difference between the process and conjugate process and the baryon asymmetry.
For the choice
g_{L}\!\!=\!\!(3/4)\,e^{-\pi/2}
,
g_{R}\!=\!1/4
,
\tilde{g}\!\!=\!\!1
, for example, we find at
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
the LO thermally averaged cross-section
for (SC-1,SC-2)
n\!\!=\!\!1
is
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle\!=\!(0.16,1)M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
,
and for
n\!\!=\!\!2
is
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle\!=\!(0.23,1.45)M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
.
For SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
the difference in the interference term in the cross section

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle\!=\!-5.2\times 10^{-4}M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{6}
,
and the scattering baryon asymmetry
is
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime(\sigma)}\!=\!3.6\times 10^{-4}M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
.
We thus can write for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
, for SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
,

\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle\!\sim\!1.45\times 10^{-2}\,g^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
,

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle\!\sim\!-5.2\times 10^{-8}g^{4}/\Lambda^{2}

and

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime(\sigma)}\!\sim\!3.6\times 10^{-6}\,g^{2}
, including the couplings scaling.

From the above
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
and
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle
numbers
for BP-B,
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
g_{L}\!\!=\!\!(3/4)\,e^{-i\pi/2}
,
g_{R}\!\!=\!\!1/4
,
\tilde{g}\!\!=\!\!1
,
with the UV propagator correction factor present,
we extract the thermal averages of the LO cross section and tree-loop interference functions parametrized
in Eqs. (31) and (32) of Ref.
[
4
]
,
and obtain for SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
,

\langle(1/(2\hat{E}_{n}\,2\hat{E}_{i}))\,\hat{f}_{00}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\!\approx\!12.3
,
and

\langle(1/(2\hat{E}_{n}\,2\hat{E}_{i}))\,\hat{f}_{01}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\!\approx\!-0.1
,
for the multiple operator scattering contributions.

In Fig.
15
we show for BP-B, SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
the dimensionless LO thermally averaged cross section
{\langle\hat{\sigma}_{0}\,v\rangle}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
),
the (negative of the) tree-loop interference cross section difference

-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{6}
),
and the baryon asymmetry from scattering
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime(\sigma)}
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
).
The UV propagator corrections are included with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}\!\!=\!\!1/2
.

Figure 15:
For BP-B, SC-2
n\!\!=\!\!2
,
x\!\!=\!\!1/2
,
the

\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
) (left-panel),

-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
(in units of
M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{6}
) (middle-panel),
and
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime(\sigma)}
(in units of

) (right-panel)
from the multiple-operator contributions,
with the UV propagator corrections included with
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
and
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}\!\!=\!\!1/2
.

In Fig.
16
we show for BP-B
the same quantities now as a function of
x
,
for
g_{L}\!\!=\!\!(3/4)e^{-i\pi/2}
,
g_{R}\!\!=\!\!1/4
,
\tilde{g}\!\!=\!\!1

for different choices of
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda,\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi})
.

For BP-B,
the
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
(in units of

) (left),

(in units of

) (middle),
and

(in units of

) (right),
from the multiple operator SC-2

contributions
for

,

,

(M_{\chi}/\Lambda,\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi})
to be

(solid),

(dot-dashed),

(dotted),
and,

We see that for
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda,\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi})\!=\!(1/100,5)
(so that
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}\!=\!1/2
as before),
we find a similar shape as for the
(1/10,1/2)
case
(with a factor of
1/10
scaling of
x
),
and the size of
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
and
-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
are both higher by a factor of about 100,
leaving
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
to be about the same.
We can also see that for
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda,\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi})\!=\!(1/10,1/10)
,
we find that the behavior is largely
as for the
(1/10,1/2)
case
except for the
\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle
and
-\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}v\rangle
both being higher by a factor of about 5,
again leaving
\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}
to be about the same.
These comparisons are without including the powers of
M_{\chi}/\Lambda
scaling factors that have been pulled out as indicated above.

With the UV propagator correction included, we see from Fig.
16
that
the
\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle
is substantial only in the domain
x\in(0.1,10)
for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\!\!=\!\!1/10
.
This domain shifts to smaller
x
as
M_{\chi}/\Lambda
decreases.
These are in contrast to the case without the UV propagator correction (see the dashed curve), for which

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle
keeps increasing as
x
decreases.
However, this growth in the
x\ll 1
region is not to be trusted
since we see the UV propagator corrections modifies the behavior as the typical momenta become bigger than
\Lambda
,
bringing in sensitivity to the UV physics.
This is particularly true here for SC-2 as the UV correction is resonant since it is in the s-channel.
The scattering baryon asymmetry with and without the UV correction
are in good agreement for
x\gtrsim 1
,
but differ for
x\lesssim 1
where the latter keeps growing but the former turns around.

Thus, if the first operator in Eq. (
2
) is also present in the effective theory
in addition to the operator in Eq. (
1
),
and if the kinematics allows (i.e. if
M_{2}\!>\!M_{1}\!+\!2M_{U}
),
the multiple operator scattering contribution obtained from crossing Fig.
9
(left)
could be dominant as it is enhanced by a loop and
(\Lambda/M_{\chi})^{2}
factors
compared to the single operator contribution.
If any of these conditions is not met, the single operator contribution of Sec.
4.1

involving only the operator in Eq. (
1
)
would remain as the only contribution to the scattering asymmetry.

5
The
\Delta B=-2
process
{U}{D}{D}\rightarrow{U}^{c}{D}^{c}{D}^{c}

The thermally averaged
{U}(q_{U}^{\prime}){D}(q_{3}){D}(q_{4})\rightarrow{U}^{c}(q_{U}){D}^{c}(q_{1}){D}^{c}(q_{2})

scattering rate appears in the collision term (rhs) of the the BE.
It is sufficient to compute this rate at tree-level, i.e. at
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
,
as the the other collision terms, for instance the
\Delta\Gamma
term, are at this order.
Here, we compute this contribution.
The Feynman diagram for this process is as shown in Fig.
17
.

Figure 17:
The S- and T-channel-like Feynman diagrams contributing to the
{U}{D}{D}\rightarrow{U}^{c}{D}^{c}{D}^{c}
amplitude.

The left diagram has kinematics which is S-channel-like while the right diagram is T-channel-like,
and we call these contributions as
S
and
T
respectively.
(Another T-channel-like diagram similar to the right diagram but with one line from each vertex
is switched between the initial and final states is not shown.)
The scattering rate is given as
|S+T|^{2}=SS^{*}+TT^{*}+ST^{*}+TS^{*}
,
which we denote simply as
SS,~TT,~ST,~TS
respectively for notational brevity.

We define a quantity for the
3\rightarrow 3
process that has the units of a cross section (i.e.
{\rm GeV}^{-2}
) as

\sigma_{0}^{\prime}\equiv M_{\chi}^{3}\prod_{i=1}^{N_{i}}\left(\frac{1}{2E_{Q_{i}}}\right)\int\,\prod_{f=1}^{N_{f}}\left(\frac{d^{3}k_{Q_{f}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E_{Q_{f}}}\right)(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(\underset{\scriptstyle i}{\Sigma}p_{Q_{i}}-\underset{\scriptstyle f}{\Sigma}k_{Q_{f}})\ |{\cal M}^{\prime}_{0}|^{2}\ ,

(34)

where

p_{Q_{i}}
is an incoming
Q
momentum and
k_{Q_{i}}
an outgoing
Q
momentum,
and here we have
N_{i}\!=\!3
and
N_{f}\!=\!3
since it is a
3\!\rightarrow\!3
process.
We write
p_{n}=q^{\prime}_{U}+q_{3}+q_{4}=q_{U}+q_{1}+q_{2}
,
and,

{\tilde{M}_{n}}^{2}=E_{n}^{2}-{p_{n}}_{r}^{\!\!2}
is the virtual mass (squared) of the
{\cal X}_{n}
.

We compute the matrix element mod-squared averaged over initial state spins and summed over final spins,
fold in phase-space and compute
\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
as in Eq. (
34
).
We define the (dimensionless)
\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{\prime}=M_{\chi}^{2}\,\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
,
and from Eq. (
34
) we write this in terms of the dimensionless
f_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{B}B}^{nm}
as

\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{\prime}=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{8}}{\Lambda^{8}}\right)\frac{1}{16}\sum_{n,m}f_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{B}B}^{nm}\ .

(35)

We view the
\sigma_{0}^{\prime}
as an intermediate quantity,
and our final quantity of interest is really the thermally averaged scattering rate that we compute below.

In this study, we include only the
SS
contribution for
n\!\!=\!\!m
as it is resonant and we expect it to be the most significant.
We show in Fig.
18
, for the benchmark points BP-A and BP-B,
the
\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{\prime}
in units of
(gM_{\chi}^{2}/(2\Lambda^{2}))^{4}
,
as a function of
\tilde{M}_{n}\!=\!\sqrt{p_{n}^{2}}
, in the CM frame with
{\bf p}_{n}\!=\!0
,
for the sample value
{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}\!=\!0
, taking
\hat{\Gamma}_{\!{\cal X}_{n}}\!=\!10^{-6}
, and integrated over all the initial state angular variables.

Figure 18:
In the CM frame, the (dimensionless)
\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{\prime}({U}{D}{D}\rightarrow{U}^{c}{D}^{c}{D}^{c})
for
{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}=0
,
for benchmark point BP-A (left) and BP-B (right).

We see clearly the peaks due to the two poles at
\tilde{M}_{n}\!=\!M_{1,2}
that are regulated by
\hat{\Gamma}_{\!{\cal X}_{n}}
.

For use in the collision term of the BE set in the early Universe,
we thermally average over the initial state 4-momenta
q^{\prime}_{U},q_{3},q_{4}
and define the
thermal average scattering rate as

\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\equiv\frac{1}{n_{\chi}^{(0)}}\int\prod_{i=1}^{N_{i}}\left(\frac{d^{3}p_{Q_{i}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}f_{Q_{i}}^{(0)}\frac{1}{2E_{Q_{i}}}\right)\,\,\prod_{f=1}^{N_{f}}\left(\frac{d^{3}k_{Q_{f}}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E_{Q_{f}}}\right)(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{(4)}(\underset{\scriptstyle i}{\Sigma}p_{Q_{i}}-\underset{\scriptstyle f}{\Sigma}k_{Q_{f}})\ |{\cal M}^{\prime}_{0}|^{2}\ ,

(36)

where
we have introduced a factor of
n_{\chi}^{(0)}
in defining
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
since we have an intermediate
{\cal X}
exchanged (although offshell) in the process,
and motivated by the (equilibrium) collision term having the
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle n_{\chi}^{(0)}
combination.
In terms of the thermally averaged

\langle f_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{B}B}^{nm}\rangle

we obtain the thermally averaged dimensionless
UDD\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
scattering rate
\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\!=\!\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle/M_{\chi}
as

\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle=\left(\frac{M_{\chi}^{8}}{\Lambda^{8}}\right)\frac{1}{16}\sum_{n,m}\langle f_{\scriptscriptstyle\bar{B}B}^{nm}\rangle\ .

(37)

The very small
\hat{\Gamma}_{\!{\cal X}_{n}}
makes the resonant
SS
contribution very large for

p_{n}^{2}\!\rightarrow\!M_{n}^{2}
as seen in Fig. (
18
),
making the numerical evaluation unstable.
To get around this,
we obtain the integral due to the pole (narrow width approximation)
by replacing the
{\cal X}_{n}
propagator by a
\delta(\tilde{M}_{n}-M_{n})
for the
SS
contribution.
This allows us to analytically integrate over the
\tilde{M}_{n}
variable, which sets
\tilde{M}_{n}\!=\!M_{n}
.
The nonpole contribution we obtain by subtracting out the pole part from the integrand and numerically integrating
(cf. Appendix
G.6.1
).
We compute the thermal average integrals numerically and
show the resulting (dimensionless) thermally averaged scattering rate
\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
in Fig.
19
for BP-A and BP-B,
in units of
(M_{\chi}/\Lambda)^{8}
.
We show the pole, nonpole, and total contributions.

Figure 19:
The (dimensionless) thermally averaged scattering rate for
{U}{D}{D}\rightarrow{U}^{c}{D}^{c}{D}^{c}
, for BP-A (top panel) and BP-B (bottom panel),
showing the pole contribution (dashed), the nonpole (dotted), and the total rate (solid).

We also show the
\hat{n}_{\chi}^{(0)}\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
in the same units, as these enter the rhs of the Boltzmann equations.

We present some example values, setting
\hat{\Gamma}_{n}\!=\!10^{-6}
,
and computing the integrals numerically.
We find for
g_{L}\!=\!1/4
,
g_{R}\!=\!3/4
we have
\hat{\sigma}_{0}^{\prime}\!=\!0.02
.
For
g_{L}\!=\!1
,
g_{R}\!=\!0
,
x\!=\!1/4
, the (pole, nonpole) values for BP-A
is
\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\!=\!(0.0023,0.09)
;
while for BP-B we have for
g_{L}\!=\!1/4
,
g_{R}\!=\!3/4
,
x\!=\!1/4
,
we have
\langle\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\!=\!(0.05,0.03)
.
The total thermally averaged scattering rate is the sum of the pole and nonpole pieces.
We can thus write with the couplings scaling,
the thermally averaged rate to be
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\!\approx\!0.1\,g^{4}(M_{\chi}^{9}/\Lambda^{8})
, for
x\!=\!1/4
, for both BP-A and BP-B.

6
The Boltzmann Equations

Starting from a zero baryon number in the early Universe, we wish to compute the net baryon number generated at late time
and compare with the observed BAU.
We do this by tracking the net baryon number
n_{B}(t)
as a function of cosmic time
t
in the expanding Universe
using the Boltzmann equations (BEs) (see, for example, Refs.
[
2
,
12
,
13
]
,
and also the discussion in Refs.
[
4
,
5
]
).

CPT
and unitarity bring about relations between forward and inverse reaction matrix elements.
We discuss these relations in our new physics theory in Appendix
C
.
We must take care to handle correctly the contributions of real onshell intermediate states (RIS) in scattering channels and related inverse channels.
Doing so brings about a sign change in the
n_{\cal X}^{(0)}
term relative to the
n_{\cal X}
term as pointed out in Ref.
[
14
]
.
We discuss these intricate aspects brought about by
CPT
and unitarity considerations in Appendix
C
.

Before we give the full set of BEs that follow from these considerations,
we list next the relevant decay and scattering thermally averaged rates that appear in the BE.
We denote the thermally averaged decay rate for the process
{\cal X}\rightarrow UDD
as
\langle\Gamma\rangle
,
and that for the conjugate process
{\cal X}\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
as
\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle
.
For the scattering processes,
the relevant scattering cross sections are

\sigma_{{\cal X}\bar{Q}}\equiv\sigma({\cal X}Q^{c}\rightarrow QQ)
,

\sigma_{{\cal X}Q}\equiv\sigma({\cal X}Q\rightarrow Q^{c}Q^{c})
,
with their inverse scattering channel cross sections being

\sigma_{QQ}\equiv\sigma(QQ\rightarrow{\cal X}Q^{c})
,

\sigma_{\bar{Q}\bar{Q}}\equiv\sigma(Q^{c}Q^{c}\rightarrow{\cal X}Q)
respectively,
and the difference in the thermally averaged scattering rates is

\langle\Delta\Gamma^{(\sigma)}\rangle=\langle\Delta\sigma\,v\rangle n_{Q}^{(0)}
with
\Delta\sigma=\sigma_{{\cal X}\bar{Q}}-\sigma_{{\cal X}Q}
.
The thermally averaged LO scattering rate of the
\Delta B\!=\!2
process
UDD\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
is
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
,
and that of the LO
2\rightarrow 4
processes is
\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle
,
\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle
,
\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UU}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle

for the
UD\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
,
DD\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c}
,
UU\rightarrow D^{c}D^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
channels respectively.
We ignore the
4\rightarrow 2
scattering processes in our analysis as we expect their contributions to be small.
We write for the decay rates
\langle\Gamma\rangle=\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle+\langle\Delta\Gamma\rangle/2
and
\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle=\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle-\langle\Delta\Gamma\rangle/2
,
and similarly for the scattering rates

\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{Q}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle=\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle+\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle/2
and
\langle\Gamma_{\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle=\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle/2
.
We recall that the LO rates
\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle
,
\langle\Gamma_{0}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
,
\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}^{\!(\sigma)}\rangle
are the same for the process and conjugate process.

The BE for
n_{\cal X}
is

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}n_{\cal X}+3Hn_{\cal X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle\left[n_{\cal X}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+2\mu_{D})}{T}}\right]

(38)

\displaystyle\hskip-42.67912pt-2\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left[n_{\cal X}\cosh{\frac{\mu_{D}}{T}}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+\mu_{D})}{T}}\right]-2\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left[n_{\cal X}\cosh{\frac{\mu_{U}}{T}}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{2\mu_{D}}{T}}\right]

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-2\left(\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle+\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle+\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)(n_{\cal X}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)})\ ,

where the
\mu_{U}
,
\mu_{D}
are the chemical potentials of the
U
and
D
,
and for the last line we have taken
\cosh(...)\approx 1
since
\mu_{U,D}/T\ll 1
, given that the baryon asymmetry is tiny.
The BE for the
{U},{D}
are

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}n_{U}+3Hn_{U}\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\langle\Gamma\rangle\,n_{\cal X}-e_{U}e_{D}^{2}n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle+\left(\bar{e}_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-e_{U}\langle\Gamma_{\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)n_{\cal X}-\left(e_{U}e_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-\bar{e}_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)}

(39)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt-\left(e_{U}e_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle+e_{D}e_{U}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+e_{U}^{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UU}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)}+\left(\bar{e}_{U}\bar{e}_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle+\bar{e}_{D}\bar{e}_{U}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+\bar{e}_{D}^{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)},

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}n_{D}+3Hn_{D}\!\!\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\langle\Gamma\rangle\,n_{\cal X}-e_{U}e_{D}^{2}n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle-\left(e_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-\bar{e}_{U}\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-\bar{e}_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)n_{\cal X}

(40)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\left(\bar{e}_{U}\bar{e}_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-e_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma_{\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle-e_{U}e_{D}\langle\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)}-\left(e_{U}e_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle+e_{D}e_{U}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+e_{D}^{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\left(\bar{e}_{U}\bar{e}_{D}^{2}\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle+\bar{e}_{D}\bar{e}_{U}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+\bar{e}_{D}^{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+\bar{e}_{U}^{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UU}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\right)n_{\cal X}^{(0)},

where
e_{U}=e^{\mu_{U}/T},e_{D}=e^{\mu_{D}/T},\bar{e}_{U}=e^{-\mu_{U}/T},\bar{e}_{D}=e^{-\mu_{D}/T}
.
The inverse channel thermal averages are written in terms of
n_{\chi}^{(0)}

by using energy conservation in the statistical distribution functions
[
2
]
.
We obtain the BE for
n_{\bar{U}},n_{\bar{D}}
by taking
e_{Q}\leftrightarrow\bar{e}_{Q}
,
\langle\Gamma\rangle\leftrightarrow\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle
, and
\langle\Gamma_{\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\leftrightarrow\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{Q}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
, for
Q=\{{U},{D}\}
.
The thermally averaged scattering rate is

\langle\Gamma_{\chi Q}^{(\sigma)}\rangle=\langle\sigma_{{\cal X}Q}v\rangle\,n_{Q}^{(0)}
for
Q\!=\!\{U,D\}
,
and for its conjugate process is

\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{Q}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle=\langle\sigma_{{\cal X}\bar{Q}}v\rangle\,n_{Q}^{(0)}
.
We obtain the BE for
n_{\Delta{U}}\!=\!n_{U}\!-\!n_{\bar{U}}
and
n_{\Delta{D}}\!=\!n_{D}\!-\!n_{\bar{D}}
by taking the difference of the corresponding BEs.

To obtain the baryon number density
n_{B}
from the
n_{\Delta{U}},n_{\Delta{D}}

let us first start by considering the Dirac limit, i.e. with the Majorana masses set to zero.
In this limit, baryon number is a good symmetry and coincides with
\chi
number
since
B(\chi)\!=\!+1
and
B(\bar{\chi})\!=\!-1
for the Dirac pair
\chi,\bar{\chi}
,
and we recall that
B({U})\!=\!B({D})\!=\!1/3
.
We write

N_{\Delta{U}}\!\equiv\!(N_{{U}}-N_{\bar{U}})
,
and
N_{\Delta{D}}\!\equiv\!(N_{{D}}-N_{\bar{D}})
.
The efficient QED processes
Q\bar{Q}\leftrightarrow\gamma,\!\gamma\gamma
etc. keep
Q\!=\!\{{U},{D}\}
in thermal equilibrium,
implying a relation between the chemical potentials
[
15
]

\mu_{Q}+\mu_{\bar{Q}}=\mu_{\gamma},2\mu_{\gamma}
.
We have
\mu_{\gamma}\!=\!0
since it is an EM neutral real gauge boson,
which then implies
\mu_{\bar{Q}}\!=\!-\mu_{Q}
, consistent with EM charge conservation.
Using these relations, we can write the relevant terms in the thermodynamic potential
[
15
]
as

\Omega(T,V,\mu_{i})\supset\mu_{\chi}N_{\chi}+\mu_{\bar{\chi}}N_{\bar{\chi}}+\mu_{U}N_{\Delta U}+2\mu_{D}N_{\Delta D}
,
where we write
2N_{\Delta D}
for
N_{\Delta D_{a}}\!+\!N_{\Delta D_{b}}
,
with the
a,b
, for instance, being different color indices.
When the
VV
interaction is also in equilibrium, it ensures
\mu_{\chi}-(\mu_{U}+2\mu_{D})\!=\!0

and
\mu_{\bar{\chi}}\!=\!-\mu_{\chi}
.
(For such relations in other contexts, see for example, Refs.
[
16
,
17
]
.)
We note that changes in
N_{\Delta{\cal X},\Delta U,\Delta D}
are only due to the VV interaction which conserves

N_{\Delta\chi}+(N_{\Delta U}+2N_{\Delta D})/3
.
This conservation is enforced in the thermodynamics by including a chemical potential
\mu_{B}

and writing
\Omega\supset\mu_{B}(N_{\Delta{\cal X}}+N_{B})
,
where we have defined
N_{B}\!\equiv\!(N_{\Delta U}+2N_{\Delta D})/3
,
and setting
-(\partial\Omega/\partial\mu_{B})_{T,V}=0
.
Matching the forms of
\Omega
we have
\mu_{\chi}\!=\!\mu_{B}
,
\mu_{Q}\equiv\mu_{U}\!=\!\mu_{D}\!=\!\mu_{B}/3
.
Returning to the Majorana mass nonzero case,
\chi
number is not a good quantum number, and consequently
\mu_{\chi}\!=\!0
for the Majorana
{\cal X}
,
thus leaving
\Omega\supset\mu_{B}N_{B}
.

N_{B}
is nothing but baryon number,
and the baryon number density
n_{B}\!=\!N_{B}/V
is thus given as
n_{B}\!=\!(n_{\Delta{U}}+2n_{\Delta{D}})/3
.
If the VV interaction is in equilibrium,
\mu_{\chi}\!=\!0
implies
\mu_{B}\!=\!0
,
and the forward and inverse rates being equal keeps
n_{B}\!=\!0
if we start with that initial condition.
After the VV interaction decouples, there is no constraint on
N_{B}
,
and if the forward and inverse rates are different in the microscopic physics,
in addition to
C
and
CP
violation as required by the Sakharov conditions,

n_{B}
could build up to nonzero values.
In the earlier sections we have shown that in our theory there is indeed such a rate asymmetry
enabling the possibility that
n_{B}
could become nonzero.
We track the build up of
n_{B}
in the early Universe through its BE;
correctly taking into account the change in sign due to the RIS contributions mentioned above,
from Eqs. (
39
) and (
40
) we obtain the BE for
n_{B}
to be

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}n_{B}+3Hn_{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\Gamma\rangle\left(n_{\cal X}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+2\mu_{D})}{T}}\right)-2\left(\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle+2\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\right)\,n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\sinh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+2\mu_{D})}{T}}

(41)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\frac{5}{3}\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left(n_{\cal X}\cosh{\frac{\mu_{D}}{T}}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+\mu_{D})}{T}}\right)-2\!\times\!\frac{5}{3}\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left(n_{\cal X}\sinh{\frac{\mu_{D}}{T}}+n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\sinh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+\mu_{D})}{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left(n_{\cal X}\cosh{\frac{\mu_{U}}{T}}-n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\cosh{\frac{2\mu_{D}}{T}}\right)-2\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\left(n_{\cal X}\sinh{\frac{\mu_{U}}{T}}+n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\sinh{\frac{2\mu_{D}}{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt-4\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\,n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\,\sinh{\frac{(\mu_{U}+\mu_{D})}{T}}-4\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\,n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\,\sinh{\frac{2\mu_{D}}{T}}-2\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UU}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\,n_{\cal X}^{(0)}\,\sinh{\frac{2\mu_{U}}{T}}\ .

We can keep track of the nontrivial changes in number density over and above that due to the Hubble expansion
by changing variables from
n
to a comoving variable
Y\!=\!n/s
, where
s
is the entropy density
(see, for example, Refs.
[
2
]
, and also Ref.
[
5
]
for a brief summary).
In this way, for each of the
n_{\cal X},n_{\cal X}^{(0)},n_{B}
, we define a corresponding
Y_{\cal X},Y_{\cal X}^{(0)},Y_{B}
.
Also, since the observed BAU
\ll 1
, we can keep terms to first order in
Y_{B}
or equivalently in
\hat{\hat{\mu}}\!\equiv\!\mu/T
,
taking
\cosh{\hat{\hat{\mu}}}\!\approx\!1
,
\sinh{\hat{\hat{\mu}}}\!\approx\!\hat{\hat{\mu}}
for
\mu_{U},\mu_{D},\mu_{B}
.
If
n_{U}^{(0)}\!\approx\!n_{D}^{(0)}\!\approx\!n_{Q}^{(0)}
and
\mu_{U}\!\approx\!\mu_{D}\!\equiv\!\mu_{Q}
,
using
n=n^{(0)}e^{\mu/T}
and
\mu_{B}\!=\!3\mu_{Q}

we obtain
n_{B}\!=\!2n_{Q}^{(0)}\sinh{(\hat{\hat{\mu}}_{B}/3)}
, which implies
\hat{\hat{\mu}}_{B}\!\approx\!(3/2)Y_{B}/Y_{Q}^{(0)}
to first order in
\hat{\hat{\mu}}_{B}
,
where
[
5
]

Y_{Q}^{(0)}=45/(4\pi^{4})\,(g_{Q}/g_{*S})\,(M_{Q}/T)^{2}K_{2}(M_{Q}/T)
.
Using these relations in Eqs. (
38
) and (
41
) we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}Y_{\cal X}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle-2\left(\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle+\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle+\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)(Y_{\cal X}-Y_{\cal X}^{(0)})\ ,

(42)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}Y_{B}

\displaystyle\approx

\displaystyle\left(\langle\Delta\Gamma\rangle+\frac{5}{3}\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle+\langle\Delta\Gamma_{\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)\left(Y_{\cal X}-Y_{\cal X}^{(0)}\right)

(43)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-2\left(\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle+2\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\right)\,Y_{\cal X}^{(0)}\frac{3}{2}\frac{Y_{B}}{Y_{Q}^{(0)}}-2\!\times\!\left(\frac{5}{3}\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle+\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}Y_{\cal X}+Y_{\cal X}^{(0)}\right)\frac{Y_{B}}{Y_{Q}^{(0)}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt-4\left(\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UU}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle\right)\,Y_{\cal X}^{(0)}\,\frac{Y_{B}}{Y_{Q}^{(0)}}\ .

We see that the rhs of the BE (with the collision terms) involve the thermally averaged decay and scattering rates, which we
have spent effort in computing in this work.
Taking these rates as inputs, we solve the BE in Ref.
[
5
]
.

7
Conclusions

In this work, we compute the baryon asymmetry generated in the decay and scattering processes
involving the electromagnetically charge-neutral fermion
\chi
and quark-like fermions
{U},{D}
,
in the effective theory developed in Ref.
[
4
]
.
In the theory, the Dirac fermion
\chi
with Dirac mass
M_{\chi}
is split into a pair of Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}

due to the presence of a baryon number violating
\chi
Majorana mass.
The
\chi,{U},{D}
interaction is of the vector-vector (VV) form

(1/\Lambda^{2})\,(\bar{\chi}\Gamma^{\mu}{U})\,(\bar{{D}^{c}}\Gamma_{\mu}{D})
.
Being a dimension-6 operator, it is suppressed by the cutoff scale
\Lambda
as shown.

For two representative choices of parameters, benchmark points BP-A and BP-B,
we compute the unitary matrix that diagonalizes the
\chi
mass matrix and determine the
VV effective vertex for the mass eigenstates
{\cal X}_{n}
.
Both the benchmark points we choose are in the pseudo-Dirac limit in which the mass splitting between the
{\cal X}_{n}
masses,
namely,
M_{2}-M_{1}\ll M_{\chi}
.
We show the dependence of the
{\cal X}_{n}
mass eigenvalues
M_{n}
on the Majorana mass and phase.

We identify loop amplitudes for the
{\cal X}_{n}
decay and scattering processes
such that the interference term between them and the tree-level amplitude is different for the process
and its conjugate process, leading to a baryon asymmetry.
The phases in the Majorana mass, and in the VV interaction coupling act like weak phases
(opposite for process and conjugate process),
and the intermediate state
{D},{U}
in a loop going on-shell produces a phase of
\pi/2
that acts like a strong phase
(same for process and conjugate process).
These phases lead to
C
and
CP
violation, and along with the baryon number violation due to the Majorana mass,
produces a baryon asymmetry.

We refer to the loop processes that contain only the above VV effective operator as the single operator contributions.
We show that if the
(1/\Lambda^{2})\,(\bar{\cal X}\Gamma^{\mu}{U})\,(\bar{U}\Gamma_{\mu}{\cal X})
effective operator
is also present in the effective theory, and if the kinematics allows it,
additional loop contributions can be identified that contain both of these effective vertices,
that also produces a baryon asymmetry.
We refer to such contributions as multiple operator contributions.
In BP-A the baryon asymmetry is from the single operator contributions,
while in BP-B it is due to the multiple operator contributions.

We write the Feynman diagrams for decay and scattering process,
and in the mass basis, compute the matrix elements, integrate over the loop momenta,
fold in the phase space, and compute the resulting baryon asymmetry.
The integrals we encounter are multi-scale and multi-dimensional,
and we expend considerable effort to make them computationally tractable.
We present our baryon asymmetry results in a general manner without picking a specific scale of new physics, i.e.
\Lambda
or
M_{\chi}
,
but give scaling factors involving the
\Lambda
and
r_{M\Lambda}\!\!=\!\!M_{\chi}/\Lambda
that can be used to compute the actual numbers in any specific scenario
with its preference for the scale.

We compute numerically the decay rate asymmetry between
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{U}{D}{D}
and
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\bar{U}\bar{D}\bar{D}
,
for the benchmark points and for sample coupling choices.
For BP-A, we find the tree-level decay width

\Gamma_{{\cal X}}\sim 10^{-6}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}\,M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4}
,
and from the single operator contributions, we find
the decay rate difference
\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\sim-10^{-12}\,\tilde{g}^{4}g_{L,R}^{4}\,M_{\chi}^{9}/\Lambda^{8}
,
which yields the baryon asymmetry
{\cal A}_{B}\sim 10^{-6}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}\,M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
.
For BP-B, we find

\Gamma_{{\cal X}}\sim 10^{-5}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{2}\,M_{\chi}^{5}/\Lambda^{4}
,
and from the multiple operator contributions, we find

\Delta\hat{\Gamma}_{01}\sim-10^{-13}\,\tilde{g}^{2}g_{L,R}^{4}\,M_{\chi}^{7}/\Lambda^{6}
,
and
{\cal A}_{B}^{\prime}\sim 10^{-8}\,g_{L,R}^{2}\,M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
.
We show the dependence of the baryon asymmetry on the theory parameters in
Figs.
6
,
7
, and
11
.

We use crossing relations on the decay matrix elements computed above to obtain the scattering matrix elements.
We then compute the thermally averaged scattering cross section difference between
the process
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{Q}\rightarrow QQ
and its conjugate process
{\cal X}_{n}Q\rightarrow\bar{Q}\bar{Q}
(with
Q=\{{U},{D}\}
)
as a function of
x=M_{\chi}/T
, with
T
being the temperature of the bath in which the
{\cal X}_{n}
are interacting.
In the thermal spectrum, there is a probability for the
{\cal X}
to have
p_{\chi}>\Lambda
,
and therefore UV physics effects can be sampled.
We capture this effect by having a propagating UV state
\xi
(as in Ref.
[
4
]
)
resolve the VV effective interaction, and compute the baryon asymmetry after including this correction.
We find that the scattering cross section asymmetry
\langle\Delta\sigma_{01}v\rangle

is generated predominantly in the
0.01\lesssim x\lesssim 10
range (cf. Figs.
14
and
16
),
indicating that our effective theory computation still gives a fairly good estimate of the size of the asymmetry generated in
our framework.
This also indicates to us that for a truly accurate scattering cross section result,
one better work with a specific UV completion.

From our numerical computation,
for BP-A, for
x=1/2
,
from the single operator contribution,
we find
the thermally averaged LO cross section

\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle\sim 10\,g_{L,R}^{2}M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
,
and from the single operator contributions,
we find the thermally averaged cross section difference between the process and conjugate process

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle\sim-10^{-4}\,g_{L,R}^{4}M_{\chi}^{6}/\Lambda^{8}
,
which yields the cross section asymmetry

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}\sim 10^{-5}\,g_{L,R}^{2}M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{4}
.
For BP-B,
x=1/2
,
from the multiple operator contribution,
we find

\langle\sigma_{0}v\rangle\sim 1.5g_{L,R}^{2}M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{4}
,

\langle\Delta\hat{\sigma}_{01}\,v\rangle\sim-5\times 10^{-4}\,g_{L,R}^{4}M_{\chi}^{4}/\Lambda^{6}
,
and,

\hat{\cal A}_{B}^{(\sigma)}\sim 4\times 10^{-4}\,g_{L,R}^{2}M_{\chi}^{2}/\Lambda^{2}
.
We vary the effective theory parameters about the benchmark points and show their effects on the baryon asymmetry generated.
We compute the tree-level thermally averaged scattering rate for the
\Delta B=-2
three-to-three process

{U}{D}{D}\rightarrow{U}^{c}{D}^{c}{D}^{c}
.
For instance, for
x\!=\!1/4
, we find
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle\!\sim\!0.1\,g^{4}(M_{\chi}^{9}/\Lambda^{8})
for both BP-A and BP-B.

We use
CPT
and unitarity to relate the forward and inverse decay and scattering matrix elements.
Armed with these relations, we develop the Boltzmann equations for the
{\cal X}
and baryon number number densities
as a function of cosmic time.
The rates we compute in this work enter as collision term inputs on the rhs of the Boltzmann equations.
To determine the preferred mass scale
M_{\chi}
that leads to the observed BAU,
we numerically solve the Boltzmann equations
in Ref.
[
5
]
. Knowing the scale would help us determine the prospects of observing such new physics
at future colliders,
or in precision experiments such as the neutron-antineutron oscillation experiments
(see Refs.
[
4
,
5
]
and references therein).

Appendix A
Renormalization of the
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function

For the Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
, we can write the effective Lagrangian in momentum space as

{\cal L}_{2}\supset(1/2)\bar{\cal X}_{m}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.03125pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}p-M_{mn}){\cal X}_{n}
,
where we show only the bilinear part relevant to us here.
A hermitian
M
ensures that the eigenvalues are real and the system is nondissipative in the
\{{\cal X}_{1},{\cal X}_{2}\}
sector.
However, if intermediate particles can go onshell, i.e. the 2-point function can be cut, a dissipative piece arises, which we can encode by adding a non-hermitian piece

i(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.03125pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}p+M)^{-1}M\Gamma/2
to the
{\cal L}_{2}
above.
We thus have a part that is singular (in the limit of zero width)
when intermediate particles in the cut are onshell giving a discontinuity in the imaginary piece,
and a regular principal value (PV) part.
One renormalization and subtraction scheme we can adopt is an
onshell scheme
in which we pick counterterms
for the
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function to cancel the loop corrections so that the tree-level masses are the renormalized masses.
We take the counterterm Lagrangian as

{\cal L}_{2}^{\rm CT}\supset(1/2)\bar{\cal X}_{m}\,\delta\Sigma_{nm}\,{{\cal X}_{n}}
, with
\delta\Sigma
hermitian.
The nonhermitian piece due to the cut cannot be cancelled by the hermitian counterterm, and so survives without any subtraction.
For
n\!\!=\!\!m
, the non-hermitian piece implies
{\cal X}_{n}
decay.
Related aspects in the onshell scheme are discussed, albeit in different contexts, for example, in Refs.
[
18
,
19
,
20
]
.

Due to the Majorana nature of the
{\cal X}_{n}
, the full 2-point function includes two different loop contributions, namely,
one from fields propagating in the loop, and another with their conjugate fields,
which we show diagrammatically in Fig.
20
.
The full 2-point function is the sum of these, i.e.
\Sigma_{nm}(p)=\Omega_{nm}(p)+\Omega^{c}_{nm}(p)
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\raisebox{0.0pt}{\includegraphics[width]{Omg.png}}\hskip 28.45274pt\raisebox{0.0pt}{\includegraphics[width]{Omgc.png}}\end{array}\hskip-7.11317pt

\hskip 14.22636pt\Omega_{nm}(p_{n})\hskip 106.69783pt\Omega^{c}_{nm}(p_{n})

Figure 20:

The two different loop contributions to the
\bar{\cal X}_{m}{\cal X}_{n}
2-point function
\Sigma_{nm}(p_{n})
.

We compute the baryon asymmetry in Sec.
3.2
with a cut in the
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function
in the B
1
diagram in Fig.
4
,
or equivalently the B diagram contribution in Fig.
5
with
p^{\prime}_{n}=p_{n}
.
The cut is over the intermediate particles in
\Omega^{c}_{nm}
for the process and
\Omega_{nm}
for the conjugate process.
As mentioned, there is no counterterm for the discontinuity,
and we take the
\im(\Omega_{nm})
and
\im(\Omega^{c}_{nm})
without any subtraction.
The
\im(\Omega_{nn}),\im(\Omega^{c}_{nn})
(for
n\!\!=\!\!m
) with both legs on-shell yields the LO decay width
\Gamma_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{c}
respectively.
The
\im(\Omega_{nm}),\im(\Omega^{c}_{nm})
(for
n\!\!\neq\!\!m
) has the
{\cal X}_{m}
offshell at
p_{n}^{2}=M_{n}^{2}

and is thus an intermediate state in some process, for instance in diagram B in our case.
Although it does not contribute to the asymmetry,
for completeness, we have computed the renormalization conditions and the subtraction for this
(cf. Appendix
E.3.3
).

As an aside, to glean further insight,
we contrast our two Majorana fermions
{\cal X}_{n}
case with the two Dirac fermions
\Psi_{n}
case,
with each containing two (different) Weyl fields
\psi_{n\alpha}
and
\psi^{c}_{n\alpha}
.
The 2-point functions
\bar{\Psi}_{n}\Psi_{m}
now have bilinears of the form
\psi^{c}_{n}\psi_{m}+h.c.

In the Dirac case also, an identical argument can be used to cancel the real parts of
\Sigma_{11},\Sigma_{22}
at
the on-shell subtraction scales
p_{1_{0}},p_{2_{0}}
and
also
\Sigma_{12}
at
p_{1_{0}}
.
Now, the
\psi^{c}_{1}\psi_{2}
and
\psi^{c}_{2}\psi_{1}
bilinears are independent, allowing an independent counterterm to cancel the
real parts of
\Sigma_{21}
at
p_{2_{0}}
also.
Thus, in the Dirac case,
all the
{\rm PV}((\Sigma_{mn}))
can be canceled at the respective
p_{n_{0}}
.
In contrast, in the Majorana case the
\Sigma_{12}
and
\Sigma_{21}
are not independent
and therefore cannot both be canceled.

Appendix B
Thermal Averaging Procedure

Our interest ultimately is in the baryon asymmetry generated due to
{\cal X}_{n}

decay and scattering taking place in a thermal bath of the hot early Universe at temperature
T
.
For a species in thermal equilibrium, the thermal average
\langle\Theta\rangle
of any quantity
\Theta(\{p_{i}\},\{q_{j}\})

with initial state momenta
\{p_{i}\}
and final state momenta
\{q_{j}\}
is given by

\langle\Theta\rangle(\{q_{j}\})=\int\Pi_{i}\left(\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}f(E_{i})\right)\,\Theta(\{p_{i}\},~\{q_{j}\})\ ,

(44)

with the equilibrium Boltzmann distribution function
f(E)=e^{(\mu-E)/T}\equiv e^{\mu/T}f^{(0)}(E)
where

\mu
is the chemical potential,
f^{(0)}(E)=e^{-E/T}
, and,
E=\sqrt{{\bf p}^{2}+M^{2}}
.
For a species with
g
degrees of freedom, we have
the number density
n=g\int d^{3}p/(2\pi)^{3}f(E)
,
and we can write
n=n^{(0)}e^{\mu/T}
with
n^{(0)}
being the number density with
\mu=0
,
being given by
n^{(0)}=g\int d^{3}p/(2\pi)^{3}f^{(0)}(E)
,
which we find to be

n^{(0)}=\frac{gM^{2}T}{2\pi^{2}}\,K_{2}\left(\frac{M}{T}\right)\ ,

(45)

where
K_{2}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind.
We note the expansions

K_{2}(x)=2/x^{2}+{\cal O}(1)
for
x\ll 1
, and
K_{2}(x)=\sqrt{\pi/(2x)}e^{-x}+{\cal O}(1/x)
for
x\gg 1
,
which for
x\!\!=\!\!M/T
give the relativistic and non-relativistic limits respectively as usual (see for example Ref.
[
21
]
).

We focus on thermal averages in scattering processes here since it is more involved given the two initial state particles.
We take the initial state kinematical variables in the lab-frame, which in our early Universe setting can be identified with the standard cosmological frame.
For the spin averaged scattering matrix element we expect a trivial dependence on
(c_{n},\phi_{n},\phi_{i})
,
and perform those integrations, leaving
d^{3}p_{n}d^{3}k_{i}=8\pi^{2}d{p_{n}}_{r}{p_{n}}_{r}^{\!\!2}dc_{i}d{k_{i}}_{r}{k_{i}}_{r}^{2}

over their usual domains
{p_{n}}_{r},{k_{i}}_{r}\in(0,\infty);\ c_{i}\in(-1,1)
.

We define
x\equiv M_{\chi}/T
, and the scaled variables
\hat{M}=M/M_{\chi}
,
\hat{p}_{r}=p_{r}/M_{\chi}
,
\hat{E}=E/M_{\chi}
.
We define a distribution function normalized as a probability distribution function, namely,

f_{P}^{(0)}(\hat{p}_{r})=\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\frac{(4\pi)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\hat{p}_{r}^{2}f^{(0)}(\hat{p}_{r})=\frac{2\pi^{2}\hat{p}_{r}^{2}}{\hat{M}^{2}K_{2}(\hat{M}x)}\,xe^{-\hat{E}x}\ ,

(46)

normalized as
\int_{0}^{\infty}d\hat{p}_{r}f^{(0)}_{P}(\hat{p}_{r})=1
.
We can equivalently write the energy probability distribution function

f_{P}^{(0)}(\hat{E})=\frac{1}{n^{(0)}}\frac{(4\pi)}{(2\pi)^{3}}\hat{E}\sqrt{\hat{E}^{2}-\hat{M}^{2}}f^{(0)}(\hat{E})=\frac{2\pi^{2}\hat{E}\sqrt{\hat{E}^{2}-\hat{M}^{2}}}{\hat{M}^{2}K_{2}(\hat{M}x)}\,xe^{-\hat{E}x}\ ,

(47)

which is normalized as
\int_{\hat{M}}^{\infty}d\hat{E}f^{(0)}_{P}(\hat{E})=1
.
Using the
f^{(0)}(E)/{n^{(0)}}
that is normalized to have unit integral, we define another thermal average

\langle\Theta\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}(\{q_{j}\})=\int\Pi_{i}\left(\frac{d^{3}p_{i}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\frac{f(E_{i})}{n^{(0)}_{i}}\right)\,\Theta(\{p_{i}\},~\{q_{j}\})\ ,

(48)
