to be with the distribution function normalized as a probability distribution function.

The rest-frame decay width (see Eq. (
8
)) can be written as

\Gamma_{0}=1/(2M)\,\int d\Pi_{3}|{\cal A}|^{2}
.
Since the decay matrix element
{\cal A}
, and the final-state phase-space element
d\Pi_{3}

are individually Lorentz invariant,
if
{\cal X}
has momentum
p=(E,{\bf p})
,
the generalization of the above rest-frame decay-width to a boosted frame is to replace the first factor as
1/(2M)\rightarrow 1/(2E)
.
In other words we have
\Gamma(E)/\Gamma_{0}=M/E
,
which clearly has the desirable physical property that the particle life-time
\tau=1/\Gamma
has a time-dilation factor of
E/M
in the boosted frame.
In a thermal bath, the decaying
{\cal X}
has a thermal energy distribution

f^{(0)}_{P}(\hat{E})
given in Eq. (
47
).
with
\hat{E}=E/M_{\chi}
, and
\hat{M}=M/M_{\chi}
.
We can find the thermal average decay width as

\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}=\int_{\hat{M}}^{\infty}d\hat{E}\,f^{(0)}_{P}(\hat{E})\,\Gamma(\hat{E})\ ,

(49)

i.e. we have
\langle\Gamma\rangle/\Gamma_{0}=\langle\hat{M}/\hat{E}\rangle
,
and
the
{\scriptscriptstyle P}
subscript in
\langle...\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}
reminding us of the
1/n_{\chi}^{(0)}
in the distribution function, normalizing it as a probability distribution.
Evaluating this, we obtain

\frac{\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}}{\Gamma_{0}}=\frac{K_{1}(\hat{M}x)}{K_{2}(\hat{M}x)}\ ,

(50)

where the
K_{n}
are the modified Bessel functions of the second kind.
We note the limits,

\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}/\Gamma_{0}=\hat{M}x/2+{\cal O}(x^{3})
for
x\ll 1
,
while

\langle\Gamma\rangle_{\scriptscriptstyle\!P}/\Gamma_{0}=1-3/(2\hat{M}x)+{\cal O}(1/x^{2})
for
x\gg 1
.
We give some numerical details on the behavior of the thermal distribution functions and thermal averages
(cf. Appendix
I
).

Appendix C
CPT
and Unitarity Relations

We start with the relations between matrix elements that follow from unitarity and the assumption of
CPT
invariance,
following Ref.
[
14
]
.

In the usual notation, we write the S-matrix as
{\cal S}\!=\!1+i{\cal T}
,
the nontrivial part of the S-matrix elements for any process
i\!\rightarrow\!j
in terms of the T-matrix elements
{\cal T}(i\rightarrow j)\!=\!{\cal T}_{ji}=\left<j|{\cal T}|i\right>
,
with
\{\left.|i\right>\}
a complete set of states,
and the amplitude
{\cal M}
for this process defined via
{\cal T}_{ji}\!=\!(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p_{i}-p_{j}){\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)
.
Unitarity, namely
{\cal S}^{\dagger}{\cal S}\!=\!{\cal S}{\cal S}^{\dagger}\!=\!1
, implies

2\im({\cal T}_{ii})\!=\!\sum_{j}{\cal T}_{ji}^{*}{\cal T}_{ji}
,
or equivalently

2\im({\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!i))\!=\!\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}
.
Supplying kinematical factors, we obtain the optical theorem,

\im({\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!i))\!=\!2E_{cm}|{\bf p}|\sigma_{\rm total}
.
Furthermore, the above unitarity condition also implies

{\cal T}^{\dagger}{\cal T}={\cal T}{\cal T}^{\dagger}
which leads to

\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}\!=\!\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(j\!\rightarrow\!i)|^{2}
.

CPT
implies
{\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)={\cal M}(\bar{j}\!\rightarrow\!\bar{i})
where
\bar{i}
represents the CP conjugate state of the state
i
.
Combining
CPT
and the above unitarity relation, we obtain

\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}\!=\!\sum_{\bar{j}}|{\cal M}(\bar{i}\!\rightarrow\!\bar{j})|^{2}
.
Since the sum over the complete set of states
j
already includes also over
\bar{j}
,
there is no distinction between summing over
j
and
\bar{j}
,
and so we can write this as

\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(i\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}\!=\!\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(\bar{i}\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}\ .

(51)

If the sum above includes a sum over the initial and final state spins as appropriate for unpolarized observables,
the distinction between
CP
transformed state
\bar{j}
and the
C
transformed state
j^{c}
is unimportant.
For this reason, we use
\bar{Q}
and
Q^{c}
interchangeably as convenient.
Also, in our case,
for the Majorana
{\cal X}_{n}
we have
{\cal X}_{n}^{c}={\cal X}_{n}
, and so we prefer to write in the notation of
j^{c}
rather than
\bar{j}
.

Consider for instance the 1-particle
{\cal X}_{n}\!=\!\{{\cal X}_{1},{\cal X}_{2}\}
sector.
Given that
{\cal X}_{n}
can decay to the 3-particle final states
B\!\equiv\!UD\bar{D}^{c}\!\sim\!UDD
and to
\bar{B}\!\equiv\!\bar{U}\bar{D}D^{c}\!\sim\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!=\!B^{c}
,
the complete set of states
{\cal X}_{n},B,B^{c},...
including the 3-particle states and other n-particle states not shown.
Only including all n-body final states preserves unitarity.
However, to leading order in perturbation theory, we can restrict to the 1- and 3-particle sectors
i=\{{\cal X}_{n},B,B^{c}\}
as we do below.
From the perspective of the 1-particle
{\cal X}_{n}
sector, the
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow B,B^{c}
decays appear as a dissipative effect,
and in perturbation theory, the leading contribution to this dissipation is due to
the discontinuity in the imaginary part of the
{\cal M}({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{\cal X}_{n})
forward amplitude
due to cuts from onshell
B,B^{c}
intermediate states.
We have used this aspect to compute the
{\cal X}_{n}
decay width.

We next obtain a relation between the inverse decay and (forward) decay matrix elements relevant for the rhs of the BE.

CPT
and unitarity as encoded in Eq. (
51
) with
i=UDD
implies

\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}\!=\!\sum_{j}|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!j)|^{2}
.
Explicitly putting in the
j
we obtain

\displaystyle|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}=

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}\ ,

(52)

where by
{\cal X}_{n}
we mean a sum over both
{\cal X}_{1,2}
,
and we restrict to the 1- and 3-body final states as appropriate for
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
in the VV interaction effects we are after, and omit higher n-particle cuts.
Here, we have explicitly separated out the contribution due to a real (onshell) intermediate state (RIS)
[
14
]

{\cal X}_{n}

in
|{\cal M}_{RIS}(UDD\!\!\rightarrow\!\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}\!\equiv\!|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}
,
and in
{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\!\rightarrow\!\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})
a pure scattering piece that should not include the RIS piece with an onshell
{\cal X}_{n}
,
i.e. we have
|{\cal M}|^{2}=|{\cal M}_{RIS}|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}|^{2}
.
Using the
CPT
relation
|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}

we obtain

\displaystyle\left(|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}-|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}\right)=

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}-|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}\right)\ .

(53)

The rhs above is nothing but the difference in the inverse decay matrix elements.
This shows how inverse decay and pure scattering matrix elements are related.
We use this relationship below.
This can also be written as

\displaystyle\left(|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}-|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}\right)=

(54)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt-\left(|{\cal M}_{RIS}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}-|{\cal M}_{RIS}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}\right)\ ,

\displaystyle{\rm or,}

\displaystyle|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}=|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}\ .

We are now in a position to see what these relations imply for the rhs of the BE
focusing on the decay and inverse decay terms first (cf. Ref.
[
4
]
),
which are given as

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}n_{B}+3Hn_{B}\!\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\!\int[d\Pi]\left[\left(|{\cal M}({\cal X}_{n}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}-|{\cal M}({\cal X}_{n}\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}\right)f_{\chi}\right.

(55)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!-\left(|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}e^{3\mu_{Q}/T}-|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}e^{-3\mu_{Q}/T}\right)f_{\chi}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\left.-2\left(|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}e^{3\mu_{Q}/T}-|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}e^{-3\mu_{Q}/T}\right)(f_{Q}^{(0)})^{3}\right]\ ,

where the
f_{\chi}
is the
{\cal X}
distribution function,
f_{\chi}^{(0)}
is the equilibrium
{\cal X}
distribution,

f_{Q}=e^{\mu_{Q}/T}f_{Q}^{(0)}
and using energy conservation we have substituted
(f_{Q}^{(0)})^{3}=f_{\chi}^{(0)}
in the second line above in which the
{\cal X}
is onshell,
the factor of 2 in the last line is due to the processes being
\Delta B=\pm 2
,
in the last line we cannot use the energy conservation trick as we did in the second line as the
{\cal X}_{n}
is not onshell,
and,
the integral measure
[d\Pi]
is shown symbolically with the actual form suppressed.
The integrals with the distribution functions lead to terms that are proportional to
n_{\chi},n_{\chi}^{(0)}
.

CPT
relates the inverse decay matrix element to the decay matrix element as

|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}\!=\!|{\cal M}({\cal X}_{n}\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}
,
and,

|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}\!=\!|{\cal M}({\cal X}_{n}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}
.
Using these relations, we notice that in the limit of
\mu_{Q}\rightarrow 0
,
the decay and inverse decay terms (first two lines on the rhs) assemble into a form that goes like
(n_{\chi}+n_{\chi}^{(0)})

and does not go to zero as
n_{\chi}\rightarrow n_{\chi}^{(0)}
.
This signals that something is amiss; what comes to the rescue is that the pure scattering contribution (third line),
which we see from Eq. (
53
) has the RIS piece contributing to inverse decay with a negative sign,
and along with the above factor of 2, together ends up overturning the sign of the
n_{\chi}^{(0)}
piece, giving in the end a
(n_{\chi}-n_{\chi}^{(0)})
behavior as expected.

For working out the result for nonzero
\mu_{Q}
,
we write

|{\cal M}^{\prime}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}_{avg}|^{2}+(1/2)\Delta|{\cal M}^{\prime}|^{2}
and

|{\cal M}^{\prime}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDD)|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}_{avg}|^{2}-(1/2)\Delta|{\cal M}^{\prime}|^{2}
,
and,

|{\cal M}(UDD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{RIS}_{avg}|^{2}+(1/2)\Delta|{\cal M}^{RIS}|^{2}
and

|{\cal M}(U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n})|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{RIS}_{avg}|^{2}-(1/2)\Delta|{\cal M}^{RIS}|^{2}
.
In terms of these, Eq. (
53
) is
\Delta|{\cal M}^{\prime}|^{2}=-\Delta|{\cal M}^{RIS}_{avg}|^{2}
.
The rhs of Eq. (
55
) can then be written as

-4\sinh{(3\mu_{Q}/T)}\int(f_{Q}^{(0)})^{3}\,|{\cal M}^{\prime}_{avg}|^{2}+2\cosh{(3\mu_{Q}/T)}\int f_{\chi}^{(0)}\,\Delta|{\cal M}^{RIS}_{avg}|^{2}\ .

Since we are interested in a tiny baryon asymmetry, i.e.
\mu_{Q}/T\ll 1
, it suffices to work to leading order in
\mu_{Q}
,
and we can take in the above rhs, the LO scattering matrix element, i.e.
|{\cal M}^{\prime}_{avg}|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}_{0}|^{2}
.
We can write the first term as
-4\sinh{(3\mu_{Q}/T)}\,n_{\chi}^{(0)}\langle\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\rangle

in terms of the thermally averaged transition rate
\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
defined in Eq. (
36
) and computed earlier. The
CPT
relation between the inverse decay and decay matrix elements given above is

\Delta|{\cal M}^{RIS}|^{2}=-\Delta|{\cal M}^{\prime}|^{2}
.
Using these results,
the contributions from decay, inverse decay, and
3\rightarrow 3
scattering,
finally leads to the first two terms on the rhs of the Boltzmann equation in Eq. (
41
).

Using the
CPT
and unitarity relations between the matrix elements above,
we write down the Boltzmann Equations (BE) for the
{\cal X}
number density
n_{\cal X}
,
and the number densities of
{\cal X},{U},{D}
, namely,
n_{\cal X},n_{U},n_{D}
, and also for
\bar{U},\bar{D}
namely
n_{\bar{U}},n_{\bar{D}}
.
From these we obtain the BE for the baryon number density
n_{B}
,
continuing our earlier discussion on the BE in Ref.
[
4
]
.

The rhs of the BE (collision terms) contain various thermally averaged decay and scattering rates.
In our theory, the relevant rates are

\langle\Gamma\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\rightarrow UDD
decay process,

\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\bar{D}\rightarrow UD
scattering process SC-1,

\langle\Gamma_{\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\bar{U}\rightarrow DD
scattering process SC-2.
Their conjugate process rates are

\langle\bar{\Gamma}\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\rightarrow UDD
decay process,

\langle\Gamma_{\chi D}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\bar{D}\rightarrow UD
scattering process SC-1,

\langle\Gamma_{\chi U}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
for the
{\cal X}\bar{U}\rightarrow DD
scattering process SC-2.
The corresponding rate at LO are
\langle\Gamma_{0}\rangle
,
\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi\bar{D}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
,
\langle\Gamma_{0\,\chi\bar{U}}^{(\sigma)}\rangle
.
The
\Delta B=\pm 2
rates at LO are

\langle\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}\rangle
for the
UDD\rightarrow U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
process,

\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,UD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle
for the
UD\rightarrow D^{c}U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
process,
and,

\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0\,DD}}^{\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma)}\ \rangle
for the
DD\rightarrow U^{c}U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}
process.
There is no asymmetry at LO and so the same rates apply for the conjugate processes at LO.
The thermally averaged rates for the inverse processes of these are also required.

A similar analysis is applicable for the scattering contribution on the rhs of the BE.
Consider first the SC-2 channel.
The
CPT
and unitarity relation in Eq. (
51
) with
i=DD
,
and
j
restricted to 2 and 4 particle states as appropriate for
\mathcal{O}(g^{4})
effect we are interested in,
implies,

\displaystyle|{\cal M}(DD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n}U^{c})|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2}=

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt|{\cal M}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n}U)|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDDU)|^{2}\ ,

(56)

after we cancel common terms on both sides by using the relations

|{\cal M}^{\prime}(DD\!\rightarrow\!DD)|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!D^{c}D^{c})|^{2}
from CPT,
and,

|{\cal M}^{\prime}(DD\!\rightarrow\!UDDU^{c})|^{2}=|{\cal M}^{\prime}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U)|^{2}
from
CPT
and crossing symmetry.
We have
|{\cal M}(DD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n}U^{c})|^{2}=|{\cal M}_{RIS}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2}
,
where the RIS piece is when the
{\cal X}_{n}
goes onshell in the scattering channel,
and similarly for its conjugate.
We can equivalently write Eq. (
56
) as

|{\cal M}_{RIS}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2}=|{\cal M}_{RIS}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDDU)|^{2}+|{\cal M}^{\prime}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDDU)|^{2}
,
or,

|{\cal M}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2}\!\!=\!\!|{\cal M}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDDU)|^{2}

combining the RIS piece and the pure scattering piece.
Using the
CPT
relations
{\cal M}(DD\!\!\rightarrow\!\!{\cal X}U^{c})\!\!=\!\!{\cal M}({\cal X}U\!\!\rightarrow\!\!D^{c}D^{c})
and

{\cal M}(DD\!\!\rightarrow\!\!{\cal X}U^{c})\!\!=\!\!{\cal M}({\cal X}U\!\!\rightarrow\!\!D^{c}D^{c})
,
and defining

\Delta|{\cal M}^{(\sigma)}|^{2}\!\!=\!\!(|{\cal M}({\cal X}U^{c}\!\!\rightarrow\!\!DD)|^{2}-|{\cal M}({\cal X}U\!\!\rightarrow\!\!D^{c}D^{c})|^{2})

and

\Delta|{\cal M}^{{}^{\prime}(\sigma)}|^{2}\!\!=\!\!(|{\cal M}^{\prime}(D^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!UDDU)|^{2}-|{\cal M}^{\prime}(DD\!\rightarrow\!U^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c})|^{2})

we obtain

\Delta|{\cal M}^{{}^{\prime}(\sigma)}|^{2}\!\!=\!\!-\Delta|{\cal M}^{(\sigma)}|^{2}
.
This relates the pure scattering piece on the lhs to the RIS piece on the rhs,
and the negative sign in conjunction with a factor of 2 from being a
\Delta B=\pm 2
scattering process,
overturns the sign of the inverse scattering piece on the rhs of the BE.
Similarly, for SC-1 the relevant scattering channel is

U^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!DDDU
and
UD\!\rightarrow\!D^{c}D^{c}D^{c}U^{c}
and the RIS piece of these give

U^{c}D^{c}\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n}D
and
UD\!\rightarrow\!{\cal X}_{n}D^{c}
respectively.
Taking into account a nonzero
\mu_{Q}
, we obtain on the rhs of the BE the form

{\cal M}^{\prime}(QQ\!\!\rightarrow...)\,e^{2\mu_{Q}/T}
and
{\cal M}^{\prime}(Q^{c}Q^{c}\!\!\rightarrow...)\,e^{-2\mu_{Q}/T}
,
and proceeding along similar lines to the earlier inverse decay case,
we define the thermally averaged scattering rate
\langle{\Gamma^{\prime}_{0}}^{\!(\sigma)}\rangle
as in Eq. (
36
) but now with
N_{i}\!=\!2
and
N_{f}\!=\!4
since the
QQ\rightarrow Q^{c}Q^{c}Q^{c}Q^{c}
process is
2\!\rightarrow\!4
, and
|{{\cal M}^{\prime}}^{(\sigma)}_{0}|^{2}
being the matrix element.
Putting these together, we obtain the form of the scattering terms in the rhs of the Boltzmann equation
in Eq. (
41
)
as the contribution from the scattering and inverse scattering processes,
where the RIS pieces have overturned the sign of the inverse scattering terms.

Appendix D
UV Completion Corrections

As the momentum transfer in the effective VV interaction of Eq.(
1
) increases to approach
\Lambda
,
the point-like contact interaction will start to become resolved by propagating states in the UV completion.
This issue is encountered, for instance, in the scattering channel when the Mandelstam
s,t,u
approaches
\Lambda^{2}
.
For instance, in computing the thermal average cross section in Eq. (
30
),
momenta
p\gtrsim\Lambda
are sampled from the thermal spectrum.
Another aspect is the fact that we rely on loop corrections to obtain a CP violating effect leading to a baryon asymmetry,
and loop momenta being unrestricted, also resolve the effective vertex.
Due to these reasons, in a strict sense, an effective theory approach is not fully accurate and one will have to work with a specific UV completion.

This issue is not so pressing for the
{\cal X}
decay channel as the momenta are limited by
M_{\chi}

and the UV completion contributions appear only for momenta
\gtrsim\Lambda
.
This is true for loop momenta as well since we place cuts on the intermediate state propagators to obtain a
baryon asymmetry. In any case we study the effects of UV physics on the decay processes as well to confirm this expectation.

Even for scattering processes, our effective theory analysis should be adequate for obtaining a reasonable estimate of the baryon asymmetry generated for the following reasons:
(a) the probability of sampling momenta
p\sim\Lambda
in the thermal spectrum for
T\sim M_{\chi}
is not very large for
M_{\chi}/\Lambda\ll 1
, and
one can get a fairly good estimate using an effective theory;
(b) the baryon number violation in our setup is due to the
{\cal X}
, and any contributions from propagating UV physics states
excited in the large momentum regions in the loops should not nullify the effects we have identified;
(c) we take differences of loop amplitudes between a process and conjugate process to obtain the baryon asymmetry,
which should have dominant contributions from the
{\cal X}
sector.
Thus, although the results in the effective theory may not be fully accurate, it should capture the dominant contributions.
A big advantage is that it captures the behavior of a whole class of UV completions,
which is the spirit of our effective theory analysis.
We quantify next how good an approximation the effective theory computation of the thermal average cross section is
by comparing it to the result with corrections from an example UV completion included.

We compute the
{\langle\sigma\,v\rangle}
in the effective theory and compare it with that obtained by including corrections from
the state that resolves the effective vertex in the UV completion as the momentum transfer approaches
\Lambda
.
To do this, for illustration, we pick the
UV completion A
discussed in Ref.
[
4
]
,
in which the vertex is resolved as the heavy state
\xi
with mass
M_{\xi}
and decay width
\Gamma_{\xi}

begins to propagate for
p\gtrsim M_{\xi}
.
The
\xi
propagator is
1/(p^{2}-M_{\xi}^{2}+iM_{\xi}\Gamma_{\xi})
.
We focus on the scattering channels SC-1 and SC-2 of Sec.
4
.
SC-1 involves
\xi
exchange in the t-channel, while SC-2 is in the s-channel.
Deviations from a contact VV interaction due to the propagating
\xi
in
\sigma v
can be captured by the replacement at each vertex

(1/\Lambda^{2})\rightarrow(1/\Lambda^{2})f_{\xi}(p)
, taking
\Lambda=M_{\xi}
,
where the correction factor due to a propagating
\xi
is
f_{\xi}(p)=1/(1-\tilde{p}^{2}-i\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi})

with
\tilde{p}=p/\Lambda
and
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}=\Gamma_{\xi}/\Lambda
.
We have
f_{\xi}(p)\rightarrow 1
as
\Lambda\rightarrow\infty
, in other words, the effective theory is recovered in the limit of
r_{M\Lambda}\equiv M_{\chi}/\Lambda\ll 1
.

For the s-channel scattering process (i.e. for SC-2),
we subtract the on-shell
\xi
(resonance) contribution as this is included in the
\xi
decay contribution
and should not also be included in the scattering channel
(for a detailed discussion of this aspect, see Ref.
[
14
]
).
We compare two alternate schemes for the subtraction:
(a) use the principal value (PV) of the
\xi
propagator, and,
(b) cut-out the contribution under a total window width of
5\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}
centered at
\tilde{M}_{\xi}\equiv M_{\xi}/\Lambda=1
.
We implement (a) by defining the (finite) PV part as
1/(p^{2}-M^{2}+i\epsilon)={\rm PV}(1/(p^{2}-M^{2}))-\pi i\delta(p^{2}-M^{2})

isolating the singular (on-shell) part as the second term.
We use the representation
\delta(p^{2}-M^{2})=\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}[(\epsilon/\pi)/((p^{2}-M^{2})^{2}+\epsilon^{2})]
,
and connect with a physical interpretation by taking
\epsilon\equiv M_{\xi}\Gamma_{\xi}
.
We thus obtain

f^{\rm(PV)}_{\xi}(p)=1/(1-\tilde{p}^{2}-i\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi})-i\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}/((1-\tilde{p}^{2})^{2}+\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}^{2})
.
We see that our choice of the delta function ensures that the PV is real.
We view
f^{\rm(PV)}_{\xi}(p)
as a (quadratic) function of the 3-momentum radial component
p_{r}
,
and since
p_{r}\geq 0
we pick up only the positive pole contribution.

We thus obtain UV propagator correction factors in the LO scattering amplitudes given by

f_{\xi\,(t)}=f_{\xi}(p_{n}-q_{2})
(for SC-1),
and,

f_{\xi\,(s)}=f_{\xi}(p_{n}+k_{i})
(for SC-2).
In the LO cross sections the correction factors are

f_{\xi\,(t)}^{(00)}=|f_{\xi}(p_{n}-q_{2})|^{2}=1/((1-\tilde{t})^{2}+\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}^{2})
(for SC-1),
and

f_{\xi\,(s)}^{(00)}=|f_{\xi}(p_{n}+k_{i})|^{2}={\rm PV}(1/((1-\tilde{s})^{2}))
(for SC-2),
where

(\tilde{s},\tilde{t})=(s,t)/\Lambda^{2}
for the Mandelstam variables
s=(p_{n}+k_{i})^{2}
and
t=(p_{n}-q_{2})^{2}
,
and

\tilde{\Gamma}_{\xi}=\Gamma_{\xi}/\Lambda
with
\Gamma_{\xi}
being the
\xi
decay width,
which we also parametrize as the dimensionless
\hat{\Gamma}_{\xi}\equiv\Gamma_{\xi}/M_{\chi}
.

Part II: Technical Details

Some technical details and basic formalism aspects are in the appendices below.
Details of the computation of the matrix elements for our decay and scattering processes are in Appendix
E
.
Our choice of independent phase-space and loop-momentum variables, and,
the phase space and loop integral measures for decay and scattering processes
are in Appendix
F
.
The matrix elements and phase space measures discussed above are combined to obtain the
decay and scattering rate asymmetries in Appendix
G
.
Implications of
CPT
and unitarity relations on the matrix elements of forward and inverse processes
from a perturbation theory perspective in our new physics theory is discussed in Sec.
H
.
Some numerical details on the behavior of the thermal distribution functions and the thermal averages are in Appendix
I
,
and on the UV propagator corrections are in Appendix
J
.

Appendix E
The Decay and Scattering Matrix Elements

Here we provide various technical details of computing the matrix elements.
We fall back on some first principles calculations to
handle carefully the two identical
{D}
in the interaction,
and the Majorana nature of the
{\cal X}_{n}
.
We start by discussing the normalization of the
{D}{D}{U}
final state.
Following this, we compute the matrix element,
which we split into two fermion-line parts, the
{\cal X}{U}
line part,
and another
{D}{D}
line part in the final state and loops, and discuss them separately.
We then put them together to compute the tree-loop interference term in the
mod-squared of the matrix element, needed for the baryon asymmetry computation.
We give the details of how we renormalize (subtract) the
\bar{\cal X}_{m}{\cal X}_{n}
2-point function part of diagram B,
to be consistent with working in the mass basis.

In computing the matrix elements, we project onto a color-singlet combination of the
{D},{D},{U}
involved,
consistent with the structure of the effective interaction,
and normalize the states appropriately as we discuss in Appendix
E.1
.
In Appendix
E.2.1
we show how we handle the two identical
{D}
in the final state in computing the matrix element,
and in Appendix
E.3.2
we show the corresponding aspects in computing the loop diagram.
We describe in detail our method of computing the tree-loop interference term in Appendix
E.3.1
.

E.1
The
{D}{D}{U}
state

In computing the matrix element, we project onto a color-singlet combination of the 3-body
{U}{D}{D}
final state as

\left.|as{{\bf q}_{1}},bt{{\bf q}_{2}},cr{{\bf q}_{U}}\right>=\frac{\epsilon^{abc}}{\sqrt{6}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,\sqrt{2E_{\bf q_{U}}}\sqrt{2E_{\bf q_{2}}}\sqrt{2E_{\bf q_{1}}}\,{\hat{a}_{{\bf q}_{U}}}^{cr\,\dagger}{a_{{\bf q}_{2}}^{bt}}^{\dagger}{a_{{\bf q}_{1}}^{as}}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>\ ,

(57)

where
a,b,c
denote the color indices,
s,t,r
the spin indices, and,
q_{1},q_{2},q_{U}
the momenta of the
{D},{D},{U}
respectively,
we specialize to the
\epsilon^{abc}
here
for having a color singlet final state with the
1/(\sqrt{2}\sqrt{6})
providing the correct normalization,
and the fermionic creation operators anti-commute.

We can write the color-singlet state as a wave packet by introducing into Eq. (
57
)
a weight
K^{str}
for the spin helicities, and
\phi({{\bf q}_{U}},{{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})
for the momenta.
We pay particular attention to the two identical particles, namely
{D}{D}
(i.e.
\widebar{{D}^{c}}{D}
) in the final-state,
but distinguished by spin or color labels.
Focusing on this sector for now,
some example combinations of the two spins are:

K^{st}=\delta^{st}
is a symmetric same spin combination;

K^{st}=1,\forall\{s,t\}
, democratically combines all spins;

K^{st}=\epsilon^{st}
is an antisymmetric different spin combination.
As required for two fermions, we must get a minus sign under the interchange of the two
{D}
particles,
i.e. under
as{{\bf q}_{1}}\leftrightarrow bt{{\bf q}_{2}}
,
which requires that
K^{st}\phi({\bf q_{1}},{\bf q_{2}})\epsilon^{ab}
combined must yield this minus sign.
Since the last (i.e. the color part) is already antisymmetric and will yield a minus sign under interchange,
the
K^{st}\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})
part must yield a plus sign.
We note in particular that the simple state obtained by taking

K^{st}=1,\forall\{s,t\}
, and
\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})=1
as in Eq. (
57
)
is a fine
{D}{D}
state with the correct minus sign being obtained
on interchange of the two particles simply on account of the anticommutation property of the
a^{\dagger}
.

E.2
The tree-level decay matrix element

The
({{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U}})^{\mu}_{n}
is given as

({{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U}})^{\mu}_{n}\equiv\bar{u}(q_{U}){\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{n}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\mu}u(p_{n})\ ,

(58)

The mod-squared of the LO matrix element for the process,
averaged over initial state spins and summed over final state spins and colors is given by

|{{\cal A}^{n}_{0}}|^{2}=\frac{1}{4\Lambda^{4}}\frac{1}{2}(|{{\cal M}_{0}^{\chi U\,\!}}|^{2})^{\mu\tau}_{nn}\,C_{00}\,(|M^{12}_{0}|^{2})_{\mu\tau}\ ,

(59)

where we have defined
|M^{12}_{0}|^{2}_{\mu\tau}\equiv(M^{12}_{0})_{\mu}(M^{12}_{0})_{\tau}^{\dagger}
.
(We note here that although written as
|...|^{2}
, this is not necessarily a real quantity due to the different Lorentz indices involved.)
The replacements for obtaining the mod-squared amplitude for the conjugate process have already been given above.

E.2.1
The
{D}{D}
final state part
(M^{12}_{0})

Here we describe the computation of
(M^{12}_{0})
, the portion of the matrix element involving the
{D}
final state,
encountered in Eq. (
7
).
We pay careful attention to the presence of more than one identical
{D}
which lead to multiple contractions in the
matrix elements.

We start by being general in the effective interaction
and include scalar-scalar (SS) and vector-vector (VV) Lorentz structures
where for the VV interaction of Eq. (
1
) we have

\widetilde{\Gamma}=\widetilde{G}=\tilde{g}\gamma^{\mu}
and
\Gamma=G^{n}_{V\mu}=\gamma_{\mu}\left(\hat{g}_{L_{n}}P_{L}+\hat{g}_{R_{n}}P_{R}\right)
,
while for the SS interaction we have a similar coupling structure but without the
\gamma_{\mu}
.
Here we explain details involving the contraction of the field bilinear
\overline{{D}^{c}}\,\widetilde{\Gamma}{D}_{b}

appearing in the interaction,
with the two identical particle
{D}{D}
(final) state.
We use this to compute the
|M_{12}|^{2}
appearing in the decay width
\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{{D}^{c}}{D}{U})
,
discussed in Sec.
3
,
where two identical
{D}
particles appear in the final state since
\widebar{{D}^{c}}{D}\sim{D}{D}
.

We evaluate the contraction of the field bilinear
\overline{{D}^{c}}\,\widetilde{\Gamma}{D}_{b}

with the 2-
{D}
state as in Eq. (
57
), for now taking the simple weighting

K^{st}=1,\forall\{s,t\}
, and
\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})=1
.
Since the two
{D}
are in the final state, we evaluate the contraction with the hermitian conjugate of

\overline{{D}^{c}}\,\widetilde{\Gamma}{D}
, namely

\overline{{D}}\,\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}{D}^{c}
,
with
\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\equiv\gamma^{0}\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\dagger}\gamma^{0}
.
Thus, one of the things we need to evaluate the decay amplitude
\Gamma({\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{{D}^{c}}{D}{U})
is the matrix element

M_{12}^{st}\equiv\left<btq_{2},asq_{1}|\overline{{D}}_{a^{\prime}}\,\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}{D}_{b^{\prime}}^{c}|0\right>/\sqrt{2}
.
Now,

\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\overline{{D}}_{a^{\prime}}(x)\,\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}{D}_{b^{\prime}}^{c}(x)\epsilon^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\supset\int\sum_{a^{\prime},b^{\prime},s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}[dq^{\prime}_{1}][dq^{\prime}_{2}]{a_{{\bf q}_{1}^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}s^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}{a_{{\bf q}_{2}^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}t^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}\epsilon^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}}\bar{u}^{s^{\prime}}_{q_{1}^{\prime}}\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}v^{t^{\prime}}_{q_{2}^{\prime}}e^{i(q^{\prime}_{1}+q^{\prime}_{2})x}+...
.
We thus have the factor

\left<0|{a_{\bf q_{1}}^{as}}{a_{\bf q_{2}}^{bt}}{a_{\bf q_{1}^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}s^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}{a_{\bf q_{2}^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}t^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}|0\right>
in
M_{12}^{st}
,
which yields two contractions (due to the two identical
{D}
), to give

-\delta({{\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}-{\bf q}_{1}})\delta({{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}-{\bf q}_{2}})\delta^{a^{\prime}a}\delta^{s^{\prime}s}\delta^{b^{\prime}b}\delta^{t^{\prime}t}+\delta({{\bf q}^{\prime}_{1}-{\bf q}_{2}})\delta({{\bf q}^{\prime}_{2}-{\bf q}_{1}})\delta^{a^{\prime}b}\delta^{s^{\prime}t}\delta^{b^{\prime}a}\delta^{t^{\prime}s}
.

The two contractions are shown in Fig.
21
.

Figure 21:
The left and right diagrams show the two contractions of the
\overline{D}\,\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\,D^{c}
part of the interaction
with the
{D}{D}
final state. The momentum flow, spin, and color indices are shown as
(q,s,a)
respectively.

Using the antisymmetry in the color indices, which flips the sign on the second contraction (thus removing the relative minus sign),
we obtain
5
5

5

We do not include the factor of

(i\epsilon^{abc}/\Lambda^{2})
from the interaction vertex here but take it into account elsewhere.
Also, we include elsewhere the factor
e^{i(q_{1}+q_{2})x}/\sqrt{6}
.
We choose to include only the
1/\sqrt{2}
factor from the final state normalization here.

M_{12}^{st}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{u}^{s}_{q_{1}}\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}v^{t}_{q_{2}}+\bar{u}^{t}_{q_{2}}\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}v^{s}_{q_{1}})\ .

(60)

Taking the mod-square of this and summing over
s,t
, we have
|M_{12}|^{2}=\sum_{s,t}|M_{12}^{st}|^{2}
, which is,

|M_{12}|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left\{{\rm Tr}[\widetilde{\Gamma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)(\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c})(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]+(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{2})\right\}\ ,

(61)

where

\Gamma^{c}\equiv C\Gamma^{*}C
,
and we have used the spinor identity

\bar{v}_{q_{1}}^{s}\Gamma u_{q_{2}}^{t}=\bar{v}_{q_{2}}^{t}(-\bar{\Gamma}^{c})u_{q_{1}}^{s}\ ,

(62)

and similarly for
u,v
flipped. We note that
\widebar{\bar{\Gamma}}=\Gamma
and
\left(\widebar{\bar{\Gamma}}\right)^{c}=\Gamma^{c}
.
In particular, these properties apply to
\widetilde{\Gamma}
also.

We first consider the
SS
interaction with
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\tilde{g}_{L}P_{L}+\tilde{g}_{R}P_{R}
.
For this, we find
\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c}
, and Eq. (
61
) then yields
|M_{12}^{SS}|^{2}=0
.
In addition to the discussion
in our earlier work
showing that the
SS
interaction cannot be written down, if one were to persist,
we have now shown that it would not cause this decay with the two identical
{D}
in the final state.
It should be remembered that this result has been obtained for
K^{st}=1,\forall\{s,t\}
, and
\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})=1
.

Continuing with the
SS
interaction, we may still ask if contracting with a 2-
{D}
state with some other

K^{st}
and
\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})
may be nonzero.
To address this question, we now generalize the above result.
Due to the presence of the
K^{st}
, writing the spinor products of Eq. (
60
) summed over the spins as a
{\rm Tr}(...)

will not work so straightforwardly.
To facilitate this, we introduce the corresponding helicity projectors and then sum unrestricted over the spins,
leading to
{\rm Tr}(...)
but now with the projectors.
The helicity projectors for
u
-spinors is
\hat{P}_{s}({\bf q})\equiv(1/2)(1\pm\hat{q}^{i}\Sigma^{i})
for
s=\{1,2\}
,
and for
v
-spinors they are
\hat{P}^{\prime}_{s}({\bf q})\equiv(1/2)(1\mp\hat{q}^{i}\Sigma^{i})
(i.e.
\hat{P}^{\prime}_{1,2}=\hat{P}_{2,1}
),
with
\Sigma^{i}=\begin{pmatrix}\sigma^{i}&0\\
0&\sigma^{i}\end{pmatrix}
, and
\hat{q}^{i}
is the unit 3-vector along
{\bf q}
.
These projectors satisfy
\hat{P}_{s}({\bf q})\hat{P}_{t}({\bf q})=\delta^{st}\hat{P}_{s}({\bf q})
.
Introducing these projectors and denoting
\hat{P}_{s}({\bf q}_{i})\equiv P_{si}
and summing unrestricted over
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}

allows us to generalize Eq. (
60
) as

|M_{12}^{st}|^{2}=\sum_{s^{\prime},t^{\prime}}|K^{st}|^{2}|\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})|^{2}\frac{1}{2}|(\bar{u}^{s^{\prime}}_{q_{1}}P_{s1}\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}P^{\prime}_{t2}v^{t^{\prime}}_{q_{2}}+\bar{u}^{t^{\prime}}_{q_{2}}P_{t2}\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}P^{\prime}_{s1}v^{s^{\prime}}_{q_{1}})|^{2}\ .

(63)

Since now the sum over
s^{\prime},t^{\prime}
is unrestricted, we can write this as a
{\rm Tr}(...)
.
Using the notation
P_{s1}\Gamma P^{\prime}_{t2}\equiv\Gamma_{s1t^{\prime}2}
we have

\displaystyle|M_{12}^{st}|^{2}=|K^{st}|^{2}|\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})|^{2}\frac{1}{2}\left\{{\rm Tr}[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{t^{\prime}2s1}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)(\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c})_{s1t^{\prime}2}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]\right.

\displaystyle\left.+{\rm Tr}[\widetilde{\Gamma}_{s^{\prime}1t2}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}+M)(\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c})_{t2s^{\prime}1}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}-M)]\right\}\ ,

(64)

where we have used the identity
(\overline{\Gamma_{t2s^{\prime}1}})^{c}=(\bar{\Gamma}^{c})_{s1t^{\prime}2}
.
Eq. (
64
) is the generalization we seek.
For the
SS
interaction, we have
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c}=\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
, and from Eq. (
64
)
we see that
|M_{12}^{st}|^{2}=0
even for a general
K^{st}
. Again, even if we persisted and considered the
SS
interaction,
it would not induce a decay with
{D}^{s}{D}^{t}
in the final state for any orientations of the spins.

We consider next the
VV
interaction for
K^{st}=1,\forall\{s,t\}
, and
\phi({{\bf q}_{1}},{{\bf q}_{2}})=1
.
For the
VV
interaction we have
\widetilde{\Gamma}\equiv\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\mu}=\tilde{g}\gamma^{\mu}
,
and we must have
\tilde{g}_{L}=\tilde{g}_{R}\equiv\tilde{g}
.
For this we have
\widebar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c}=\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}={\tilde{g}}^{*}\gamma^{\mu}
,
and from Eq. (
60
), we have

(M^{12})^{st}_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{u}^{s}_{q_{1}}{\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}_{\mu}v^{t}_{q_{2}}+\bar{u}^{t}_{q_{2}}{\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}_{\mu}v^{s}_{q_{1}})\ .

(65)

Similarly, for the conjugate process, we find

(M^{c\,12})^{st}_{\mu}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\bar{u}^{s}_{q_{1}}(\widetilde{G}_{V})_{\mu}v^{t}_{q_{2}}+\bar{u}^{t}_{q_{2}}(\widetilde{G}_{V})_{\mu}v^{s}_{q_{1}})\ .

(66)

The mod-squared of this (for different Lorentz indices
\mu,\tau
) summed over
s,t
,
is
|{M_{12}}|^{2}_{\mu\tau}=\sum_{s,t}{M_{12}^{st}}_{\tau}^{\dagger}{M_{12}^{st}}_{\mu}
, which, from Eq. (
61
), is

\displaystyle|M^{12}|^{2}_{\mu\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{{\rm Tr}[(\widetilde{G}_{V})_{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M){\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]+(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{2})\right\}\ ,

(67)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|\tilde{g}|^{2}\left\{{\rm Tr}[\gamma_{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)\gamma_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]+(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{2})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2|\tilde{g}|^{2}{\rm Tr}[\gamma_{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)\gamma_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]\ ,

(68)

where the last line is due to the fact that the
(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{2})
term gives the same contribution
since the trace of an odd number of
\gamma
-matrices is zero.
We show at the end of Appendix
E.3.2
that this can be thought of equivalently as the cut of a loop diagram,
and introduce the notation
|M^{12}|^{2}=L_{12{}}
to capture this equivalence.
For the conjugate process, we similarly have

\displaystyle|M^{c\,12}|^{2}_{\mu\tau}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\{{\rm Tr}[{\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}_{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M)(\widetilde{G}_{V})_{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M)]+(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{2})\right\}\ ,

(69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|M^{12}|^{2}_{\mu\tau}\ .

E.3
The single operator tree-loop interference terms

We present here the details involved in the computation of the tree-loop interference term
for the decay and scattering rate difference.

E.3.1
The
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}
matrix element part

We obtain here the contractions leading to the tree-loop interference terms
for the decay rate of
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow{D}_{a}\overline{{D}^{c}}_{b}{U}_{c}

depicted in Fig.
5
.
For this purpose we write the mode expansion of the fermion fields as

\psi(x)=\int d^{3}p\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E_{\bf p}}}\left(u_{p}a_{\bf p}e^{-ipx}+v_{p}b_{\bf p}^{\dagger}e^{ipx}\right)\ ,\quad{\cal X}(x)=\int d^{3}p\frac{1}{\sqrt{2E_{\bf p}}}\left(u_{p}c_{\bf p}e^{-ipx}+v_{p}c_{\bf p}^{\dagger}e^{ipx}\right)\ ,

(70)

where
\psi=(D,U)
are Dirac fields, while
{\cal X}
is a Majorana field.
The charge-conjugate field
\psi^{c}(x)=C\psi^{*}(x)
has
a_{\bf p}\leftrightarrow b_{\bf p}
,
while
{\cal X}
is self-conjugate, i.e.
{\cal X}^{c}(x)={\cal X}(x)
.
We use the VV interaction of Eq. (
1
) to obtain the tree-loop interference term.

We start by deriving an expression for the
\chi-U
fermion line in the tree-loop interference term
in coordinate space, which is obtained from

{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}\sim\left<0|\right.c_{n}{\cal T}\left((\widebar{U^{c}}{G_{V}^{n}}^{\tau}{\cal X}_{n})\,(\bar{U}{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{m}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\sigma}{\cal X}_{m})\,(\widebar{{\cal X}_{m}}{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{\Lambda}^{m}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\mu}U^{c})\,(\widebar{U^{c}}{G_{\Lambda}^{n}}^{\nu}{\cal X}_{n})\right)c_{n}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>

(71)

where
{\cal T}
is the time-ordering symbol, and each
(...)
is at a different space-time point that is integrated over.
Plugging in the mode expansion of Eq. (
70
) into this
gives us the momentum space diagram shown in Fig.
22
.

Figure 22:
From this primitive diagram, the four different tree-loop interference processes A, B, C, D can be generated by
contracting the
q_{i}
with the various
q^{\prime}_{j}
as explained in the text.
The direction of the
q^{\prime}_{i}
momentum is such as to be in line with the momentum
q_{j}
with which it is contracted.

This gives the expression for
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}
in momentum space given in Eq. (
13
).
For the conjugate process,
all fermion-line arrows are flipped in the diagram, and we have

{{\cal M}_{01}^{c\,\chi U\,\!}}\sim\left<0|\right.c_{n}{\cal T}\left((\widebar{{\cal X}_{n}}{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{\Lambda}^{n}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\tau}U^{c})\,(\widebar{U^{c}}{G_{\Lambda}^{m}}^{\sigma}{\cal X}_{m})\,(\widebar{{\cal X}_{m}}{G_{V}^{m}}^{\mu}U)\,(\bar{U}{\phantom{\,}\bar{G}_{V}^{n}\phantom{\,\!}}^{\nu}{\cal X}_{n})\right)c_{n}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>\ ,

(72)

which yields a similar expression to that shown in Eq. (
13
) for
{{\cal M}_{01}^{\chi U\,\!}}

but with the change
G_{V}\leftrightarrow\bar{G}_{\Lambda}
.

We obtain next the expression for the
{D}
-loop in the tree-loop interference term.
What we need for this is

\left<0|\right.a_{1}a_{2}{\cal T}\left((\bar{{D}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{D}^{c})(\bar{{D}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{D}^{c})\right)b_{4}^{\dagger}b_{3}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>

(73)

where
a_{i}
is short for
a_{{\bf q}_{i}}
etc. for the
{D}
particle,
and where, again, each
(\bar{D}\cdot D^{c})
field bilinear is at a different
space-time point that is integrated over.
We have the particular choice of the final
\left<0|\right.a_{2}a_{1}
and the initial
b_{4}^{\dagger}b_{3}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>

since our focus here is on the contribution for the process (and not the conjugate process)
that is relevant for generating the baryon asymmetry,
which are themselves to be obtained via the VV interactions, i.e. from

-\left<0|\right.{\cal T}\left((\widebar{{D}^{c}_{2}}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\tau}{D}_{1})(\bar{{D}}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{D}^{c}_{4^{\prime}})(\bar{{D}}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{D}^{c}_{2^{\prime}})(\widebar{{D}^{c}_{4}}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\nu}{D}_{3})\right)\left.|0\right>

(74)

where we have shown this in momentum space with
D_{i}\equiv D_{q_{i}}
.
This is shown diagrammatically in Fig.
22
.
The overall minus sign is due to the labeling of the momenta as in the figure.

Putting together the color factors associated with these vertices we have

C_{01}^{(i)}=(\epsilon^{\alpha_{4}\alpha_{3}c^{\prime}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}^{\prime}\alpha_{1}^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{4}^{\prime}\alpha_{3}^{\prime}c}\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}c})/(\sqrt{6})^{2}\ ,

(75)

where the color index
\alpha_{i}
is associated with
q_{i}
etc.,
and the
1/\sqrt{6}
factor is from the color-part of the normalization of the
{D}{D}{U}
final state (see Eq. (
57
)).

From the two bilinears
(\bar{{D}}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{D}^{c}_{4^{\prime}})(\bar{{D}}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{D}^{c}_{2^{\prime}})
,
we need two
a^{\dagger}
’s and two
b
’s to give us a nonzero contraction.
There are four such possibilities, namely,

-\left<0|\right.a_{2}a_{1}\left(b_{3^{\prime}}b_{4^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{1^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{2^{\prime}}+a^{\dagger}_{3^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{4^{\prime}}b_{1^{\prime}}b_{2^{\prime}}+a^{\dagger}_{3^{\prime}}b_{4^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{1^{\prime}}b_{2^{\prime}}+b_{3^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{4^{\prime}}b_{1^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{2^{\prime}}\right)b_{4}^{\dagger}b_{3}^{\dagger}\left.|0\right>\ ,

where the momenta associated with
a^{\dagger},b^{\dagger}
flow outward while the momenta associated with the
a,b
flow inward.
These amount to attaching the different
q_{i}
to the various
q^{\prime}_{j}
in Fig.
22
.
The direction of the
q^{\prime}_{i}
momentum is such as to be in line with the momentum
q_{j}
with which it is contracted.
These four possibilities with the adjacent
a
’s and
b
’s contracted
(i.e. the momenta on them identified, and so too with the associated color indices)
correspond to the processes A, B, C, D respectively shown diagrammatically in Fig.
5

where we have not shown the diagram corresponding to the fourth term above (i.e. process D)
which is just a flip of process C about the horizontal fermion-line,
i.e. process D corresponds to interchanging
q_{1^{\prime}}\leftrightarrow q_{2^{\prime}}
and
q_{3^{\prime}}\leftrightarrow q_{4^{\prime}}
in process C.
Any time there is a cross of the contracted lines, we get a
(-1)
factor associated with an anticommutation of these fermionic operators.
All such
(-1)
factors are multiplied to give us an overall sign of that term.
Note that contracting non-adjacent operators, in effect, gives
a negative sign if it corresponds to doing an odd number of anticommutations.
There are totally four possibilities within each term including the adjacent and non-adjacent contractions, which can be viewed
as twists of the various legs in the diagrams.
Each contraction also has the associated color factor.
Including the spinor factors, we have more fully,

\displaystyle-\left<0|\right.a_{2}a_{1}\,\bar{v}_{2}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\tau}u_{1}\left(b_{3^{\prime}}b_{4^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{1^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{2^{\prime}}\ \bar{v}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{u}_{4^{\prime}}\ \bar{u}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{v}_{2^{\prime}}+a^{\dagger}_{3^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{4^{\prime}}b_{1^{\prime}}b_{2^{\prime}}\ \bar{u}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{v}_{4^{\prime}}\ \bar{v}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{u}_{2^{\prime}}+\right.

\displaystyle\left.a^{\dagger}_{3^{\prime}}b_{4^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{1^{\prime}}b_{2^{\prime}}\ \bar{u}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{u}_{4^{\prime}}\ \bar{u}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{u}_{2^{\prime}}+b_{3^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{4^{\prime}}b_{1^{\prime}}a^{\dagger}_{2^{\prime}}\ \bar{v}_{3^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{v}_{4^{\prime}}\ \bar{v}_{1^{\prime}}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{v}_{2^{\prime}}\right)b_{4}^{\dagger}b_{3}^{\dagger}\,\bar{u}_{4}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\nu}v_{3}\left.|0\right>\ .

(76)

The initial-spin averaged total decay rate has all of the color and spin indices summed over.
It turns out that in all cases the overall sign associated with twists within each process is undone by the color index structure.
Thus all four twists come with the same overall sign after including color also,
although the trace structure could be different.

In the way of an example, consider the first term, and contract the adjacent indices associated with the
a,a^{\dagger}
and
b,b^{\dagger}
,
i.e. we have
1\!\!=\!\!1^{\prime},2\!\!=\!\!2^{\prime},3\!\!=\!\!3^{\prime},4\!\!=\!\!4^{\prime}
,
and since no fermion contraction lines cross, the overall sign is +1.
The spinor factors with these contractions are

-\bar{v}_{2}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\tau}u_{1}\ \bar{v}_{3}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}{u}_{4}\ \bar{u}_{1}\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}{v}_{2}\ \bar{u}_{4}\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\nu}v_{3}
,
which upon summing over all spins yields

-{\rm Tr}[\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\tau}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{1}+M_{D})\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\mu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{2}-M_{D})]\,{\rm Tr}[\bar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}^{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M_{D})\widetilde{G}_{V}^{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}-M_{D})]
,
as given in Eq. (
16
).
From Eq. (
75
), the associated color factor with this is

C_{01}^{A}=\epsilon^{\alpha_{4}\alpha_{3}c^{\prime}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}c^{\prime}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{4}\alpha_{3}c}\epsilon^{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{1}c}/(\sqrt{6})^{2}

with all these indices summed over,
which yields
C_{01}^{A}=2\delta^{c^{\prime}c}2\delta^{c^{\prime}c}/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=12/(\sqrt{6})^{2}=2
as noted below Eq. (
16
).
It turns out that all four twists yields the same overall sign (after including the signs due to the anticommutations and the color factor)
and the same Tr[…] expression, explaining the factor of 4 for the
L_{D}^{A}
contribution in Eq. (
16
).
While writing the spinor products as
{\rm Tr}[...]
we sometimes make use of the spinor identity in Eq. (
62
).

A similar computation for all the four processes and twists therein gives the expressions in Eq. (
16
),
with the color-factors shown below that equation.
This explicitly tells us also that the expressions for processes C and D are identical.

Turning now to the
{D}
-loop expressions for the conjugate process,
we notice that the changes are only of the form
u_{p}\leftrightarrow v_{p}
,
which could potentially change the sign of the
M_{D}
terms in the
{\rm Tr}[...]
expressions,
and are therefore reflected only in terms with odd powers of
M_{D}
.
But such terms have the trace of an odd number of
\gamma
matrices, which is zero.
Therefore we conclude that the
{D}
-loop expressions for the conjugate process are the same
as for the process, i.e.
L^{c\,(i)}_{D}=L_{D}^{(i)}
given in Eq. (
16
).

E.3.2
The
{D}{D}
loop function in diagram A
1

Here we compute the
{D}{D}
Loop Function
L_{34}^{\sigma\nu}
appearing in diagram A
1
in
the loop contribution to the decay amplitude for
{\cal X}_{n}\rightarrow\widebar{{D}^{c}}{D}{U}
,
discussed in Sec.
3
, and shown in Fig.
4
.
We handle carefully the two identical
{D}
particles appearing in the loop.

For the
general Lorentz structure of the interaction alluded to in Appendix
E.2.1
,
the loop function in coordinate space is

L_{34}\propto\left<0|{\cal T}\ (\bar{D}_{a}\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}D^{c}_{b}\epsilon^{abc})_{y}(\bar{D}^{c}_{b^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Gamma}D_{a^{\prime}}\epsilon^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}})_{w}|0\right>,

where
{\cal T}
is the time-ordering symbol,
and the two operator bilinears are evaluated at different space-time points
y,w
.
The
D,D^{c}
fields when contracted yield two propagators
\Delta_{yw}
connecting the points.
From the usual mode expansion for the fields, in momentum space, we find the term

L_{34}\propto\left<0|{b_{\bf q_{3}}^{as}}{b_{\bf q_{4}}^{bt}}{b_{\bf q_{4}^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}t^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}{b_{\bf q_{3}^{\prime}}^{a^{\prime}s^{\prime}}}^{\dagger}|0\right>(\bar{v}_{q_{3}}^{s}\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}u_{q_{4}}^{t})(\bar{u}_{q^{\prime}_{4}}^{t^{\prime}}\widetilde{\Gamma}v_{q^{\prime}_{3}}^{s^{\prime}})\epsilon^{abc}\epsilon^{a^{\prime}b^{\prime}c^{\prime}}\ .

From the first factor it is evident that two contractions are possible, which is due to the two
{D}
particles being identical.
The two contractions yield

\delta({{\bf q}_{4}-{\bf q}^{\prime}_{4}})\delta({{\bf q}_{3}-{\bf q}^{\prime}_{3}})\delta^{aa^{\prime}}\delta^{ss^{\prime}}\delta^{bb^{\prime}}\delta^{tt^{\prime}}-\delta({{\bf q}_{3}-{\bf q}^{\prime}_{4}})\delta({{\bf q}_{4}-{\bf q}^{\prime}_{3}})\delta^{ab^{\prime}}\delta^{st^{\prime}}\delta^{ba^{\prime}}\delta^{ts^{\prime}}\ ,

and the relative minus sign in the second term is from two
b
’s being (anti)commuted before contracting.
These contractions are diagrammatically shown in Fig.
23
.

Figure 23:
The first (left) and second (right) contraction for the loop
{D}{D}
loop,
showing the routing of momentum, the spin index, and the color index, as
(q,s,a)
respectively.

Enforcing the
\delta
functions and summing over
s,t
,
we get
6
6

6

The
integration over the loop momenta
q_{3},q^{\prime}_{U}
is implied but is not explicitly written down in our discussion here.
We have also not included here the
i/\Lambda^{2}
factor, nor the denominators of the two fermion propagators,
nor a factor of
\epsilon^{abc}\epsilon^{abc^{\prime}}
.
All of these will be included elsewhere.

\displaystyle L_{34}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{s,t}(\bar{u}_{q_{4}}^{t}\widetilde{\Gamma}v_{q_{3}}^{s}\bar{v}_{q_{3}}^{s}\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}u_{q_{4}}^{t}+\bar{u}_{q_{3}}^{s}\widetilde{\Gamma}v_{q_{4}}^{t}\bar{v}_{q_{3}}^{s}\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}u_{q_{4}}^{t})

(77)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\rm Tr}[\widetilde{\Gamma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}-M)(\bar{\widetilde{\Gamma}}-\widetilde{\Gamma}^{c})(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M)]\,

(78)

where we have used the spinor identity of Eq. (
62
) and
note that an extra minus sign has flipped back the sign of the second term in Eq. (
77
)
due to interchanging the color indices
ab
.

In particular, for the
VV
interaction of Eq. (
1
), we get

\displaystyle(L_{34})^{\sigma\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,{\rm Tr}[(\widetilde{G}_{V})^{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}-M){\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}^{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M)]=2|\tilde{g}|^{2}\,{\rm Tr}[\gamma^{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}-M)\gamma^{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M)]\ .

(79)

We note that the RHS is in fact symmetric under
(q_{3}\leftrightarrow q_{4})
interchange
since the only term which breaks this symmetry, namely the term linear in
M_{D}
,
is zero since the trace of an odd number of
\gamma
-matrices is zero.
Similarly, for the conjugate process we find

\displaystyle(L^{c}_{34})^{\sigma\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\,{\rm Tr}[{\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}}^{\nu}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{3}-M)(\widetilde{G}_{V})^{\sigma}(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.00002pt{\hfil$q$\hfil}\hss}/_{4}+M)]=(L_{34})^{\sigma\nu}\ .

(80)

We note the similarity of the above
(L_{34})
with the
|M^{12}|^{2}
in Eq. (
68
).
Comparing the direction of momenta and making the translation
q_{3}\rightarrow-q_{1}
,
q_{4}\rightarrow-q_{2}
,
and
({\widebar{\widetilde{G}}_{V}})\leftrightarrow(\widetilde{G}_{V})
,
we obtain
L_{12{}}

and see that the mapping is exact.
We thus can think of the
{D}{D}
final state equivalently as a cut of the loop process
|M^{12}|^{2}\equiv L_{12{}}
.

E.3.3
The
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function counterterms

We have already discussed in Appendix
A

the important aspects of the renormalization and subtraction of the
{\cal X}_{n}\bar{\cal X}_{m}
2-point function.
Although not explicitly needed for our calculation of the baryon asymmetry in Sec.
3.2
, for completeness,
we present next the counterterms and the subtraction conditions for the hermitian part, i.e. the non-dissipative or PV part, of the 2-point function
to demonstrate the subtractions that keep us consistently in the mass basis when loop corrections generate off-diagonal terms.
We can split the hermitian counterterm
\delta\Sigma_{nm}
into individual counterterms and write

\delta\Sigma_{nm}\!\!=\!\!\delta\Omega_{nm}+\delta\Omega^{c}_{nm}
,
and we can try to pick each piece to cancel the loop contributions above at the subtraction point
p_{0}
.

For
n\!\!=\!\!m
, the 2-point function
\Sigma_{nn}(p_{n})
has both legs the same particle and onshell.
We impose a renormalization condition such that the loop contribution to the hermitian part of the 2-point function is
canceled by a counterterm at the on-shell subtraction points
p_{n}\!\!=\!\!p_{n_{0}}
(for
n\!\!=\!\!1,2
), with

p_{1_{0}}^{2},p_{2_{0}}^{2}\!\!=\!\!M_{1,2}^{2}
,
leaving
M_{n}\!\!=\!\!M_{1,2}
uncorrected and as the renormalized masses, consistent with being in the mass-basis.
We can take

\delta\Omega_{nn}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega_{nn}(p_{n_{0}}))
and
\delta\Omega^{c}_{nn}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega^{c}_{nn}(p_{n_{0}}))
,
which implies
\delta\Sigma_{nn}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{nn}(p_{n_{0}}))
.
With this subtraction, there is no loop contribution to the PV part
for
nm\!\!=\!\!\{11,22\}
at the subtraction point
\{p_{1_{0}},p_{2_{0}}\}
respectively
as they are entirely canceled by the counterterms.

For
n\!\!\neq\!\!m
, with the
{\cal X}_{n}
-leg on-shell but the
{\cal X}_{m}
-leg necessarily off-shell.
Due to the Majorana nature of
{\cal X}_{n}
, we can show generally that

\bar{\cal X}_{m}\Sigma_{nm}{\cal X}_{n}\!\!=\!\!\bar{\cal X}_{n}(C\gamma^{0}\Sigma_{nm}^{T}\gamma^{0}C){\cal X}_{m}
(no sum on
n,m
),
and therefore we must have the relation
\Sigma_{mn}\!\!=\!\!C\gamma^{0}\Sigma_{nm}^{T}\gamma^{0}C
.
In our theory with the VV interaction, we find that
\Omega_{mn}\!\!=\!\!C\gamma^{0}\Omega_{nm}^{cT}\gamma^{0}C
,
and the above relation is satisfied in an interesting way.
Thus, for Majorana fermions,
\delta\Sigma_{nm}
and
\delta\Sigma_{mn}

cannot be independently chosen to simultaneously cancel both
{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{21}(p_{2_{0}}))
and
{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))
.
We can at best cancel one of them, say
{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))
by the counterterm choice

\delta\Sigma_{12}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))
,
suggesting
\delta\Omega_{12}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))
and
\delta\Omega^{c}_{12}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega^{c}_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))
.
Owing to the earlier relation, this then fixes
\delta\Sigma_{21}
also,
which individually fixes
\delta\Omega_{21}\!\!=\!\!-C\gamma^{0}{\rm PV}({\Omega_{12}^{c}}^{\!\!T}(p_{1_{0}}))\gamma^{0}C\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega_{21}(p_{1_{0}}))
and

\delta\Omega^{c}_{21}\!\!=\!\!-C\gamma^{0}{\rm PV}({\Omega}^{T}_{12}(p_{1_{0}}))\gamma^{0}C\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Omega^{c}_{21}(p_{1_{0}}))
implying

\delta\Sigma_{21}\!\!=\!\!-{\rm PV}(\Sigma_{21}(p_{1_{0}}))
.

Finally, we can write the renormalized 2-point function as

\Omega_{nm}^{(ren)}(p)=\Omega_{nm}(p)-\Omega_{nm_{s}}\ ,

(81)

where the subtraction term for
n\!\!=\!\!m
is
\Omega_{nn_{s}}\!\!=\!\!{\rm PV}(\Omega_{nn}(p_{n_{0}}))
with
p_{n_{0}}\!\!=\!\!M_{n}^{2}
,
while for
n\!\!\neq\!\!m
it is
\Omega_{nm_{s}}\!\!=\!\!{\rm PV}(\Omega_{nm}(p_{1_{0}}))
with
p_{1_{0}}\!\!=\!\!M_{1}^{2}
.
These now include both the discontinuity and the regular pieces of the 2-point function,
with a subtraction for the regular part but none for the discontinuity.
We similarly define the subtracted
\Omega_{nm}^{c\,(ren)}(p)
.

We can write the
\Omega_{mn}
as

\Omega_{mn}\!\!=\!\!J^{\mu\nu}(M_{U})L_{\mu\nu}(M_{D})
,
where the
J
is the
{\cal X}{U}
fermion line, and the
L
is the
{D}
loop,
and similarly
\Omega^{c}_{mn}\!\!=\!\!J^{c\mu\nu}(M_{U})L^{c}_{\mu\nu}(M_{D})
.
We can define dimensionless versions of the above loop functions that are functions of the scaled (hatted) masses and momenta defined in
Sec.
3.2
, namely
\hat{p}_{i}\!\!\equiv\!\!p_{i}/M_{n}
and
\hat{M}_{i}\!\!\equiv\!\!M_{i}/M_{n}
.
We thus write

\Omega_{nm}\!\!=\!\!(M_{n}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})\hat{\Omega}_{nm}

with
\hat{\Omega}_{nm}\!\!=\!\!\hat{J}_{nm}^{\mu\nu}(\hat{M}_{U})\hat{L}_{nm\mu\nu}(\hat{M}_{D})
,
where
the
1/\Lambda^{4}
follows from the 2 VV interactions, implying the
M_{n}^{5}
scaling for the
\Omega_{nm}
since it has mass dimension +1.
We then have

\Omega_{nm}^{(ren)}(p)=(M_{n}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})\,\left[\hat{J}_{nm}(p/M_{n},M_{U}/M_{n})\hat{L}_{nm}(p/M_{n},M_{D}/M_{n})-(M_{1}/M_{n})^{5}\hat{J}_{nm_{s}}\hat{L}_{nm_{s}}\right]\ ,

(82)

where we have defined

\hat{J}_{nm_{s}}\!\equiv\!{\rm PV}(\hat{J}_{nm}(p_{1_{0}}/M_{1},M_{U}/M_{1}))
,
and

\hat{L}_{nm_{s}}\!\equiv\!{\rm PV}(\hat{L}_{nm}(p_{1_{0}},M_{D}/M_{1}))
.
We similarly define

\Omega_{nm}^{c}\!\!=\!\!(M_{n}^{5}/\Lambda^{4})\hat{\Omega}_{nm}^{c}

with
\hat{\Omega}_{nm}^{c}\!\!=\!\!\hat{J}_{nm}^{c\mu\nu}\hat{L}^{c}_{\mu\nu}
,
and write an analogous expression for
\Omega_{nm}^{c\,(ren)}(p)
.

E.4
Contributions from diagrams without arrow clash

Here we discuss contributions to the baryon asymmetry from diagrams without a clash in the arrow depicting baryon number charge flow,
and show that these do not generate any rate difference.

E.4.1
Single operator contribution

Consider for example, the diagram shown in Fig.
24
, and also similar diagrams with the
{D}^{c}
(also
\widebar{{D}^{c}}
) and
{U}
interchanged.
Although it may appear to contribute, upon a more careful analysis, we find that it does not.
We explain the reasons below.

Figure 24:

The diagram representing the tree-loop interference term
that does not have a clash of fermion number flow (i.e. no arrow clash),
where the arrows on the fermion lines show the direction of baryon number flow.
The dashed line shows the cut propagators.

The figure shows the diagram that corresponds to the tree-loop interference contribution
{\cal A}_{01}={\cal A}_{1}{\cal A}_{0}^{*}
for the process,
where
{\cal A}_{0}
is the LO amplitude and
{\cal A}_{1}
is the loop amplitude.
The corresponding contribution
{\cal A}_{01}^{c}
for the conjugate process is got by reversing all the arrows
changing all fields to conjugate fields and picking up the appropriate couplings.
The dashed lines in the figure shows the cuts we make in the diagram.
The ones labeled
Rcut
together with
M2cut
give the 3-body phase space for
{\cal A}_{01}
, where the part of the diagram to the right of these cuts
is nothing but
{\cal A}_{0}^{*}
and to the left of these cuts
{\cal A}_{1}
.
As explained earlier, the “weak phase” (which flips in
{\cal A}_{01}
vs.
{\cal A}_{01}^{c}
) is provided by complex couplings,
and the “strong phase” (which does not flip) by making the cuts labeled
Lcut
and
M3cut
in
{\cal A}_{1}
.
Taking into account the
i
’s in the vertices, propagators and the cuts, overall, we find an
i
that remains and acts as the strong phase.

We recall from Sec.
2.1
that the baryon asymmetry is given by

{\cal A}_{B}\propto\,2\,{\rm Re}({\cal A}_{01}-{\cal A}_{01}^{c})=2\,{\rm Re}(\Delta{\cal A}_{01})=\Delta{\cal A}_{01}+\Delta{\cal A}_{01}^{*}
.
We realize that
{\cal A}_{01}^{*}
corresponds to interchanging the interpretations of
Lcut
and
Rcut
,
i.e. viewing the
Lcut
together with
M2cut
as giving the 3-body phase-space with the piece to the left of it being

{\cal A}_{0}
and the right of if being
{\cal A}_{1}^{*}
,
and the
Rcut
together with
M3cut
as contributing the strong phase to
{\cal A}_{1}^{*}
.
This different interpretation amounts to interchanging
q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{4}
and
q_{U}\leftrightarrow q_{U}^{\prime}
.
We therefore add this interchanged contribution and consider

2{\rm Re}({\cal A}_{01})={\cal A}_{01}(q_{1},q_{U},q_{4},q_{U}^{\prime})+{\cal A}_{01}(q_{1}\leftrightarrow q_{4},q_{U}\leftrightarrow q_{U}^{\prime})\equiv{\cal A}_{01}^{(L\leftrightarrow R)}
.
We take the difference between this and the corresponding amplitude for the conjugate process and obtain

\Delta{\cal A}_{01}^{(L\leftrightarrow R)}
.

We split up the
\Delta{\cal A}_{01}^{(L\leftrightarrow R)}
into different Dirac traces where we pick either the
\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to5.03125pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}p
or the
M

in each of the four fermion propagator numerators in the
{\cal X}-{U}
fermion line
(the
{D}
loop yields another Dirac-trace which we include in full in each of them),
namely,
(i)
M_{n},M_{m},M_{U}
independent contribution,
(ii)
M_{m}M_{U}
contribution,
(iii)
M_{n}M_{U}
contribution,
(iv)
M_{U}M_{U}
contribution.
The rest are all zero in the difference.
Given the remaining strong phase
i
that we identified, we seek the Im part of each of them,
which has two possibilities

{\rm Re}({\rm couplings}){\times}{\rm Im}({\rm Tr}[...])
, or,
{\rm Im}({\rm couplings})\times{\rm Re}({\rm Tr}[...])
.
The Im(Tr[…]) yields four 4-momenta contracted with the
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\sigma\tau}
,
which evaluated in the proper frame (see Sec.
F
) picks up the
0^{th}
component (
E
) of one of the momenta involved
times a scalar triple product (of the other 3-momenta involved).
This is odd in the azimuthal angle
\phi_{3}
or
\phi_{U^{\prime}}

(we set the azimuthal angles
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{U}
to zero without loss of generality as we are interested in the
spin-averaged decay rate of the unpolarized
{\cal X}_{n}
in its rest frame),
and when integrated over these azimuthal angles, the decay rate difference between the process and conjugate process
due to these
\epsilon^{....}
terms is zero.
Another way to see this is to consider this in the
{\cal X}_{n}
rest frame.
By 3-momentum conservation, the three final-state 3-momenta lie in a plane and their scalar triple product is zero.
Now, the remaining
{\rm Im}({\rm couplings})\times{\rm Re}({\rm Tr}[...])
contributions we find are all zero also,
where (ii) and (iii) become zero only in the
L\leftrightarrow R
contribution discussed above.
Thus, in summary, the diagram of Fig.
24
does not contribute to
{\cal A}_{B}
.
We therefore focus on the diagrams with an arrow clash as detailed in the main text.

E.4.2
Multiple operator contribution

We consider next diagrams
A_{1}^{\prime(2),(3)}
in Fig.
8

and show that no asymmetry arises from these,
as we could anticipate from there being no arrow clash in these diagrams.
Putting the cut propagators on-shell we find that the decay rate difference between the process and conjugate process

\Delta{\Gamma}^{(2),(3)}_{01}\propto\Delta{\cal A}^{\prime(2),(3)}_{01}

can be written in terms of couplings times a combination of the two integrals

\Delta\hat{\cal I}^{\prime}_{a,b}\equiv\int\frac{d^{4}q_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\ p_{n}^{\mu}q_{1}^{\nu}q_{2}^{\sigma}q_{3}^{\tau}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\sigma\tau}\left\{p_{n}\cdot(q_{1}-q_{2})\,,\ q_{3}\cdot(q_{1}-q_{2})\right\}\ .

(83)

These integrals involve Lorentz invariant dot-products of momenta, and each dot-product can be evaluated in a convenient frame,
as already discussed in Sec.
3.2
.
Much of the techniques of that section apply here by realizing that the following simplifications in the variables of that section help us get results here:
identify
q_{U}^{\prime}\equiv q_{U}
, identify the
*
-frame and
{\oasterisk}
-frame, integration is now only over
q_{3}
(and
not
also over
q_{U}^{\prime}
),
and applying the cuts discussed above imply that
{q_{3}}_{r}
is not independent anymore.
Making these changes, we find that the integrands of these master integrals are proportional to
\sin{\phi_{3}^{*}}
,
and therefore when integrated over
\phi_{3}^{*}
to get the total rate, these integrals are zero.
The same conclusion can be reached by considering the dot products in the
{\cal X}_{n}
rest-frame.
The first dot product is a scalar triple product of
{\bf q}_{1},{\bf q}_{2},{\bf q}_{3}

that is odd with respect to flipping the direction of
{\bf q}_{3}
.
This is sufficient to show that
\Delta\hat{\cal I}^{\prime}_{a}
is zero.
Now for showing
\Delta\hat{\cal I}^{\prime}_{b}
is zero,
we observe that the integrand is odd with respect to flipping
{\bf q}_{3},{\bf q}_{1},{\bf q}_{2}
simultaneously, and thus the integral is zero
after the integration over phase space is also done.
We thus find no integrated rate asymmetry from diagrams
A_{1}^{\prime(2),(3)}
.

Appendix F
The Phase-space and Loop Integration Variables

Here we arrive at our choice of independent variables for the 3-body phase-space momenta
and the loop momenta
in the decay process
{\cal X}_{n}(p_{n})\rightarrow{D}(q_{1}){D}(q_{2}){U}(q_{U})
,
and write down the phase-space and loop-integration measures.
Our notation is that
q_{i}
is a 4-vector, while
{\bf q}_{i}
is the corresponding 3-vector
with 3-magnitude (radial component)
{q_{i}}_{r}=|{\bf q}_{i}|
.
An on-shell particle has
E_{i}=\sqrt{{q_{i}}^{2}_{r}+M_{i}^{2}}
.
All final state particles are on-shell;
the
{D},{U}
particles in loops are also on-shell
given our focus on computing the baryon asymmetry as explained in the text.

F.1
The 3-body phase-space variables and measure (for
{\cal X}
decay)

We start by writing the 3-body phase-space integral of the matrix element as a cut of the corresponding loop diagram as

\int d\Pi_{3}\,|{\cal A}|^{2}=\int\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{4}q_{U}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}q_{1}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}q_{2}}{(2\pi)^{4}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{\rm cut}_{12U}\,(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p_{n}-(q_{1}+q_{2}+q_{U}))|{\cal A}|^{2}\ ,\quad

(84)

where we put the three propagators on-shell by replacing them with

7
7

7

The
i
is omitted in the expressions for
d\Pi_{3}
here but is included elsewhere in the text.

(-2\pi i)^{3}\delta^{\rm cut}_{12U}

where
\delta^{\rm cut}_{12U}\equiv\delta(q_{U}^{2}-M_{U}^{2})\delta(q_{1}^{2}-M_{1}^{2})\delta(q_{2}^{2}-M_{2}^{2})
,
where for the decay channel,
M_{1}=M_{2}=M_{D}
.
We include a factor of
1/2
to account for the two identical
{D}
in the final state.
We deal with the kinematical aspects of the phase-space here.

At the outset, we choose to enforce the overall energy-momentum conserving
\delta
-function in Eq. (
84
)
by performing the
d^{4}q_{2}
integral, which sets
q_{2}=p_{n}-(q_{1}+q_{U})
,
and giving the form of Eq. (
93
).
This leaves 8 integration variables,
with the measure

d^{4}q_{U}d^{4}q_{1}=dq_{U}^{0}dc_{U}d\phi_{U}d{q}_{U_{r}}{q}_{U_{r}}^{2}dq_{1}^{0}dc_{1}d\phi_{1}d{q_{1}}_{r}{q_{1}}_{r}^{2}
,

where
c_{i}\equiv\cos{\theta_{q_{i}}}
and
\phi_{i}\equiv\phi_{q_{i}}
.
We have

\delta(q_{i}^{2}-M_{i}^{2})=\delta({q_{i}^{0}}^{2}-E_{i}^{2})=\delta({q_{i}^{0}}-E_{i})/|2E_{i}|

where
E_{i}^{2}={q_{i}}_{r}^{2}+M_{i}^{2}
for each of the
q_{U},q_{1}
, picking the positive root for a physical on-shell particle.

The Lorentz invariance of each factor allows us to take the
d^{4}q_{U}
and
d^{4}q_{1}
in
different
frames.
A convenient frame to consider is what we call as the *-frame in which
{\bf p}_{n}^{*}={\bf q}_{U}^{*}
.
In this frame, 3-momentum conservation implies
{\bf q}_{2}^{*}=-{\bf q}_{1}^{*}
, i.e. these are back-to-back.
The advantage of this frame is that the end-points of
{q_{1}}_{r}^{\!*}
are independent of the angular variables.

For a given 4-vector in the lab-frame, to obtain the corresponding 4-vector in the *-frame, we make a Lorentz transformation as follows.
We first form the 4-vector
k_{B}\equiv(p_{n}-q_{U})=(E_{B},{\bf k}_{B})
.
The boost rapidity
\eta
that takes
k_{B}
to
k_{B}^{*}\equiv(M_{B},{\bf 0})
, i.e. to the
k_{B}
rest-frame,
is given by
t_{\eta}\equiv\tanh\eta={k_{B}}_{r}/E_{B}
.
The required Lorentz transformation is a boost with this rapidity in the direction of the unit-vector
\!\!\hat{\,\,\bf k}_{B}={\bf k}_{B}/{k_{B}}_{r}
.
More explicitly, consider any lab-frame 4-vector
p=(E_{p},{\bf p})
for which we seek the corresponding *-frame 4-vector
p^{*}=(E_{p}^{*},{\bf p}^{*})
.
We first decompose as
{\bf p}={\bf p}_{\parallel}+{\bf p}_{\perp}
, where
{\bf p}_{\parallel}=({\bf p}\!\cdot\!\!\!\hat{\,\,\bf k}_{B})\,\!\!\hat{\,\,\bf k}_{B}
and
{p_{\parallel}}_{r}=|{\bf p}_{\parallel}|
, and,
{\bf p}_{\perp}={\bf p}-{\bf p}_{\parallel}
.
Then,
{\bf p}^{*}={\bf p}_{\parallel}^{*}+{\bf p}_{\perp}^{*}
with
{\bf p}_{\parallel}^{*}={p_{\parallel}}_{r}^{\!*}\,\!\!\hat{\,\,\bf k}_{B}
, and

\begin{pmatrix}E_{p}^{*}\\
{p_{\parallel}}_{r}^{\!*}\end{pmatrix}={\cal B}_{\eta}\begin{pmatrix}E_{p}\\
{p_{\parallel}}_{r}\end{pmatrix}{\rm where}\ {\cal B}_{\eta}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}c_{\eta}&-s_{\eta}\\
-s_{\eta}&c_{\eta}\end{pmatrix}\ ;\quad{\bf p}_{\perp}^{*}={\bf p}_{\perp}\ ,

with
s_{\eta}=\sinh\eta=t_{\eta}/\sqrt{1-t_{\eta}^{2}}
and
c_{\eta}=\cosh\eta=1/\sqrt{1-t_{\eta}^{2}}
.
The inverse transformation that takes a *-frame 4-vector to the lab-frame is obtained the same way, but with the rapidity
-\eta
.

Using the Lorentz invariance of each measure as mentioned above,
we take the
d^{4}q_{U}\delta(q_{U}^{2}-M_{U}^{2})
in the lab-frame (i.e. the
{\cal X}
rest-frame for decays),
and the
d^{4}q^{*}_{1}\delta({q^{*}_{1}}^{2}-M_{1}^{2})
and the remaining
\delta({q^{*}_{2}}^{2}-M_{2}^{2})
in the *-frame.
The
dq_{U}^{0}
and
d{q^{*}_{1}}^{0}
integrals can be performed enforcing the first two
\delta
-functions, leaving

d\Pi_{3}\,=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}}\frac{1}{2}dc_{U}d\phi_{U}d{q}_{U_{r}}\frac{{q}_{U_{r}}^{2}}{2E_{U}}d\phi^{*}_{1}dc^{*}_{1}d{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}\frac{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}^{2}}{2E^{*}_{1}}\delta({q^{*}_{2}}^{2}-M_{2}^{2})\ .

(85)

We write the remaining
\delta-
function as
\delta({q^{*}_{2}}^{2}-M_{2}^{2})\equiv\delta(f({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}))
, viewing the argument now as a function of
{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}
,
i.e.
f({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r})={q^{*}_{2}}^{2}-M_{2}^{2}={E_{2}^{*}}^{2}-{q_{2}^{*}}_{\!r}^{2}-M_{2}^{2}
,
with
E_{2}^{*}=E_{n}^{*}-E_{U}^{*}-E_{1}^{*}({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r})
and
{q_{2}^{*}}_{\!r}={q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}
, owing to
{\bf p}^{*}_{n}={\bf q}^{*}_{U}
.
We have
\delta(f({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}))=\delta({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}-{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}_{z})/|df/d{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}|
where
{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}_{z}
is the zero of
f
, and the denominator is evaluated at the zero.
We find that

{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}_{z}=(M_{B}/2)\{[1+(M_{1}^{2}-M_{2}^{2})/M_{B}^{2}]^{2}-4M_{1}^{2}/M_{B}^{2}\}^{1/2}
,

|df/d{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}|=2({q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}/E_{1}^{*})(E_{n}^{*}-E_{U}^{*})
,
and

M_{B}^{2}=(E_{n}^{*}-E_{U}^{*})^{2}=(p_{n}-q_{U})^{2}
being a Lorentz invariant quantity can be computed in any frame.
We can then perform the
d{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}
integral enforcing the last
\delta-
function which sets
{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}={{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}_{z}
,
and we denote henceforth the zero simply as
{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}
.
We finally have

d\Pi_{3}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}}\frac{1}{2}dc_{U}d\phi_{U}d{q}_{U_{r}}d\phi^{*}_{1}dc^{*}_{1}\frac{{q}_{U_{r}}^{2}}{2E_{U}}\frac{{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}}{4(E_{n}^{*}-E_{U}^{*})}\ ,

(86)

in terms of the five independent 3-body phase-space variables
c_{U},\phi_{U},{q}_{U_{r}},c^{*}_{1},\phi^{*}_{1}
.

For the decay process, in the
{\cal X}
rest-frame, the matrix element will be independent of
\phi_{U}
and the
d\phi_{U}
integral just yields
2\pi
,
and we can include this factor and conveniently set
\phi_{U}=0
.
The limits of integration for the independent variables are straightforward, except for the
{q}_{U_{r}}
, which is bounded above.
Clearly, the minimum value of
{q}_{U_{r}}
is
0
, i.e.
{q}_{U_{r}}^{(min)}=0
.
We can find the maximum value
{q}_{U_{r}}^{(max)}
straightforwardly in the restframe of
{\cal X}
i.e. with
{p_{n}}_{r}=0
, as follows.
From the Lorentz invariance of
M_{B}^{2}
, we can also write
M_{B}^{2}=(p^{*}_{n}-q^{*}_{U})^{2}=(q_{1}^{*}+q_{2}^{*})^{2}=(E_{1}^{*}+E_{2}^{*})^{2}

where we have used 4-momentum conservation and the fact that
{\bf q}^{*}_{1}+{\bf q}^{*}_{2}=0
.
We also have
M_{B}^{2}=(E_{n}^{*}-E_{U}^{*})^{2}=(p_{n}-q_{U})^{2}=M_{n}^{2}+M_{U}^{2}-2(E_{n}E_{U}-{p_{n}}_{r}{q}_{U_{r}}c_{nU})
with
c_{nU}={\bf p}_{n}\!\cdot\!{\bf q}_{U}/({p_{n}}_{r}{q}_{U_{r}})
,
and for
{p_{n}}_{r}=0
, we see that
{q}_{U_{r}}^{(max)}
is attained when
M_{B}
takes its minimum value,
which is for
{q_{1}^{*}}_{\!r}={q_{2}^{*}}_{\!r}=0
, and is
M_{B}^{(min)}=(M_{1}+M_{2})
.
From this we find
{q}_{U_{r}}^{(max)}=(M_{n}/2)\{[1+(M_{U}^{2}-(M_{1}+M_{2})^{2})/M_{n}^{2}]^{2}-4M_{U}^{2}/M_{n}^{2}\}^{1/2}
.
Thus, the
{q}_{U_{r}}
domain of integration is
{q}_{U_{r}}\in(0,{q}_{U_{r}}^{(max)})
, while the other integrations are over the usual full range.

F.2
The loop variables and measure (for
{\cal X}
decay)

This analysis closely parallels
that of the 3-body phase-space variables discussed in Sec.
F.1
.

F.2.1
Two-loop integral measure

We have the two-loop integral measure (such as for Fig.
5
) with three cut propagators given by
8
8

8

The
i
is included elsewhere in the text.

d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{4}q^{\prime}_{U}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}q_{3}}{(2\pi)^{4}}\frac{d^{4}q_{4}}{(2\pi)^{4}}(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{\rm cut}_{34U}\,(2\pi)^{4}\delta^{4}(p_{n}-(q_{3}+q_{4}+q^{\prime}_{U}))\ ,

(87)

with

\delta^{\rm cut}_{34U}=\delta({q^{\prime}_{U}}^{2}-M_{U}^{2})\delta(q_{3}^{2}-M_{3}^{2})\delta(q_{4}^{2}-M_{4}^{2})
with
M_{3}=M_{4}=M_{D}
.
We have shown also the overall 4-momentum conserving
\delta-
function,
which we enforce at the outset by performing the
d^{4}q_{4}
integral, which sets
q_{4}=p_{n}-q_{3}-q^{\prime}_{U}
,
and yields

d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}=(1/2)\,(1/(2\pi)^{5})d^{4}q_{3}\,d^{4}q^{\prime}_{U}\,\delta^{\rm cut}_{34U}
.

We follow a procedure identical to the 3-body phase-space discussion in Sec.
F.1
, but with the following changes:

q_{U}\rightarrow q^{\prime}_{U}
,
q_{1}\rightarrow q_{3}
,
q_{2}\rightarrow q_{4}
,
M_{1}\rightarrow M_{3}
,
M_{2}\rightarrow M_{4}
,
forming
k^{\prime}_{B}\equiv(p_{n}-q^{\prime}_{U})=(E^{\prime}_{B},{\bf k}^{\prime}_{B})
, and analogous to the *-frame earlier as the rest-frame of
k_{B}
,
we now construct the
{\oasterisk}
-frame as the rest-frame of the
k^{\prime}_{B}
, i.e.
{k^{\prime}_{B}}^{\oasterisk}=(M^{\prime}_{B},{\bf 0})
, with
{M^{\prime}_{B}}^{2}={k^{\prime}_{B}}^{2}
.
In the
{\oasterisk}
-frame the
{\bf q}_{3}^{\oasterisk}=-{\bf q}_{4}^{\oasterisk}
, i.e. these are back-to-back.
The boost rapidity that takes us from the lab-frame to the
{\oasterisk}
-frame is now
t^{\prime}_{\eta}\equiv\tanh\eta^{\prime}={k^{\prime}_{B}}_{r}/E^{\prime}_{B}
,
and the corresponding
p^{\oasterisk}=(E_{p}^{\oasterisk},{\bf p}^{\oasterisk})
with
{\bf p}^{\oasterisk}={\bf p}_{\parallel}^{\oasterisk}+{\bf p}_{\perp}^{\oasterisk}
is constructed in an analogous way.
We use the Lorentz invariance of each factor in the integration measure and take
q^{\prime}_{U}
in the lab-frame, and
q_{3}^{\oasterisk}
in the
{\oasterisk}-
frame.
We finally write the measure analogously as

d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{5}}\frac{1}{2}dc^{\prime}_{U}d\phi^{\prime}_{U}d{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}dc^{\oasterisk}_{3}d\phi^{\oasterisk}_{3}\frac{{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}^{2}}{2E^{\prime}_{U}}\frac{{q_{3}}_{r}^{\!\!{\oasterisk}}}{4({E_{n}}^{\!\!{\oasterisk}}-{E^{\prime}_{U}}^{\!\!{\oasterisk}})}\ ,

(88)

in terms of the five independent loop variables
c^{\prime}_{U},\phi^{\prime}_{U},{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r},c^{\oasterisk}_{3},\phi^{\oasterisk}_{3}
for integration over the on-shell loop momenta.
The domain of integration for these variables are the usual full range,
except for
{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}\in(0,{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}^{(max)})
with
{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}^{(max)}={q}_{U_{r}}^{(max)}
, as before, in the
{\cal X}
rest-frame.
We find
{M^{\prime}_{B}}^{2}=M_{n}^{2}+M_{U}^{2}-2(E_{n}E^{\prime}_{U}-{p_{n}}_{r}{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r}c^{\prime}_{nU})
with
c^{\prime}_{nU}={\bf p}_{n}\!\cdot\!{{\bf q}^{\prime}_{U}}/({p_{n}}_{r}{q^{\prime}_{U}}_{\!r})
giving
c^{\prime}_{nU}=c^{\prime}_{U}
for us.
We also find
{q_{3}}_{r}^{\!\!{\oasterisk}}=(M^{\prime}_{B}/2)[(1-2M_{D}^{2}/{M^{\prime}_{B}}^{2})^{2}-4M_{D}^{4}/{M^{\prime}_{B}}^{4}]^{1/2}
.

For each numerical integration sampling point, using the Lorentz transformations given,
we transform the
q_{1}^{*}
and the
q_{3}^{\oasterisk}
to the lab-frame and compute all 4-momentum dot-products using the lab-frame momenta
\{p_{n},q_{U},q_{1},q_{2},q^{\prime}_{U},q_{3},q_{4}\}
.
We also compute the
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\sigma\tau}{p_{i}}_{\mu}{p_{j}}_{\nu}{p_{k}}_{\sigma}{p_{l}}_{\tau}
with the
p_{i,j,k,l}
being any of these lab-frame momenta.
Using the so computed (Lorentz invariant) dot-products, we evaluate the matrix element,
fold in the
d\Pi_{3}\,d\Pi^{\prime}_{3}
measures and obtain the required integrals numerically.

F.2.2
One-loop integral measure
